Daher and Dranishnikov established the analogous implication for closed orientable totally non-spin four-manifolds with residually finite fundamental group (Daher and Dranishnikov 2019, Theorem 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[daherDranishnikov2019](#ref-daherDranishnikov2019)]. These topological advances concern universal covers and finite bounds; the present quantitative statement is formulated for arbitrary complete manifolds with a bound depending only on dimension after scalar normalization.

The geometric method uses the minimal-hypersurface approach of Schoen and Yau, in which stability and the Gauss equation pass scalar-curvature information to hypersurfaces (Schoen and Yau 1979, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[schoenYau1979Structure](#ref-schoenYau1979Structure)], and the torical symmetrization mechanism described by Gromov (Misha Gromov 2018, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[gromov2018Metric](#ref-gromov2018Metric)]. Gromov’s $\mu$-bubble method uses prescribed mean curvature to localize the hypersurfaces entering scalar-curvature descent (M. Gromov 1996§$5\frac56$) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[gromov1996](#ref-gromov1996)]. The weighted variational formulation and its relation to warped-circle geometry and stability are developed by Chodosh and Li (Chodosh and Li 2024, sec. 3 and Remark 11) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[chodoshLi](#ref-chodoshLi)].

Removing singular regions while retaining scalar-curvature information is also central to Lohkamp’s splitting theory (Lohkamp 2022, sec. 1.2 and Theorem 3.15) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[lohkamp2022SplittingsII](#ref-lohkamp2022SplittingsII)]. In the construction below, profile updates exclude singular points from the regions where the next scalar bound is required; the wall and both retained sides are then regularized together.

Recent results also address quantitative width and singular descent. Antonelli proves width bounds under a positive scalar-curvature lower bound, an almost-nonnegative Ricci lower bound, and uniform noncollapse, with constants depending on the volume lower bound (Antonelli 2026, Theorem 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[antonelli2026Volume](#ref-antonelli2026Volume)]. Bi and Zhu develop singular scalar-curvature descent for enlargeability obstructions and cubical-width inequalities (Bi and Zhu 2026, Theorem B and Section 4) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[biZhu2026](#ref-biZhu2026)]. The present construction retains both bulk sides and the wall through countably many cuts, with compatible product ends.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The proof and its main constructions

Fix an original length $D$, depending only on $n$, and a separated net $(x_i)$ in $(M,g)$. The inner balls of radius $D$ cover $M$, and the closed outer balls of radius $2D$ are locally finite. Each center has its own label. We construct a continuous collapse map $q:M\to M$ with $d_g(q(x),x)<1$, together with compatible walls separating the inverse images of each inner ball and the exterior of its outer ball. The map $q$ records original positions while cylinders are inserted along the cuts. The final walls have no common intersection of $n-1$ distinct labels. Continuous sign coordinates $t_i:M\to[0,1]$ are chosen to equal $1$ on the $q$-preimage of the inner ball and $0$ on the $q$-preimage of the exterior of its outer ball. Their transition regions lie in wall neighborhoods chosen simultaneously to preserve the intersection bound. They give the desired map: at most $n-2$ coordinates are fractional at a point, and some coordinate is one. Equality of all coordinates confines both collapse images to the same original outer ball, giving a bound for the entire fiber.

Three points are essential to make this construction uniform and to allow arbitrary complete metrics.

First, the separations must become as wide as prescribed without spending curvature in proportion to the number of labels. For a metric $h$ and a smooth function $t$, put <a id="intro:drift-scalar"></a>$$\label{intro:drift-scalar}
 \mathcal D(h,t)=\mathop{\mathrm{Scal}}_h+2\Delta_h t-|\mathrm dt|_h^2.$$ Fix a background metric $h_0$ throughout a round. The local stretching estimate changes $h$ monotonically and increases $t$, while paying for its loss with a decrease in $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_h h_0$. The pressure law in [pressure:law](#pressure:law) provides a quadratic budget in $t$. This makes the initial scale depend only on $n$, even when there is no bound on the overlap multiplicity of the original balls. Trace losses telescope over a countable locally finite round. Each label stops after its own finite number of rounds, so the entire construction stabilizes on every compact set (). Diagonal circle warps convert the drift scalar bound to an ordinary scalar-curvature inequality on every required finite product.

Second, a cut produces both extended bulk sides and a wall of one lower base dimension. We keep all these pieces, called *packets*, with compatible product collars. Each label has two open *forced cores*, one of each sign, on which that sign must be retained. A label is *decided* at a point when the point lies in one of its cores. On an $m$-dimensional packet, positive scalar curvature is required only where at least $m-1$ remaining labels are outside their forced cores. Minimizing walls may be singular in high dimensions. Near the dangerous singular region, additional pressure profiles make the remaining labels decided. Hardy estimates, compactness across the bulk–cylinder seam, and a maximum estimate for concentrating potentials pay for this shielding. A guarded regularization and positive supersolutions then restore smooth scalar-curvature control. The full finite-diagram arguments are proved in . They include the stability and variation computations used here; classical elliptic and geometric-measure-theory results are cited with their hypotheses at their points of use.

Third, a countable list needs more than finiteness on compact subsets. After a first radial cut, only finitely many labels and warp factors are nonconstant on any *whole* packet and on each finite incident diagram needed by a step. All other labels are already forced; their potentials form a summable list of spatial constants. Approximations reproduce those constants exactly. This invariant permits the finite packet arguments to be iterated with successively smaller positive budgets, without a common positive floor for all labels. An intersection of $n-1$ distinct walls would produce a complete one-dimensional packet. The positive scalar-curvature invariant on its finite warped product yields a Riccati inequality that cannot hold on the complete arclength line (), giving the required wall-intersection bound.

Finally, the analytic cylinders are used only through finite truncations in the topological reconstruction. Successive collar compressions are supported over the corresponding original outer balls. On one fixed copy of $M$, the collapse maps and the old wall statuses are therefore eventually unchanged near every point. This local stabilization, together with a locally finite thickening of the walls, gives continuity in the ordinary polyhedral topology. The resulting constant is $$C_n=4D+2,$$ where the original scale $D$ is chosen in terms of $n$ before any metric-dependent analytic estimates. The construction does not aim to optimize this constant.

The proof follows two separate inputs. Sections <a href="#pressure:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pressure:section">2</a>–<a href="#sec:rounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rounds">4</a> construct the geometric stretching and its scalar reserve. Section <a href="#sec:finite-cuts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite-cuts">5</a> states the finite-label cutting procedure, whose complete proof occupies Appendices <a href="#sec:packets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:packets">9</a>–<a href="#sec:regularization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:regularization">13</a>. Section <a href="#sec:countable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:countable">6</a> proves its countable-label adaptation, and Section <a href="#sec:reconstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reconstruction">7</a> constructs the width map. Section <a href="#sec:closed-covers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:closed-covers">8</a> verifies the uniform simplex metric and proves the closed-cover consequence in dimensions two and three.

<a id="stmt-conv-signs"></a>
<div id="conv:signs" class="convention">

**Convention 5**. The Laplacian is $\Delta=\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}$, and round spheres have positive scalar curvature. For a hypersurface with unit normal $\nu$, its second fundamental form is $B(v,w)=\langle\nabla_v\nu,w\rangle$, and its mean curvature is $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}B$. Integration and divergence use Riemannian densities, so no orientation is needed. Distances in the final fiber bound are always measured in the original metric $g$.

</div>

<a id="pressure:section"></a>
### A pressure law with a quadratic budget

The local metric deformation will use a pressure depending both on a height variable $x$ and on the drift parameter $t$. Its derivative in $x$ must be large, while a large positive derivative in $x$ must be accompanied by a favorable derivative in $t$. The following construction supplies these properties with constants chosen before any local geometric data.

<a id="stmt-pressure-law"></a>
<div id="pressure:law" class="theorem">

<a id="pressure:estimates"></a>
**Theorem 6** (Pressure law). *For every $A_0,P_0,\varepsilon,b_0>0$ there exist $0<S_0\le1$, $T\ge1$, and a smooth function $$F:(-S_0,S_0)\times[T,\infty)\longrightarrow\mathbb R$$ which is odd in $x$, has $F_x>0$, and satisfies $$\label{pressure:estimates}
\begin{gathered}
 F_x\ge A_0t,\qquad |F_t|\le\varepsilon|F|,\qquad FF_t\le\varepsilon F_x,\\
 F_x\le b_0t^2\quad\text{or}\quad -FF_t\ge P_0F_x,\\
 F_x\le b_0t^2+\varepsilon F^2.
\end{gathered}$$ For each fixed $t\ge T$, $$\lim_{x\to-S_0}F(x,t)=-\infty,
 \qquad \lim_{x\to S_0}F(x,t)=+\infty.$$ These limits are uniform when $t$ ranges over a compact subinterval of $[T,\infty)$. The choices depend only on $A_0,P_0,\varepsilon,b_0$ and fixed numerical constants. In particular, they are independent of any manifold, metric, local cutoff, or subsequent deformation parameter. The lower bound $T$ may be increased after the construction.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pressure-law"><summary>Proof of stmt-pressure-law</summary>

<a id="pressure:inverse"></a>
*Proof.* We construct $F$ by prescribing its inverse in the first variable. For the moment, let $k>0$ and let $g$ be a positive smooth function on $[0,\infty)$, both to be chosen. Write $f>0$ for an independent pressure value and, for $t>0$, set $$\label{pressure:inverse}
 X(f,t)=\frac1k\arctan\frac{f^{1/3}}{g(y)},\qquad
 q=f^{2/3},\qquad y=\frac tq.$$ Put $A(y)=g(y)+2yg'(y)$. Differentiation at fixed $f$ or at fixed $t$, respectively, gives <a id="pressure:inverse-derivatives"></a>$$\label{pressure:inverse-derivatives}
 fX_t=-\frac{g'(y)}{k(1+g(y)^2/q)},\qquad
 X_f=\frac{A(y)}{3kq^2(1+g(y)^2/q)}.$$ Thus $A>0$ will make $X$ invertible in $f$. For the inverse, $F_x=1/X_f$ and $-FF_t/F_x=fX_t$. We will arrange the favorable pressure branch on a decreasing part of $g$, and obtain the quadratic branch from a lower bound for $t^2X_f$. We choose $g$ to decrease for small $y$ and to equal $y$ for large $y$, while keeping $A$ positive through the transition.

We construct $g$ as a smooth rounded maximum with a controlled negative derivative. Fix a nonnegative smooth probability density $\rho$ supported in $[1,2]$, positive in $(1,2)$, and vanishing to infinite order at its endpoints. For example, normalize $$\rho(r)=
 \begin{cases}
 c_\rho\exp\bigl(-1/((r-1)(2-r))\bigr),&1<r<2,\\
 0,&r\notin(1,2),
 \end{cases}
 \qquad \int_1^2\rho(r)\,\mathrm dr=1.$$ Write $\mu=\int_1^2r\rho(r)\,\mathrm dr$, so $1<\mu<2$. For $D_g>0$ set <a id="pressure:rounded-max"></a>$$\label{pressure:rounded-max}
 h(y)=D_g(1+y)^{-1/4},\qquad
 g(y)=\int_1^2\max\{y,rh(y)\}\rho(r)\,\mathrm dr,
 \qquad y\ge0.$$ Here and below smoothness at $y=0$ means smooth extendibility to a neighborhood of $[0,\infty)$. To verify smoothness, put $$M(z)=\int_1^2\max\{z,r\}\rho(r)\,\mathrm dr.$$ Then $M''=\rho$, with $\rho$ extended by zero, and $g(y)=h(y)M(y/h(y))$. Thus $g$ is smooth; moreover $M(z)=\mu$ for $z\le1$ and $M(z)=z$ for $z\ge2$. Consequently $g>0$, $g\ge y$, and $g(y)=y$ for all sufficiently large $y$.

<a id="pressure:g-bounds"></a>
For a direct derivative estimate, let $$L(z)=\int_{r\le z}\rho(r)\,\mathrm dr,
 \qquad R(z)=\int_{r\ge z}r\rho(r)\,\mathrm dr.$$ At $z=y/h(y)$ the identities $$g=yL(z)+hR(z),\qquad
 g'=L(z)-\frac{hR(z)}{4(1+y)}$$ give $$\label{pressure:g-bounds}
 g\ge y,\qquad
 -\frac{g}{4(1+y)}\le g'\le1,\qquad
 A(y)=g(y)+2yg'(y)\ge\frac{y+2}{2(y+1)}g(y)\ge\frac12g(y).$$ In particular the lower bound for $A/g$ is numerical and does not deteriorate as $D_g$ increases.

We now specify the order of all choices. Choose <a id="pressure:k-Y"></a>$$\label{pressure:k-Y}
 k\ge\max\{A_0,\varepsilon^{-1},\pi/2\},\qquad
 Y\ge1\quad\text{with}\quad Y^{-3}\le\frac{b_0}{12k}.$$ Choose $D_g$ only after $k,Y$, large enough that <a id="pressure:D-choice"></a>$$\label{pressure:D-choice}
 D_g\ge\max\left\{
 Y(1+Y)^{1/4},\,
 \frac{8kP_0(1+Y)^{5/4}}{\mu}
 \right\}.$$ Since $y(1+y)^{1/4}$ is increasing, $y/h(y)\le1$ on $[0,Y]$. Hence throughout this interval <a id="pressure:decreasing-range"></a>$$\label{pressure:decreasing-range}
 g(y)=\mu D_g(1+y)^{-1/4},\qquad
 g'(y)=-\frac{\mu D_g}{4(1+y)^{5/4}}\le-2kP_0.$$ The equality cases at the endpoints are covered by the smooth matching already proved.

With this $g$ fixed, define the finite constants $$g_0=\min_{0\le y\le Y}g(y)>0,\qquad
 G_0=\max_{0\le y\le Y}g(y),\qquad
 B=\sup_{y>Y}\frac{g(y)}y<\infty.$$ For example, $$1\le B\le\max\left\{1,\frac{2D_g}{Y(1+Y)^{1/4}}\right\},$$ because $g(y)\le\max\{y,2D_g(1+y)^{-1/4}\}$. Finally choose <a id="pressure:T-choice"></a>$$\label{pressure:T-choice}
 T\ge\max\left\{
 1,\frac6\varepsilon,\frac{12kB^2}{b_0},\,
 YG_0^2,\frac{12kY}{\varepsilon g_0}
 \right\}.$$ Thus the order is $k$, then $Y$, then $D_g$ and $g$, and then $T$; none of the preceding choices depends on a later one.

We now invert the map $X$ in <a href="#pressure:inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pressure:inverse">[pressure:inverse]</a>. By <a href="#pressure:g-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pressure:g-bounds">[pressure:g-bounds]</a>, $X_f>0$. As $f\downarrow0$, the argument $y$ tends to infinity, where $g(y)=y$, and $X(f,t)\to0$. As $f\to\infty$, $y\to0$, $g(y)\to g(0)>0$, and $X(f,t)\to S_0:=\pi/(2k)\le1$. Thus for every $t>0$, $X(\cdot,t)$ maps $(0,\infty)$ bijectively onto $(0,S_0)$ with positive derivative. Its inverse $f=F(x,t)$ is smooth by the implicit function theorem.

On a neighborhood of $f=0$ the exact identity $g(y)=y$ gives $$X(f,t)=\frac1k\arctan(f/t),\qquad F(x,t)=t\tan(kx).$$ Define $F(0,t)=0$ and extend $F$ oddly to $-S_0<x<0$. The displayed formula proves joint smoothness across $x=0$, including at the endpoint $t=T$ by smooth extension to $t>0$. The inverse construction also proves the asserted pole limits. For uniformity, if $I\subset[T,\infty)$ is compact and $H>0$, then $\max_{t\in I}X(H,t)<S_0$. Whenever $x$ exceeds this maximum, monotonicity gives $F(x,t)>H$ for every $t\in I$. The negative pole follows by oddness.

It remains to prove <a href="#pressure:estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pressure:estimates">[pressure:estimates]</a>. For $x>0$ set $f=F(x,t)$ and use the variables in <a href="#pressure:inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pressure:inverse">[pressure:inverse]</a>. Implicit differentiation of $X(F(x,t),t)=x$ yields <a id="pressure:signed-identities"></a>$$\label{pressure:signed-identities}
 F_x=\frac1{X_f},\qquad F_t=-\frac{X_t}{X_f},\qquad
 \frac{FF_t}{F_x}=\frac{g'}{k(1+g^2/q)},\qquad
 \frac{F_t}{F}=\frac{3yg'}{t(g+2yg')}.$$ In particular the sign in the last identity is the sign of $g'$. Since $A\le g+2y\le3g$ and $y\le g$, we have <a id="pressure:lower-slope"></a>$$\label{pressure:lower-slope}
 tX_f=\frac{yA}{3k(q+g^2)}\le\frac1k.$$ This proves $F_x\ge kt\ge A_0t$ without a constant depending on $g$. Also $|yg'|\le g$: for $g'\ge0$ this follows from $g'\le1$ and $y\le g$, whereas for $g'<0$ the lower derivative bound in <a href="#pressure:g-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pressure:g-bounds">[pressure:g-bounds]</a> even gives $|yg'|\le g/4$. Therefore $$\left|\frac{F_t}{F}\right|\le\frac6t\le\varepsilon,
 \qquad
 \frac{FF_t}{F_x}\le\frac1k\le\varepsilon.$$ For the second inequality a negative numerator is already favorable; for a nonnegative numerator use $g'\le1$.

Consider first $y>Y$. Using $A\ge g/2\ge y/2$ and $g/y\le B$ gives <a id="pressure:large-y"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 t^2X_f
 &=\frac{y^2A}{3k(1+yg^2/t)}\notag\\
 &\ge\frac{y^3}{6k(1+B^2y^3/T)}
  =\frac1{6k(y^{-3}+B^2/T)}
  \ge\frac1{6k(Y^{-3}+B^2/T)}
  \ge\frac1{b_0}.
 \label{pressure:large-y}
\end{aligned}$$ The last inequality uses [pressure:k-Y,pressure:T-choice] [pressure:k-Y](#pressure:k-Y), [pressure:T-choice](#pressure:T-choice). This bound remains uniform as $y\to\infty$, and it proves $F_x\le b_0t^2$ throughout this region.

If $0<y\le Y$, then $q=t/y\ge T/Y$ and $$\frac{g^2}{q}\le\frac{YG_0^2}{T}\le1.$$ It follows from [pressure:decreasing-range,pressure:inverse-derivatives] [pressure:decreasing-range](#pressure:decreasing-range), [pressure:inverse-derivatives](#pressure:inverse-derivatives) that <a id="pressure:favorable-branch"></a>$$\label{pressure:favorable-branch}
 -\frac{FF_t}{F_x}=fX_t
   =\frac{-g'}{k(1+g^2/q)}\ge P_0.$$ The two regions therefore establish the alternative in <a href="#pressure:estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pressure:estimates">[pressure:estimates]</a>. In this second region we also have <a id="pressure:quadratic-upper"></a>$$\label{pressure:quadratic-upper}
 F^2X_f=\frac{qA}{3k(1+g^2/q)}
   \ge\frac{qg_0}{12k}
   \ge\frac{g_0T}{12kY}\ge\frac1\varepsilon,$$ so $F_x\le\varepsilon F^2$. Together with the small-$F_x$ bound for $y>Y$ this proves the final inequality of <a href="#pressure:estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pressure:estimates">[pressure:estimates]</a>.

Oddness preserves all five inequalities on $x<0$, since $F_x$, $F^2$, and $FF_t$ are even and $|F_t|,|F|$ are even. At $x=0$ they follow by continuity; more explicitly, $F=F_t=0$ and $F_x=kt$, while $T\ge12kB^2/b_0\ge k/b_0$ gives the small-$F_x$ branch there. All claims follow. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pressure-parameter-independence"></a>
<div id="pressure:parameter-independence" class="remark">

*Remark 7*. When $A_0,P_0,\varepsilon,b_0$ are prescribed using only the dimension and fixed numerical constants, the same is true of $S_0,T$ and the entire pressure law. In particular, the small parameters denoted by $a$ and $d$ in the local deformation may be chosen after this theorem has been applied, without modifying the pressure or increasing $T$.

</div>

<a id="step:section"></a>
### A compactly supported stretching step

For a metric $h$ and a smooth function $t$, put $$\mathcal D(h,t)=\mathop{\mathrm{Scal}}_h+2\mathop{\mathrm{\Delta}}_h t-|\mathrm dt|_h^2.$$ The next theorem enlarges the distance from a compact set while preserving a quadratic reserve in this quantity. Its error has two parts: a trace decrement, which telescopes under successive metric enlargements, and an arbitrarily small quadratic loss.

<a id="stmt-step-main"></a>
<div id="step:main" class="theorem">

<a id="step:conclusion"></a>
<a id="step:distance"></a>
**Theorem 8** (Local stretching). *Fix $n\ge2$ and $0<\delta<1/10$, and put $L_*=1.004$. There are constants $W,T\ge1$, depending only on $n,\delta$, with the following property. Let $h_0$ be a complete smooth metric on a manifold $M^n$ without boundary, let $h\ge h_0$ be smooth, and let $t\ge T$ be smooth. Let $A$ be a nonempty compact set and $B$ a nonempty closed set such that $$\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_{h_0}(A,B)\ge W.$$ For every $0<d<\delta$ there are smooth $h'\ge h$ and $t'\ge t$ such that the changes have compact support in $\{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_{h_0}(A,\cdot)<W\}$, $$\label{step:conclusion}
 \mathcal D(h',t')\ge \mathcal D(h,t)+(t'^2-t^2)
 -\delta\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_h h_0-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_{h'}h_0\bigr)t'^2-dt'^2,$$ and $$\label{step:distance}
 \mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_{h'}(A,B)\ge L_*W.$$ In particular, $h'$ is complete. Neither $W$ nor $T$ depends on $d$, the geometry of $M$, or the sets $A,B$.*

</div>

The proof uses a modified graph equation. Its modification near zero slope supplies an adjustable small error, whereas at moderate and large slopes it is the ordinary graph equation. A positive floor in the spatial cutoff permits a closed-manifold existence argument. We subsequently send that floor to zero only on a fixed annulus, where the solution decays exponentially. All geometric estimates used for these two operations take place on one compact auxiliary manifold.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### The slope switch and the order of constants

We first specify the deformation associated with a provisional smooth height $u$; the height itself will be chosen below. Write $s=|\mathrm du|_h$. Fix $s_0=10^{-2}$ and a smooth nondecreasing function $\rho:[0,\infty)
\to[0,1]$ such that $$\rho=0\text{ on }[0,s_0],\qquad
 \rho=1\text{ on }[5s_0,\infty),\qquad 0\le\rho'\le60.$$ For $0<a\le1/2$ and $s\ge0$ define <a id="step:switch"></a>$$\label{step:switch}
 \mu=a^2+(1-a^2)\rho(s),\quad
 \theta=\frac{\mu s^2}{1+s^2},\quad
 l=(1-\theta)^{1/2},\quad \beta=\log l.$$ For $s>0$ also put <a id="step:chi"></a>$$\label{step:chi}
 \chi=\left(1+\frac{a^2s_0^2}{\theta}\right)^{1/2},\qquad
 m=\frac{\mathrm d\log\chi}{\mathrm d\log\sqrt\theta}
   =-\frac{a^2s_0^2}{\theta+a^2s_0^2}.$$ Here $\theta$ is strictly increasing. The following bounds will be used repeatedly: <a id="step:switch-bounds"></a>$$\label{step:switch-bounds}
\begin{gathered}
 0\le -s\beta_s\le2,\qquad -1\le m\le0,\qquad
 1\le\chi\le2\quad(s\ge s_0),\\
 m\le-\tfrac12,\quad \chi\ge s_0/s,\quad
 \theta\chi^2\le2a^2s_0^2\quad(0<s\le s_0).
\end{gathered}$$ Indeed, on $s\le5s_0$, $$-s\beta_s=\frac{s\theta_s}{2(1-\theta)}
 \le30s^3+\frac{s^2}{1+s^2}<2,$$ and on $s\ge5s_0$ it equals $s^2/(1+s^2)$. The remaining bounds follow directly from $\theta\ge a^2s^2/(1+s^2)$ and the formula with $\mu=a^2$ on $s\le s_0$.

For $t\ge1$ we also have <a id="step:beta-budget"></a>$$\label{step:beta-budget}
 \frac{(t-\beta)^2-t^2}{\theta}\le4(1+s)t\qquad(s>0).$$ To check this, write $b_*=-\beta$. If $\theta\le1/2$, then $b_*\le\theta$, and the left side is at most $3t$. Otherwise use $b_*\le\tfrac12\log(1+s^2)\le\log(1+s)\le\sqrt s$ to bound it by $4t\sqrt s+2s\le4(1+s)t$.

<a id="step:raw-output"></a>
<a id="step:trace"></a>
For the provisional height $u$, define the raw metric and drift by $$\label{step:raw-output}
 h_u=h+\frac{\theta}{(1-\theta)s^2}\,\mathrm du^2
     =h+\frac{\mu}{1+(1-\mu)s^2}\,\mathrm du^2,
 \qquad t_u=t-\beta.$$ These expressions are smooth also at $s=0$, and $h_u\ge h$, $t_u\ge t$. To see explicitly the smoothness needed at zero slope, note that near $s=0$ the coefficient of $\mathrm du^2$ is $a^2/[1+(1-a^2)s^2]$, while the drift increment is $-\tfrac12\log([1+(1-a^2)s^2]/[1+s^2])$. Both are smooth functions of the first jet of $u$; therefore $C^3$ convergence of $u$ to zero gives $C^2$ convergence of these metric and drift increments to zero. For $s\ge5s_0$, $h_u=h+\mathrm du^2$. At points of nonzero slope in the original manifold, let $e=\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}_hu/s$. With the given background metric $h_0$, the rank-one inverse formula gives $$\label{step:trace}
 \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_hh_0-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_{h_u}h_0=\theta b^2,
 \qquad b^2=h_0(e,e)\le1.$$

<a id="step:a-choice"></a>
We now fix all constants that are required to be independent of the local geometry and of $d$. Let <a id="step:constants"></a>$$\label{step:constants}
 A_0=1000,\qquad P_0=20,\qquad C_1=s_0^{-1},\qquad
 b_0=\frac{\delta s_0^2}{1000},\qquad
 0<\varepsilon\le\min\left\{\frac{s_0}{100},\frac1{8n},\frac1{8C_1}\right\}.$$ Apply <a href="#pressure:law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pressure:law">6</a> with these constants, obtaining $F,S_0,T$. In particular $S_0\le1$ and $T\ge1$. Put $C_2=2C_1b_0$. Only after a loss $d$ has been specified do we choose $a$ so that $$\label{step:a-choice}
 0<a\le\tfrac12,\qquad (2C_2+3)a^2\le d/2.$$ The width $W$ will be a sufficiently large numerical constant; it may, and will, be at least $10^5$. It is chosen to satisfy the explicit cutoff and distance inequalities below. Thus the choices of $W,T$ precede $d,a$ and all local geometric choices.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Cutoffs and a closed auxiliary manifold

<a id="step:cutoffs"></a>
Write $r_A(x)=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_{h_0}(A,x)$. There exist smooth functions $\psi\ge0$ and $0\le\eta_1,\zeta\le1$ such that $$\label{step:cutoffs}
\begin{gathered}
 \inf_A\psi>W/5,\qquad |\mathrm d\psi|_{h_0}\le1,
 \qquad\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\psi\subset\{r_A<W/2\},\\
 \eta_1=1\text{ on }\{r_A\le .55W\},\qquad
 \eta_1=0\text{ on }\{r_A\ge .8W\},\qquad
 |\mathrm d\eta_1|_{h_0}\le s_0/8,\\
 \zeta=1\text{ on }\{r_A\le .82W\},\qquad
 \zeta=0\text{ on }\{r_A\ge .98W\}.
\end{gathered}$$ Choose also a fixed compact annulus $\mathcal A_\zeta$ in $\{.8W<r_A<W\}$ containing $\overline{\{0<\zeta<1\}}$. All cutoff error estimates below are imposed on this whole annulus, including any intermediate plateau of $\zeta$.

Here no injectivity-radius or curvature bound is needed. For clarity, a smooth approximation to a Lipschitz function on the compact neighborhood in question can be made in finitely many coordinate charts. Shrink each chart until its metric distortion is as small as desired, convolve there, and combine with a smooth partition of unity. The gradient of the combination is the convex combination of the local gradients plus terms bounded by the chosen uniform approximation error times the derivatives of the partition. Choose the local errors small enough to make the latter sum arbitrarily small. One may preserve prescribed constant regions by using charts lying inside them and leaving a buffer between each such region and the transition. Apply this construction to $(W/3-r_A)_+$, with value error less than $W/100$ and gradient bound $1.05$, then divide by $1.05$ and use the zero-region buffer to obtain $\psi$. Smooth cutoff profiles of $r_A/W$ give the other two functions; for $\eta_1$ the gradient can be bounded by $12/W<s_0/8$. This proves <a href="#step:cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:cutoffs">[step:cutoffs]</a> after increasing $W$ once.

For $0<r\le1/2$ define the positive-floor cutoff <a id="step:floor"></a>$$\label{step:floor}
 \eta=r+(1-r)\eta_1^2.$$ Then $\eta=1$ near $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\psi$, $\eta=r$ on $\{r_A\ge.8W\}$, and, uniformly in $r$, <a id="step:floor-bounds"></a>$$\label{step:floor-bounds}
 0<\eta\le1,\qquad |\mathrm d\eta|_h\le s_0/4,
 \qquad \frac{|\mathrm d\eta|_h^2}{\eta}\le1.$$ The same statements hold with $h_0$ in place of $h$; this follows from $h\ge h_0$ and $|\mathrm d\eta|^2/\eta\le4|\mathrm d\eta_1|^2$.

Completeness of $h_0$ and compactness of $A$ make the closed neighborhoods used above compact. Enclose a slightly larger neighborhood, say $\{r_A\le1.1W\}$, in a smooth relatively compact domain. Outside this neighborhood, modify $h$ and $t$ near the boundary of the domain to be products, and double the domain. Convex interpolation for $t$ preserves $t\ge T$, and interpolation of positive definite tensors gives the metric extension. We thereby obtain a closed auxiliary manifold $N$ on which $h,t,\psi,\eta_1,\zeta$ are smooth, with the last three functions extended by zero where appropriate. The extensions of $h,t$ are fixed independently of $r$. If the relevant component of $M$ is already compact, it can simply be used as $N$. The construction can be made on finitely many components if $A$ meets more than one. From now until the final cutoff, we work on $N$. Constants allowed to depend on this compact geometry and on $a$ will be denoted by $C$; they will never enter $W,T$.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The modified graph equation

For a covector $p$ with $s=|p|_h$, set $$\mathcal A(p)=\frac{\sqrt\mu}{\sqrt{1+s^2}}p^\sharp,
 \qquad J(s)=\sqrt{\theta+a^2s_0^2}.$$ The symbols $\mu,\theta,l,\beta,\chi,m$ will always be evaluated at $s=|\mathrm du|_h$ when a function $u$ is under discussion. The maps $\mathcal A,J$ are smooth at $p=0$: there $\mu=a^2$ is constant. The tangential and radial eigenvalues of $\mathcal A_p$ are $\sqrt\theta/s$ and $(\sqrt\theta)_s$, respectively; both are positive, and both have limit $a$ at the origin.

<a id="step:PDE"></a>
For $\tau\in[0,1]$ consider $$\label{step:PDE}
 \mathop{\mathrm{div}}_h\mathcal A(\mathrm du)-\mathrm dt\bigl(\mathcal A(\mathrm du)\bigr)
 =J(|\mathrm du|_h)F\left(\frac{u-\tau\psi}{\eta},t\right).$$ The pressure is evaluated only where its first argument lies in $(-S_0,S_0)$. We first prove existence with bounds independent of $r$.

<a id="stmt-step-existence"></a>
<div id="step:existence" class="lemma">

<a id="step:C01"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Height and gradient bounds). *For each fixed $a>0$ as above and every $0<r\le1/2$, <a href="#step:PDE" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:PDE">[step:PDE]</a> at $\tau=1$ has a smooth solution $u$ on $N$. There are $S_1<S_0$ and $G<\infty$, independent of $r$ and of $\tau\in[0,1]$, such that every solution along the continuation satisfies $$\label{step:C01}
 |u-\tau\psi|\le S_1\eta,\qquad |\mathrm du|_h\le G.$$ The constants $S_1,G$ may depend on $a$ and on the compact data.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-step-existence"><summary>Proof of stmt-step-existence</summary>

*Proof.* For $S<S_0$ the functions $u_\pm=\tau\psi\pm S\eta$ have uniformly bounded first and second derivatives, independently of $r,\tau,S$. Consequently the left side of <a href="#step:PDE" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:PDE">[step:PDE]</a> at these functions is bounded. Since $J\ge as_0>0$ and $t(N)$ is compact, the uniform pole limits of $F$ permit one choice $S_1<S_0$ for which $u_+$ has strictly negative residual (left side minus right side) and $u_-$ has strictly positive residual. Comparison at a maximum or minimum, using strict monotonicity in $u$, gives the height estimate.

One can make this argument before a solution has been constructed by extending $F$ smoothly to all first arguments with $F_x>0$, retaining its original values on a slightly larger interval than $[-S_1,S_1]$. For example, extend the positive even function $F_x$ by a smooth cutoff to a positive constant and integrate from zero. Solve this globally defined equation. The same barriers force every solution into $[-S_1,S_1]$, so that the extension is never used in the resulting solution.

<a id="step:angle-operator"></a>
<a id="step:angle-Jacobi"></a>
We give the gradient estimate, including the dependence of the pressure on the graph angle. If $\max_N|\mathrm du|_h\le1$ there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, near a point of maximum slope, $\mu=1$ and the graph in $(N,h)\times\mathbb R$ is an ordinary graph. Write $$w=(1+|\mathrm du|_h^2)^{-1/2},\qquad
 \nu=(N_u,-w),\qquad N_u=w\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}_hu,$$ for its downward unit normal. Denote its second fundamental form and mean curvature by $\mathrm{II}$ and $H$, with $H=\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\nu$. Extend $t$ constantly in the vertical direction and put $X=-\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}t$ and $K=-\partial_z$. The equation is $$H+\langle X,\nu\rangle=p(x,z,w),\qquad
 p(x,z,\omega)=\sqrt{1-\omega^2+a^2s_0^2}\,
 F\left(\frac{z-\tau\psi(x)}{\eta(x)},t(x)\right).$$ Let $K^\top=K-w\nu$. Under a normal variation with speed $v$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \dot H&=-\mathop{\mathrm{\Delta}}v-(|\mathrm{II}|^2+\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu,\nu))v,\\
 \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm dq}\langle X,\nu\rangle
 &=-\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}t(\nu,\nu)v-\langle X^\top,\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v\rangle,\\
 \dot w&=-\langle K^\top,\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v\rangle.
\end{aligned}$$ Thus the linearization of the equation, with all terms moved to the left, is $-\mathcal Lv$, where $$\label{step:angle-operator}
 \mathcal L=\mathop{\mathrm{\Delta}}+
 \langle X^\top-p_\omega K^\top,\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}\,\cdot\,\rangle
 +|\mathrm{II}|^2+\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nu,\nu)+\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}t(\nu,\nu)
 +\partial_\nu^{\mathrm{fixed}\ \omega}p.$$ The last derivative differentiates the base and height variables while holding the angle fixed. Translation in direction $K$ leaves the angle, $H$, and $\langle X,\nu\rangle$ unchanged and differentiates the residual by $p_z$. Its normal speed is $w$; its tangential component differentiates an identically zero residual. We obtain the exact identity $$\label{step:angle-Jacobi}
 \mathcal Lw=-p_z.$$ In particular the term $-p_\omega K^\top$ in the drift has not been discarded.

Put $\xi=(u-\tau\psi)/\eta$. On the graph, $$p_z=\frac{JF_x}{\eta},\qquad
 \partial_\nu^{\mathrm{fixed}\ \omega}p
 =J\left\{
 \frac{F_x}{\eta}\bigl[-w-\tau\mathrm d\psi(N_u)-\xi\mathrm d\eta(N_u)\bigr]
 +F_t\mathrm dt(N_u)\right\}.$$ The height bound puts $F,F_x,F_t$ in a fixed compact range. Moreover $|N_u|\le1$, $|\mathrm d\psi|_h\le1$, and $|\mathrm d\eta|_h\le s_0/4$. The zero-order coefficient in <a href="#step:angle-operator" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:angle-operator">[step:angle-operator]</a>, after omitting the nonnegative term $|\mathrm{II}|^2$, is therefore at least $-C/\eta$, with $C$ independent of $r,\tau$. At the minimum of $w$ under consideration, the slope is at least one, so $J\ge1/\sqrt2$ and $p_z\ge A_0T/(\sqrt2\eta)$. Since $\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}w=0$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{\Delta}}w\ge0$, <a href="#step:angle-Jacobi" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:angle-Jacobi">[step:angle-Jacobi]</a> implies $$\frac{A_0T}{\sqrt2\eta}\le\frac C\eta w.$$ This gives a positive lower bound for $w$, and hence the asserted $G$.

We finish the fixed-$r$ continuation argument in enough detail to specify its regularity inputs. A classical $C^{2,\alpha}$ solution is smooth before we apply estimates requiring smoothness. Indeed, writing its equation as $a^{ij}u_{ij}=f$, the coefficient functions $a^{ij}$ and $f$ are $C^{1,\alpha}$. On nested coordinate balls, a difference quotient $u_h$ satisfies $$a^{ij}(x)\partial_{ij}u_h
 =f_h-(a^{ij})_h\partial_{ij}u(x+h).$$ For each nonzero $h$, $u_h$ is already $C^{2,\alpha}$; its right side has a $C^{0,\alpha}$ bound independent of $h$. The classical interior Schauder estimate and passage to $h=0$ give $u\in C^{3,\alpha}$, and iteration gives smoothness. These qualitative estimates may depend on the existing $C^{2,\alpha}$ norm; the following a priori estimates do not. At $\tau=0$, $u=0$ solves the equation. For fixed $r>0$, the height and gradient bounds put $(x,u,\mathrm du)$ in a compact set on which the principal coefficients are uniformly elliptic and all explicit coefficient derivatives are bounded. In a coordinate chart write the divergence-form equation as $$\partial_i\mathfrak A^i(x,Du)=\mathfrak B(x,u,Du).$$ Differentiating with $v=\partial_ku$ gives <a id="step:differentiated"></a>$$\label{step:differentiated}
 \partial_i(\mathfrak A^i_{p_j}\partial_jv)
 -\mathfrak B_{p_j}\partial_jv
 =\mathfrak B_u v+\mathfrak B_{x_k}
   -\partial_i\mathfrak A^i_{x_k}.$$ The $x$ derivatives on the right are explicit derivatives of the coefficient functions. All nondifferentiated terms and the vector field inside the last divergence are bounded using only the compact jet range; no second derivative estimate has been assumed. The local Hölder estimate for uniformly elliptic divergence-form equations, with bounded lower-order coefficients and bounded divergence data, now gives a uniform $C^{1,\alpha}$ bound for $u$ on a finite cover. The equation in nondivergence form then has Hölder coefficients and right side, so local Schauder estimates give $C^{2,\alpha}$ bounds, followed by bounds of every order. The Hölder and interior Schauder estimates used here are respectively Simon (2015, Lecture 18, Theorem 3, and Lecture 12, Theorem 2) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[simonPDE](#ref-simonPDE)]. All their uses are on compact coordinate balls with a positive ellipticity constant; their constants may depend on fixed $r$.

Fix the floor $r>0$, and choose $0<\alpha<1$ smaller, if necessary, than the exponent in the preceding Hölder estimate. The linearization in $u$ has strictly negative zero-order coefficient $-JF_x/\eta$; angle and gradient derivatives affect only its principal and first-order terms. We verify that this linearization is an isomorphism from $C^{2,\alpha}(N)$ to $C^{0,\alpha}(N)$.

First consider $\mathop{\mathrm{\Delta}}_h-1$. For $f\in L^2(N)$, minimize on $H^1(N)$ the strictly convex coercive functional $$\frac12\int_N\bigl(|\mathrm dv|_h^2+v^2\bigr)
                 \,\mathrm d\operatorname{vol}_h
 +\int_N fv\,\mathrm d\operatorname{vol}_h.$$ Its Euler equation is $(\mathop{\mathrm{\Delta}}_h-1)v=f$ in the weak sense. Testing the homogeneous difference with itself proves uniqueness among $H^1$ weak solutions. For $f\in C^{0,\alpha}(N)$ choose smooth $f_j$ converging uniformly to $f$, with uniformly bounded $C^{0,\alpha}$ norms, using coordinate mollification and a finite partition of unity. Weak interior regularity makes the corresponding weak solutions $v_j$ smooth (Simon 2015, Lecture 6, Theorem 1 and Corollary 1) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[simonPDE](#ref-simonPDE)]. The maximum principle gives $\|v_j\|_{C^0}\le\|f_j\|_{C^0}$. A finite interior cover and the Schauder estimate (Simon 2015, Lecture 12, Theorem 2) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[simonPDE](#ref-simonPDE)] then bound $v_j$ uniformly in $C^{2,\alpha}(N)$. Passing to a subsequence in $C^{2,\alpha'}(N)$, where $0<\alpha'<\alpha$, gives a solution for $f$. The common $\alpha$-Hölder bound for the second derivatives passes to the limit, so this solution belongs to $C^{2,\alpha}(N)$. Weak uniqueness identifies it with the energy solution, and the maximum principle also gives uniqueness in the classical class.

Let $L_q$, $0\le q\le1$, interpolate the coefficients of $\mathop{\mathrm{\Delta}}_h-1$ and the linearization written in nondivergence form. Uniform ellipticity and a uniform strictly negative zero-order bound persist along this path. The maximum principle and the same finite-cover Schauder estimate give $$\|v\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(N)}
 \le C\|L_qv\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(N)},
 \qquad 0\le q\le1.$$ Invertibility is open by the Neumann series. To see closedness, suppose $q_j\to q$ through invertible parameters and, for a fixed $f\in C^{0,\alpha}(N)$, put $v_j=L_{q_j}^{-1}f$. The estimate above bounds $v_j$ and gives $$\|v_j-v_k\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}
 \le C\|(L_{q_k}-L_{q_j})v_k\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}
 \longrightarrow0.$$ Thus $v_j$ converges in $C^{2,\alpha}$ to a solution of $L_qv=f$; injectivity follows from the same estimate. This proves the required linearized isomorphism.

The implicit function theorem now gives openness of the nonlinear continuation in $\tau$. The preceding difference-quotient bootstrap applies to the $C^{2,\alpha}$ solutions obtained by implicit inversion. The uniform a priori $C^{2,\alpha}$ bound makes its higher derivative bounds uniform along continuation at this fixed positive floor. In particular these bounds give compactness in $C^{2,\alpha}(N)$, so a convergent sequence of soluble parameters has a solution at its limit. The height bound keeps the pressure argument in its fixed compact interval. The soluble set is therefore closed as well as open and contains $0$, hence equals $[0,1]$. The local bootstrap makes the resulting solution smooth. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Decay on the region of constant floor

<a id="stmt-step-locality"></a>
<div id="step:locality" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Locality). *Let $u_r$ be the solutions from <a href="#step:existence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:existence">9</a> at $\tau=1$. On any compact subset of the fixed open region where $\psi=0$ and $\eta_1=0$, the functions $u_r$ tend to zero in every $C^k$ norm as $r\downarrow0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-step-locality"><summary>Proof of stmt-step-locality</summary>

<a id="step:mass-equation"></a>
*Proof.* Here $\eta=r$ and $|u_r/r|\le S_1$. Expanding the left side of <a href="#step:PDE" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:PDE">[step:PDE]</a> and using $F(0,t)=0$ writes its equation as $$\label{step:mass-equation}
 a^{ij}(x)\partial_{ij}u_r+b^i(x)\partial_i u_r
 -\frac{c_r(x)}r u_r=0,
 \qquad
 c_r=J\int_0^1F_x(qu_r/r,t)\,\mathrm dq\ge as_0A_0T>0.$$ The coefficients $a^{ij}$ are uniformly elliptic and bounded, and $b^i$ are bounded, independently of $r$. Indeed they are smooth functions of $(x,Du_r)$ on the fixed compact jet range from <a href="#step:C01" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:C01">[step:C01]</a>; the explicit $x$ part of the flux vanishes at zero covector and is therefore a bounded first-order term. Also $c_r$ is bounded above independently of $r$. No derivatives of these coefficients are needed for the next comparison.

Take a coordinate box compactly contained in the constant-floor region. Let $\ell_f$ be its inward affine distance from face $f$ in these coordinates and set $$V_r=M_0\sum_f\exp(-\gamma\ell_f/\sqrt r),$$ where $M_0$ dominates $|u_r|$ on the boundary for every $r$. Let $\Lambda$ bound $a^{ij}(\partial_i\ell_f)(\partial_j\ell_f)$, let $c_*>0$ be the lower bound for $c_r$, and let $B_*$ bound the first-order contributions. The operator in <a href="#step:mass-equation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:mass-equation">[step:mass-equation]</a> applied to each summand of $V_r$ is at most that summand times $$\frac{\Lambda\gamma^2-c_*}{r}+\frac{B_*\gamma}{\sqrt r}.$$ Choose $\gamma>0$ with $\Lambda\gamma^2<c_*/2$. For small $r$ this quantity is negative. Comparison with both $u_r$ and $-u_r$ proves, on each smaller box, $|u_r|\le C\exp(-c/\sqrt r)$. A finite covering gives this estimate on any fixed compact subset.

To upgrade the estimate to derivatives, rescale a coordinate ball of radius comparable to $r$ by $$x=x_0+ry,\qquad u_r(x)=r v(y).$$ The equation becomes <a id="step:rescaled"></a>$$\label{step:rescaled}
 \partial_{y_i}\mathfrak A^i(x_0+ry,Dv)
 =r\mathfrak B_0(x_0+ry,v,Dv),$$ where $\mathfrak B_0$ includes the drift term and $JF(v,t)$ and has no negative power of $r$. We have $|v|\le S_1$ and $|Dv|\le G$. The principal ellipticity is uniform. Explicit $y$ derivatives of $\mathfrak A$ have a factor $r$, explicit $y$ derivatives of $r\mathfrak B_0$ have a factor $r^2$, and its $v,p$ derivatives have a factor $r$. Thus all explicit coefficient derivatives are bounded uniformly on this fixed jet range. Apply the Hölder estimate to the differentiated [step:differentiated](#step:differentiated), followed by the Schauder bootstrap on fixed balls in $y$. For every $k$ this gives $$|D_y^kv|\le C_k,\qquad |D_x^ku_r|\le C_kr^{1-k},$$ on smaller balls, with constants uniform in $x_0$ in a fixed compact set and in small $r$. Finally, interior interpolation between the exponentially small $C^0$ bound and the polynomial bound for one higher derivative proves $C^k$ convergence to zero for each fixed $k$. For example, on fixed nested compact subsets of the constant-floor region, interpolation bounds the third derivatives by a constant times $r^{-9/4}\exp(-c/(4\sqrt r))+\exp(-c/\sqrt r)$, which tends to zero. This last case ensures $C^2$ convergence of all metric and drift increments in <a href="#step:raw-output" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:raw-output">[step:raw-output]</a>, which are smooth functions of $Du_r$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### The scalar identity and its two slope regimes

All comparisons involving $h_0$ in this subsection are made on the original neighborhood $\{r_A<1.1W\}$, where the auxiliary data agree with the given data. No extension of $h_0$ to the double is needed.

Apply the construction in <a href="#step:raw-output" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:raw-output">[step:raw-output]</a> to the solution $u_r$ from <a href="#step:existence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:existence">9</a> at $\tau=1$, and write $u=u_r$. We now prove its scalar estimate before applying the final spatial cutoff.

<a id="stmt-step-raw-estimate"></a>
<div id="step:raw-estimate" class="lemma">

<a id="step:raw-inequality"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Raw scalar estimate). *On this original neighborhood, the raw output satisfies $$\label{step:raw-inequality}
 \mathcal D(h_u,t_u)\ge\mathcal D(h,t)+(t_u^2-t^2)
 -\delta(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_hh_0-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_{h_u}h_0)t_u^2-\frac d2t^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-step-raw-estimate"><summary>Proof of stmt-step-raw-estimate</summary>

<a id="step:foliation-scalar"></a>
*Proof.* We derive the scalar comparison explicitly. Near a point with $\mathrm du\ne0$ use $q=u$ as the transverse coordinate and write $$h=v^2\mathrm dq^2+\gamma_q,\qquad v=1/s,\qquad
 e=v^{-1}\partial_q,\qquad
 \mathrm J=\frac1{2v}\partial_q\gamma_q,\qquad H=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\mathrm J.$$ Here $\mathrm J$ is the second fundamental form of the level sets, and all tangential norms and derivatives below use $\gamma_q$. Put $S=H-et$. The Gauss equation and normal variation of $H$ give $$\mathop{\mathrm{Scal}}_h=\mathop{\mathrm{Scal}}_{\gamma}-|\mathrm J|^2-H^2-2eH
              -2\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{\Delta}}_{\gamma}v}{v},
 \qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{\Delta}}_ht=\mathop{\mathrm{\Delta}}_{\gamma}t+e(et)+H et
           +\langle\mathrm d_\gamma\log v,\mathrm d_\gamma t\rangle.$$ Consequently $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal D(h,t)
 &=-|\mathrm J|^2-S^2-2eS+\mathop{\mathrm{Scal}}_\gamma
    +2\mathop{\mathrm{\Delta}}_\gamma t-|\mathrm d_\gamma t|^2\notag\\
 &\hspace{8mm}-2\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{\Delta}}_\gamma v}{v}
    +2\langle\mathrm d_\gamma\log v,\mathrm d_\gamma t\rangle.
 \label{step:foliation-scalar}
\end{aligned}$$ The deformation replaces $v$ by $v/l$ and $t$ by $t-\beta$. The change in the tangential terms on the right is $$2\langle\mathrm d_\gamma\log v,\mathrm d_\gamma\beta\rangle
       -|\mathrm d_\gamma\beta|^2
 =c_\beta(2-c_\beta)|\mathrm d_\gamma\log v|^2\ge0,
 \qquad c_\beta=-s\beta_s\in[0,2].$$ This computation follows by expanding $\mathop{\mathrm{\Delta}}_\gamma(v/l)/(v/l)$ and $|\mathrm d_\gamma(t-\beta)|^2$; the $\mathop{\mathrm{\Delta}}_\gamma\beta$ and the terms involving $\mathrm d_\gamma t$ cancel.

<a id="step:E"></a>
The divergence equation can be written $$S+e\log\sqrt\theta=\chi F(\xi,t),\qquad
 \xi=(u-\psi)/\eta.$$ Define $$M=\chi F,\qquad K=\frac{l^2}{\theta}e\beta.$$ Because $e\log\sqrt\theta=-K$, we have $S=M+K$. For the new metric, $e'=le$, $\mathrm J'=l\mathrm J$, and $$S'=l(S+e\beta)=lM+K/l.$$ Substitution in the normal terms of <a href="#step:foliation-scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:foliation-scalar">[step:foliation-scalar]</a> gives $$\label{step:E}
 \mathcal D(h_u,t_u)-\mathcal D(h,t)\ge\theta E,
 \qquad E=|\mathrm J|^2+M^2+K^2/l^2-2MK+2eM.$$ For completeness, the derivative terms before simplification are $-2le(lM+K/l)+2e(M+K)$. Their $eK$ terms cancel; using $e\beta=\theta K/l^2$ leaves $2\theta eM-2\theta MK+
2\theta K^2/l^2$, which combines with the squared terms to yield <a href="#step:E" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:E">[step:E]</a>.

<a id="step:B"></a>
Since $e\chi=-m\chi K$ and $et=H-M-K$, $$eM=-mKM+\chi(F_t et+F_xe\xi).$$ Completing the $H$ and $K$ squares gives $E\ge\mathcal B$, where $$\label{step:B}
 \mathcal B=\chi^2\left\{
 [1-l^2(1+m)^2]F^2
 -2[1+l^2(1+m)]FF_t-nF_t^2\right\}
 +2\chi F_xe\xi.$$ An exact verification, useful for tracking every sign, is $$\begin{aligned}
 E-\mathcal B
 &=\left(|\mathrm J|^2-\frac{H^2}{n-1}\right)
 +\frac{(H+(n-1)\chi F_t)^2}{n-1}\\
 &\quad+\left(\frac K l-l\chi[(1+m)F+F_t]\right)^2
 +\theta\chi^2F_t^2\ge0.
\end{aligned}$$ All pressure expressions here are evaluated at $(\xi,t)$.

Suppose first that $s\ge s_0$. Denote the expression in braces in <a href="#step:B" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:B">[step:B]</a> by $\mathcal C$. Its $F^2$ coefficient is nonnegative, and $1\le1+l^2(1+m)\le2$. From <a href="#pressure:estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pressure:estimates">[pressure:estimates]</a> we get <a id="step:brace-estimates"></a>$$\label{step:brace-estimates}
 \mathcal C\ge-5\varepsilon F_x,
 \qquad
 -FF_t\ge P_0F_x\ \Longrightarrow\ \mathcal C\ge P_0F_x.$$ Indeed, if $FF_t\ge0$, then $F_t^2\le\varepsilon FF_t$ and $\mathcal C\ge-(4+n\varepsilon)FF_t\ge-5\varepsilon F_x$. If $FF_t<0$, the same absolute-value estimate gives $\mathcal C\ge(2-n\varepsilon)|FF_t|\ge|FF_t|$.

Where $\eta\ne1$ we have $\psi=0$, and $$e\xi=\frac{s-\xi e\eta}{\eta}\ge\frac{3s}{4\eta}\ge\frac{3s}4.$$ Using $1\le\chi\le2$, [step:constants,step:brace-estimates] [step:constants](#step:constants), [step:brace-estimates](#step:brace-estimates) give $E\ge sF_x$ there. Where $\eta=1$, we have $e\xi=s-e\psi\ge s-b$, with $b^2=h_0(e,e)$ as in <a href="#step:trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:trace">[step:trace]</a>, because $|\mathrm d\psi|_{h_0}\le1$. If $s>8b$, the same estimates give $$E\ge(7s/4-20\varepsilon)F_x\ge sF_x.$$ It remains to consider $s_0\le s\le8b$. The possible deficit from $sF_x$ is at most $10F_x$: indeed $2\chi(s-b)-s\ge-4$ and $20\varepsilon<1$. On the favorable pressure branch, however, $$E-sF_x\ge[20\chi^2+2\chi(s-b)-s]F_x\ge0,$$ since $s\le8b\le8$. Thus a deficit is possible only when $F_x\le b_0t^2$. In that case $b\ge s_0/8$ and our choice of $b_0$ gives $10b_0\le\delta(s_0/8)^2$. We conclude throughout $s\ge s_0$ that <a id="step:large-slope"></a>$$\label{step:large-slope}
 E\ge sF_x-\delta b^2t^2
 \ge4(1+s)t-\delta b^2t^2.$$ The last inequality follows from $F_x\ge1000t$ and $s\ge.01$. Combining [step:beta-budget,step:E,step:trace] [step:beta-budget](#step:beta-budget), [step:E](#step:E), [step:trace](#step:trace) proves the raw inequality in this region, even without its $d$ term.

Now suppose $0<s<s_0$. Since $m\le-1/2$, the first coefficient in $\mathcal C$ is at least $3/4$. Using $|F_t|\le\varepsilon|F|$ gives $$\mathcal C\ge(3/4-4\varepsilon-n\varepsilon^2)F^2\ge\tfrac12F^2.$$ Where $\eta=1$, $e\xi/\chi\ge-1$. Elsewhere, a negative $e\xi$ can occur only if $s\le|\mathrm d\eta|_h$, and then $$\frac{|e\xi|}{\chi}
 \le\frac{|\mathrm d\eta|_h s}{s_0\eta}
 \le\frac{|\mathrm d\eta|_h^2}{s_0\eta}\le C_1.$$ By the quadratic pressure upper bound, <a id="step:small-slope"></a>$$\label{step:small-slope}
 E\ge\chi^2\{(1/2-2C_1\varepsilon)F^2-2C_1b_0t^2\}
 \ge-C_2\chi^2t^2.$$ Since $\theta\chi^2\le2a^2s_0^2$, the loss in <a href="#step:E" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:E">[step:E]</a> is at most $2C_2a^2t^2$. Also $-\beta\le\theta\le a^2s_0^2$ and $t\ge1$, so $t_u^2-t^2\le3a^2t^2$. The choice in <a href="#step:a-choice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:a-choice">[step:a-choice]</a> pays both terms with $(d/2)t^2$. The trace penalty is favorable and may be omitted in this regime. This proves <a href="#step:raw-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:raw-inequality">[step:raw-inequality]</a> wherever $s>0$. At a zero of $\mathrm du$ it follows by continuity unless $\mathrm du$ vanishes on a neighborhood; on such a neighborhood the raw output equals the input and the inequality is immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Cutting off the deformation and obtaining distance

Define on the original manifold <a id="step:final-output"></a>$$\label{step:final-output}
 h'=h+\zeta(h_u-h),\qquad t'=t+\zeta(t_u-t),$$ and extend by $(h,t)$ outside the auxiliary neighborhood. The result is smooth, $h'\ge h$, $t'\ge t$, and its changes have compact support in $\{r_A<W\}$. We now choose $r$ small enough to verify both required estimates.

First, the scalar inequality must use the trace of the actual cutoff metric. On the fixed compact annulus $\mathcal A_\zeta$, define the residual for any positive definite metric $H$ and function $z$ by $$\mathfrak E(H,z)=\mathcal D(H,z)-\mathcal D(h,t)-(z^2-t^2)
 +\delta\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_hh_0-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_Hh_0\bigr)z^2+dz^2.$$ This is a continuous function of the $C^2$ jets of $(H,z)$ on a compact set of positive definite metrics. By <a href="#step:raw-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:raw-inequality">[step:raw-inequality]</a>, $$\mathfrak E(h_u,t_u)\ge dt_u^2-(d/2)t^2\ge(d/2)T^2.$$ The annulus lies compactly in the fixed region $\psi=0$, $\eta_1=0$. By <a href="#step:locality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:locality">10</a>, $u_r\to0$ there in $C^3$; hence both the raw increments and their products with $\zeta$ tend to zero in $C^2$. Choose $r$ so small that $$|\mathfrak E(h',t')-\mathfrak E(h_u,t_u)|<dT^2/4$$ on this annulus. Then $\mathfrak E(h',t')>0$ there. Off the annulus the output is either raw or unchanged, and the same desired inequality holds directly. This proves <a href="#step:conclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:conclusion">[step:conclusion]</a>. In particular, no rank-one trace identity for the uncut metric has been substituted for the trace of the cutoff metric.

It remains to prove the distance gain, including for curves that revisit the cutoff annulus or leave the auxiliary neighborhood. Introduce the globally defined effective height $$v_{\mathrm{eff}}=\zeta u,$$ extended by zero outside the neighborhood. On $A$ the height barrier and <a href="#step:cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:cutoffs">[step:cutoffs]</a> give $v_{\mathrm{eff}}>W/5-1$, whereas on $B$ it is zero. Decrease $r$ further, if necessary, so that <a id="step:height-cutoff"></a>$$\label{step:height-cutoff}
 |\mathrm dv_{\mathrm{eff}}|_h\le .01$$ throughout $\mathcal A_\zeta$; locality justifies this choice. Consider a piecewise smooth curve from $A$ to $B$ of $h'$ length at most $L_*W$. Its $h$ length is at most $L_*W$. Partition its parameter interval according to $0<\zeta<1$, $\zeta=1$, and $\zeta=0$. At points where $\zeta$ equals either endpoint, $\mathrm d\zeta=0$ because $\zeta$ is smooth and valued in $[0,1]$. The total variation of $v_{\mathrm{eff}}$ on $\{0<\zeta<1\}$ is at most $.01L_*W$, counting all repeated visits. On the region where $\zeta=1$ and $s\le5s_0=.05$, its variation is at most $.05L_*W$. Where $\zeta=0$ it has zero variation. Therefore the variation on $$\Omega_{\mathrm{graph}}=\{\zeta=1,\ s\ge .05\}$$ is greater than $$(1/5-.06L_*)W-1>.11W.$$ The strict last inequality holds for our fixed sufficiently large $W$. On this region $h'=h+\mathrm du^2$, while everywhere $h'\ge h$. If $\gamma$ is parametrized on an interval $I$, the integral triangle inequality in $\mathbb R^2$ yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{Length}_{h'}(\gamma)
 &\ge\int_I\sqrt{|\dot\gamma|_h^2+
      \mathbf1_{\Omega_{\mathrm{graph}}}|(u\circ\gamma)'|^2}
       \,\mathrm dq\\
 &\ge\left\{
   \operatorname{Length}_h(\gamma)^2+
   \left(\int_{\gamma^{-1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{graph}})}
     |(u\circ\gamma)'|\,\mathrm dq\right)^2
   \right\}^{1/2}\\
 &>W\sqrt{1+.11^2}>L_*W.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $\operatorname{Length}_h(\gamma)\ge W$ follows from $h\ge h_0$ and the original separation of $A,B$. This contradiction proves <a href="#step:distance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:distance">[step:distance]</a>; curves with infinite length are irrelevant. Finally, a smooth metric dominating a complete metric is complete: an $h'$-Cauchy sequence is $h_0$-Cauchy, and local equivalence of the two smooth metrics supplies convergence in $h'$. The proof of <a href="#step:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="step:main">8</a> is complete.

<a id="sec:rounds"></a>
### Wide separations at a fixed original scale

The local estimate pays for a metric change by a decrease in a trace. That decrease can be summed over arbitrarily many labels. We first carry out this summation, then explain why different labels may require different finite numbers of rounds.

<a id="rounds:delta"></a>
Fix $L_*=1.004$, and choose $\delta>0$, depending only on $n$, so small that $$\label{rounds:delta}
  2(n+1)\delta<1-L_*^{-2}.$$ Let $W,T$ be the constants in [step:main](#step:main). Enlarge $T$ if necessary to make $T\ge1$, and choose <a id="rounds:original-scale"></a>$$\label{rounds:original-scale}
  R_0\ge\sqrt2\,T,\qquad D=WR_0.$$ All of these constants depend only on $n$.

<a id="stmt-rounds-round"></a>
<div id="rounds:round" class="lemma">

<a id="rounds:separation"></a>
<a id="rounds:curvature"></a>
**Lemma 12** (A complete round). *Suppose that $h_0$ is a complete metric, $t_0\ge T$ is smooth, and $$\mathcal D(h_0,t_0)\ge c+t_0^2$$ for a constant $c$. Let $(A_i,B_i)$ be a finite or countable family of nonempty closed pairs, with each $A_i$ compact and $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_{h_0}(A_i,B_i)\ge W$. Suppose there are locally finite compact sets $K_i$ such that the open $h_0$-distance-$W$ neighborhood of $A_i$ is contained in $K_i\setminus B_i$. Then there are smooth $h_1\ge h_0$ and $t_1\ge t_0$, obtained by a locally finite sequence of compactly supported changes inside the $K_i$, such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_{h_1}(A_i,B_i)&\ge L_*W \quad\text{for every }i,\label{rounds:separation}\\
 \mathcal D(h_1,t_1)&\ge c+
 \bigl(1-(n+1)\delta\bigr)t_1^2.\label{rounds:curvature}
\end{aligned}$$ The metric $h_1$ is complete.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rounds-round"><summary>Proof of stmt-rounds-round</summary>

<a id="rounds:trace-induction"></a>
*Proof.* Enumerate the labels and choose $d_i>0$ with $\sum_i d_i<\delta$. Apply [step:main](#step:main) in this order, keeping $h_0$ as the background metric throughout. Earlier changes increase distances, so every pair still meets the separation hypothesis when it is processed. If $(h^{(j)},t^{(j)})$ denotes the data after $j$ changes, write $$Q_j=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_{h^{(j)}}h_0,\qquad
 A_j=1-\delta(n-Q_j)-\sum_{i\le j}d_i.$$ Thus $Q_0=n$, $0<Q_j\le n$, and $A_j\le1$. We claim that $$\label{rounds:trace-induction}
 \mathcal D(h^{(j)},t^{(j)})\ge c+A_j(t^{(j)})^2.$$ It holds at $j=0$. In a single step, abbreviate the old coefficient by $A$, the new one by $A'$, and the old and new functions by $t,t'$. The local estimate gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal D(h',t')
 &\ge c+At^2+(t'^2-t^2)
       -\bigl(\delta(Q-Q')+d\bigr)t'^2\\
 &=c+A't'^2+(1-A)(t'^2-t^2)\ge c+A't'^2.
\end{aligned}$$ The last inequality uses both $A\le1$ and $t'\ge t\ge T>0$. This proves <a href="#rounds:trace-induction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rounds:trace-induction">[rounds:trace-induction]</a>. Its coefficient is greater than $1-(n+1)\delta$, which proves <a href="#rounds:curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rounds:curvature">[rounds:curvature]</a>. Each processed separation satisfies <a href="#rounds:separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rounds:separation">[rounds:separation]</a>, and later changes preserve it.

The supports lie in the locally finite family $(K_i)$. Hence every point has a neighborhood on which the sequence is eventually constant; its limit is smooth and satisfies the same inequalities. Completeness follows from $h_1\ge h_0$: an $h_1$-Cauchy sequence converges in $h_0$, and near its limit the two smooth metrics are equivalent. ◻

</details>

The passage from a positive scalar-curvature form to circle warps uses torical symmetrization; see Misha Gromov (2018, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[gromov2018Metric](#ref-gromov2018Metric)]. The following identity fixes the finite-dimensional convention used here.

<a id="stmt-rounds-warp-identity"></a>
<div id="rounds:warp-identity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Scalar curvature of diagonal circle warps). *For smooth functions $p_1,\ldots,p_k$ on $(M,h)$, the metric $\widehat h=h+\sum_{\alpha=1}^k e^{2p_\alpha}\mathrm d\theta_\alpha^2$ on $M\times\mathbb T^k$ has scalar curvature $$\mathop{\mathrm{Scal}}_{\widehat h}=\mathop{\mathrm{Scal}}_h-2\Delta_h P-|\mathrm dP|_h^2
                    -\sum_{\alpha=1}^k|\mathrm dp_\alpha|_h^2,
 \qquad P=\sum_{\alpha=1}^k p_\alpha.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rounds-warp-identity"><summary>Proof of stmt-rounds-warp-identity</summary>

*Proof.* Let $E_\alpha=e^{-p_\alpha}\partial_{\theta_\alpha}$ be the unit vertical fields. For horizontal fields $X,Y$, the Koszul formula gives $$\widehat\nabla_XY=\nabla^h_XY,\quad
 \widehat\nabla_XE_\alpha=0,\quad
 \widehat\nabla_{E_\alpha}X=(Xp_\alpha)E_\alpha,\quad
 \widehat\nabla_{E_\alpha}E_\alpha=-\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}_h p_\alpha,$$ and $\widehat\nabla_{E_\alpha}E_\beta=0$ for $\alpha\ne\beta$. Therefore, for a horizontal unit vector $X$, the mixed sectional curvatures are $$K(X,E_\alpha)=-\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}_h p_\alpha(X,X)-(Xp_\alpha)^2,
 \qquad
 K(E_\alpha,E_\beta)=-\langle\mathrm dp_\alpha,\mathrm dp_\beta\rangle_h.$$ Summing twice over all orthonormal two-planes gives the identity. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rounds-wide"></a>
<div id="rounds:wide" class="theorem">

<a id="rounds:prescribed"></a>
<a id="rounds:stabilized"></a>
**Theorem 14** (Prescribed widths for a locally finite family). *Let $(M,g)$ be complete, connected and smooth, with $\mathop{\mathrm{Scal}}_g\ge1$. There is a countable set of centers $(x_i)$ such that $$A_i=\overline B_g(x_i,D),\qquad
 B_i=M\setminus B_g(x_i,2D)$$ have the following properties: the $A_i$ cover $M$, and the closed balls $\overline B_g(x_i,2D)$ are locally finite. Suppose that every $B_i$ is nonempty. Given arbitrary finite numbers $L_i>0$, there exist a complete smooth metric $h\ge g$ and a countable family of smooth functions $(p_\alpha)$, whose supports form a locally finite family of compact sets, for which $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_h(A_i,B_i)&>L_i \quad(i\ge1),\label{rounds:prescribed}\\
 \mathop{\mathrm{Scal}}_h-2\Delta_h\sum_\alpha p_\alpha
       -\left|\mathrm d\sum_\alpha p_\alpha\right|_h^2
       -\sum_\alpha|\mathrm dp_\alpha|_h^2&\ge\frac25.\label{rounds:stabilized}
\end{aligned}$$ Every sum in <a href="#rounds:stabilized" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rounds:stabilized">[rounds:stabilized]</a> is locally finite. If some $B_i$ is empty, then $\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}_g M\le4D$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rounds-wide"><summary>Proof of stmt-rounds-wide</summary>

*Proof.* A complete connected Riemannian manifold is proper. Choose a maximal $D$-separated set of centers. Maximality gives the covering by closed $D$-balls. A compact set contains only finitely many centers; more generally, centers whose closed $2D$-balls meet a fixed compact set lie in a fixed compact neighborhood of that set. This proves local finiteness of the outer balls and countability of the net. The triangle inequality gives $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_g(A_i,B_i)\ge D$. The last assertion follows immediately if $M=B_g(x_i,2D)$.

<a id="rounds:start"></a>
We keep a scalar reserve of $1/2$ in the original units. Put $R_j=L_*^jR_0$, start with $h^{[0]}=g$, $t^{[0]}=T$, and work in round $j$ with the metric $h^{[j]}/R_j^2$. At the start of that round we require $$\label{rounds:start}
 \mathcal D(h^{[j]}/R_j^2,t^{[j]})
 \ge \frac{R_j^2}{2}+(t^{[j]})^2.$$ For $j=0$, this follows from $\mathop{\mathrm{Scal}}_g\ge1$ and $R_0^2/2\ge T^2$.

A label remains active until its separation exceeds its prescribed $L_i$. At the start of round $j$, every active pair has separation at least $WR_j$ in $h^{[j]}$; this is initially true and follows inductively from <a href="#rounds:separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rounds:separation">[rounds:separation]</a>. Apply [rounds:round](#rounds:round) to the active pairs in the working metric, with reserve $c=R_j^2/2$. The required support condition follows from $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_{h^{[j]}/R_j^2}(A_i,B_i)\ge W$: the open distance-$W$ neighborhood of $A_i$ misses $B_i$, and is therefore contained in the original outer ball $B_g(x_i,2D)$. We may take $K_i=\overline B_g(x_i,2D)$.

Return the output metric to original units and denote it by $h^{[j+1]}$. It dominates $h^{[j]}$. Scalar curvature, the Laplacian and squared gradient norms all multiply by $R^2$ when a metric is divided by $R^2$. Consequently, on passing from working scale $R_j$ to $R_{j+1}$, the round estimate becomes $$\mathcal D(h^{[j+1]}/R_{j+1}^2,t^{[j+1]})
 \ge \frac{R_{j+1}^2}{2}
 +L_*^2\bigl(1-(n+1)\delta\bigr)(t^{[j+1]})^2.$$ By <a href="#rounds:delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rounds:delta">[rounds:delta]</a>, the coefficient of the final square is greater than one. Thus <a href="#rounds:start" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rounds:start">[rounds:start]</a> is restored. In original units the reserve remains exactly $1/2$; it has not been spent by the change of scale.

Each fixed label becomes inactive after finitely many rounds, since its separation is at least $WR_j$ as long as it remains active and $R_j\to\infty$. A compact set meets only finitely many of the original outer balls. Each of their labels is processed only finitely many times. Thus there are only finitely many changes on a neighborhood of any compact set over the *entire* construction, not merely in each round. The limiting data $(h,t)$ are smooth, satisfy $$h\ge g,\qquad t\ge T,\qquad \mathcal D(h,t)\ge\frac12,$$ and satisfy <a href="#rounds:prescribed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rounds:prescribed">[rounds:prescribed]</a>. The same comparison argument as in [rounds:round](#rounds:round) proves completeness of $h$.

It remains to realize the drift term by ordinary finite-dimensional warped products on each compact window. Enumerate the nonzero compactly supported increments of $t$ as $\tau_\nu\ge0$, so that $t-T=\sum_\nu\tau_\nu$. The supports are locally finite. For each $\nu$, choose an integer $m_\nu\ge1$ such that $$\frac1{m_\nu}\sup_M|\mathrm d\tau_\nu|_h^2\le\frac{2^{-\nu}}{10}.$$ The supremum is finite because the support is compact. Use $m_\nu$ fresh log warps, all equal to $-\tau_\nu/m_\nu$. Listing these functions as $(p_\alpha)$, we obtain $$\sum_\alpha p_\alpha=-(t-T),\qquad
 \sum_\alpha|\mathrm dp_\alpha|_h^2\le\frac1{10}.$$ On a neighborhood meeting only finitely many supports, equip a finite product of circles with metric $h+\sum_\alpha e^{2p_\alpha}\mathrm d\theta_\alpha^2$, omitting all unused unit factors. Its scalar curvature is the left side of <a href="#rounds:stabilized" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rounds:stabilized">[rounds:stabilized]</a>; this is the identity in [rounds:warp-identity](#rounds:warp-identity). Substitution gives $\mathcal D(h,t)-\sum_\alpha|\mathrm dp_\alpha|_h^2\ge1/2-1/10=2/5$. This proves <a href="#rounds:stabilized" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rounds:stabilized">[rounds:stabilized]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rounds-no-infinite-torus"></a>
<div id="rounds:no-infinite-torus" class="remark">

*Remark 15*. The list of warps is bookkeeping for compatible finite-dimensional calculations. We never construct an infinite-dimensional torus. Adding unit factors changes neither the weight nor the scalar formula. After the radial partition in [countable:construction](#countable:construction), only finitely many initial warp supports occur on any whole packet; later stages add finite groups of factors. This stronger finiteness is used in the one-dimensional obstruction, not just local finiteness of a series on the original manifold.

</div>

<a id="sec:finite-cuts"></a>
### Finite cuts with compatible ends

The stretching construction supplies separated sets and a scalar-curvature reserve. We now describe the second input: a cutting procedure that retains both sides of every cut and controls how later cuts meet them. Its output is a family of smooth walls and complete manifolds obtained by attaching cylinders to the closed sides. The definitions below make its hypotheses and output explicit; Appendices <a href="#sec:packets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:packets">9</a>–<a href="#sec:regularization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:regularization">13</a> give the proofs. Section <a href="#sec:countable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:countable">6</a> will separately justify its use with countably many labels.

Let $X=M$ denote the original complete manifold, let $g$ be its original metric, and write $d_0=d_g$ for its distance. The metric used in an analytic packet can change; the map to $X$ always records its position in the original manifold. Fix finitely many of the supplied ball pairs $$A_i=\overline B_{d_0}(x_i,D),\qquad
 B_i=X\setminus B_{d_0}(x_i,2D),\qquad 1\le i\le\ell,$$ with $A_i,B_i$ nonempty. There is one label for each ball pair; these labels are also called *colors*. An additional first label is radial. Thus $N_*=\ell+1$, and the order is radial, then $1,\ldots,\ell$. Properness of $(X,g)$ makes each closed outer ball compact. No covering-space or quotient hypothesis is used.

The analytic input at stage zero is a complete smooth metric $h\ge g$ on $X$ and finitely many smooth positive circle warps for which the stabilized scalar curvature is at least $.4$. Each prescribed distance $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_h(A_i,B_i)$ can be required to exceed any given finite number. <a href="#rounds:wide" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rounds:wide">14</a> supplies these data. For finitely many requested pairs its construction uses only finitely many compactly supported increments and hence finitely many circle factors. The countable version has a locally finite list of nonunit warps and is used in <a href="#countable:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="countable:construction">26</a>; the geometric statements below apply to each finite collection of the relevant factors. No uniform geometry bounds on $h$, or uniform packetwise constants, are assumed.

For a single smooth cut, let $u$ be a smooth function on an $m$-manifold $E$ without boundary, with $0$ a regular value, and put $L=u^{-1}(0)$. Attach $L\times[0,\infty)$ to each closed side $\{\pm u\ge0\}$ along its boundary, and also retain $L$ as a separate space. The two extended sides have dimension $m$, while the wall has dimension $m-1$. The cylinders replace the new boundaries by ends. The following definition records these spaces and their incidences after repeated cuts.

<a id="stmt-packet-category"></a>
<div id="packet:category" class="definition">

**Definition 16** (Packet diagram). At stage $r$, a status is $\alpha\in\{+,-,*\}^{r}$. Its entries record the first $r$ processed cuts. A sign $+$ or $-$ selects the corresponding retained side of a cut, and a star $*$ selects the wall, called a *hit*. The space $E_\alpha$ is a smooth second countable manifold without boundary, possibly empty or disconnected; when nonempty its base dimension is $m(\alpha)=n-\#\{i:\alpha_i=*\}$. Its connected components are called *packets*. Replacing signs by stars gives the face order. If $B$ is a packet with $d$ additional stars relative to a coarser status, then for that coarser status it has exactly one incident packet $E$ and one corresponding occurrence of a full product tail <a id="packet:tail"></a>$$\label{packet:tail}
 \iota_{BE}:B\times(T_1,\infty)\times\cdots\times(T_d,\infty)
       \longrightarrow E.$$ The normal coordinates are labelled by the changed status entries. Thresholds may depend on the incidence. For a single entry, tails from distinct face components are disjoint. Intersections of sufficiently far tails for different entries are exactly the tails over their common further faces. The identifications commute: composing face maps in any order gives the same occurrence and the same individually labelled normal coordinates. After slightly raising the entry thresholds, each closed tail is properly embedded.

Each packet $E$ is equipped with a continuous map $q_E:E\to X$. After the radial stage we require the following additional properties. Every packet has only finitely many incident faces and cofaces, including through chains; the complement of its sufficiently far elementary tails is compact. On each sufficiently far face product these maps satisfy $q_E(\iota_{BE}(b,\mathbf t))=q_B(b)$. A metric, scalar function, warp, or set is *compatible* if, in every sufficiently far tail, it is exactly the product or pullback of the corresponding face datum. For metrics the normal factors are $\mathrm dt_1^2+\cdots+\mathrm dt_d^2$; all warps are independent of the normal coordinates there. The thresholds for different data may differ.

</div>

There is no boundary in an analytic packet. Its face is represented by an entire cylindrical end, not by a boundary subset. Finite boundaries appear only when we truncate these ends.

A *regular split* applies the preceding construction using compatible smooth defining functions whose zero sets are regular on every status space. Each zero set is the new hit space. In inherited ends the operation is the corresponding face operation times the old normal factors. The proof that these operations preserve the packet diagram is <a href="#packet:split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:split">31</a>.

For the diagrams constructed here, these maps are defined recursively, starting with $q_X=\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}_X$. In the split of <a href="#packet:split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:split">31</a>, let $A$ be a new stage-$(r+1)$ packet with stage-$r$ parent $E$. Define $p_A:A\to E$ by inclusion on a hit space or the bulk of a joined side, and by $p_A(x,t)=x$ on each newly attached cylinder. Set $q_A=q_E\circ p_A$. Thus $q_A$ is pulled back from the base on each fresh cylinder. In a sufficiently far inherited tail of $A$, let $C$ be the corresponding new face with old parent $B$, and let $I$ denote the old normal intervals. Under the identifications of these tails with $C\times I$ and $B\times I$, the map $p_A$ is $p_C\times\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}_I$; explicitly, $$p_A\circ\iota_{CA}=\iota_{BE}\circ(p_C\times\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}_I),\qquad
 q_A\bigl(\iota_{CA}(c,\mathbf t)\bigr)=q_C(c).$$ Thus the recursion preserves compatibility. The proper maps $q_r$ on finite reconstructions are constructed in <a href="#packet:reconstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:reconstruction">35</a>.

Figure <a href="#fig:regular-packet-split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:regular-packet-split">1</a> depicts one extended side and the separate hit space.

<figure id="fig:regular-packet-split">

<figcaption>A regular cut retains each closed sign side with a full cylinder attached, and also retains the wall as a separate hit space of one lower base dimension. The attachment seam is internal to the extended side. In old product ends this entire operation is a product. The raw metric and its later smoothing are defined in Appendices <a href="#sec:cut" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cut">10</a> and <a href="#sec:regularization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:regularization">13</a>.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-regular-packet-split"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:regular-packet-split`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,>=Stealth,font=\small]
  \fill[blue!7] (0,0) rectangle (2.3,2.2);
  \fill[orange!12] (2.3,0) rectangle (5,2.2);
  \draw[thick] (0,0) -- (5,0);
  \draw[thick] (0,2.2) -- (5,2.2);
  \draw[densely dashed,thick] (2.3,0) -- (2.3,2.2);
  \node[align=center] at (1.1,1.2) {retained\\closed side};
  \node[align=center] at (3.6,1.2) {$L\times[0,\infty)$\\attached\\cylinder};
  \draw[->,thick] (4.4,.5) -- (5.3,.5) node[right] {$s$};
  \node[align=center,below] at (2.3,-.1) {internal attachment seam};
  \draw[very thick,teal!70!black] (7,0) -- (7,2.2);
  \node[align=center,right] at (7.2,1.1) {separate hit space $L$\\base dimension $m-1$};
  \node[above] at (2.5,2.7) {either sign side: base dimension $m$};
\end{tikzpicture}
```


The label set and the status depth are finite in this section. The family of connected packets may be countable; finiteness is required only for the directed face and coface chains incident to each packet. The downward diagram of a packet consists of that packet and all its further faces. Numerical requests are allocated downward through these chains before actual data are constructed upward from faces. This does not require finite components of the undirected incidence graph.

<a id="packet:constants"></a>
We now specify the analytic data that must survive each split. For the finite-label statements in this section and the appendices we set $\sigma=1$ and use the following constants: $$\label{packet:constants}
\begin{gathered}
 \lambda_r=\frac14-\frac{r}{8N_*},\qquad
 b_r=1.12-\frac{.05r}{N_*},\qquad
 a_r=.60+\frac{.03r}{N_*}\quad(0\le r\le N_*),\\
 \pi_0=\frac{1}{10N_*},\qquad k=399,\qquad
 C=\frac{4k}{k+1}=3.99,\qquad
 e_0=\frac{\lambda_r-\lambda_{r+1}}{100}
     =\frac{1}{800N_*}\quad(r<N_*).
\end{gathered}$$ Here $k$ is the number of circle factors added at each split; the initial number may be different. In particular $\lambda_r\ge1/8$, $a_r\le.63$, and $1.07\le b_r\le1.12$. The common floor $\pi_0$ and the equally spaced stage parameters are bookkeeping for a finite list, not uniform lower bounds for the countable construction.

<a id="stmt-packet-invariant"></a>
<div id="packet:invariant" class="definition">

<a id="packet:required-set"></a>
**Definition 17** (Analytic invariant). At stage $r$, write $J_r$ for the unprocessed labels. On a packet $E$ of base dimension $m$, retain its base metric and all circle warps. Write $R_E$ for the scalar curvature of that full warped metric and $\omega_E$ for the product of its circle lengths, suppressing fixed circle-volume constants. Gradients of invariant functions are base gradients; their integrals use $\omega_E\,\mathrm d\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_E$, and their Laplacian is the corresponding weighted base Laplacian. For every $j\in J_r$ the data comprise a compatible continuous profile $\mu_j:E\to[-\infty,\infty]$ with locally Lipschitz finite truncations, a compatible smooth potential $\Pi_j\ge\pi_0$, and compatible open cores $U_j^+,U_j^-$ satisfying <a id="packet:core-poles"></a>$$\label{packet:core-poles}
 \overline{U_j^\pm}\subset
 \operatorname{int}_E\{\mu_j=\pm\infty\}.$$ On the open finite band the profile obeys, almost everywhere, <a id="packet:profile"></a>$$\label{packet:profile}
 2|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}\mu_j|\le a_r\mu_j^2+\Pi_j.$$ Set $$\label{packet:required-set}
 G_j=E\setminus(U_j^+\cup U_j^-),\qquad
 D_E=\bigl\{x:\#\{j\in J_r:x\in G_j\}\ge m-1\bigr\}.$$ The required scalar bound is <a id="packet:curvature"></a>$$\label{packet:curvature}
 R_E\ge\lambda_r+b_r\sum_{j\in J_r}\Pi_j
       \quad\hbox{on }D_E.$$ For an unprocessed color $i$, the cores contain $q_E^{-1}(A_i)\subset U_i^+$ and $q_E^{-1}(B_i)\subset U_i^-$. Enlarging cores must preserve these conditions. The sets $D_E$ are closed: they are finite unions of finite intersections of the closed sets $G_j$ (and equal $E$ when the count threshold is nonpositive). All data are exact products in the sense of <a href="#packet:category" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:category">16</a>; at stage zero the single space is $X$, with no product-end requirement.

</div>
