The threshold $m-1$ is preserved by the two kinds of output of a split. A side keeps its dimension and loses one unprocessed label; a hit loses both one dimension and that label. The lower threshold $m-2$ defines a guard containing the projections of all new required sets, even after the remaining cores have been enlarged.

<a id="stmt-packet-counts"></a>
<div id="packet:counts" class="lemma">

<a id="packet:guard"></a>
**Lemma 18** (The two count inclusions). *If $B$ is a $d$-fold further face of $E$, the product projection sends $D_E$ in that tail into $D_B$. At a split of label $j_0$ on a parent of dimension $m$, suppose the remaining cores have been enlarged, giving $G'_j\subset G_j$ for $j\ne j_0$, and define the closed guard $$\label{packet:guard}
 H_E^*=\bigl\{x:\#\{j\in J_r\setminus\{j_0\}:x\in G'_j\}
                      \ge m-2\bigr\}.$$ Suppose a regular zero wall $L$ lies in $\operatorname{int}G_{j_0}$. Pull the remaining cores to a new side or hit packet $A$ by bulk inclusion, wall restriction, and projection of the attached cylinder. Define $D_A$ using these cores, the remaining labels, and the dimension of $A$, as in <a href="#packet:required-set" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="packet:required-set">[packet:required-set]</a>. Then $D_A$ projects into both $H_E^*$ and the parent’s old set $D_E$. Guards likewise project into guards on further faces.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-packet-counts"><summary>Proof of stmt-packet-counts</summary>

*Proof.* In an exact product tail all label memberships pull back unchanged, whereas the face dimension is $m-d$. A count at least $m-1$ is at least $(m-d)-1$, proving the first assertion. The same argument with thresholds $m-2$ and $(m-d)-2$ proves the guard assertion. On a new side, whose dimension is $m$, a point of $D_A$ has at least $m-1$ remaining undecided labels. This puts its projection in the guard and, because $G'_j\subset G_j$, in the old required set. On a hit packet, whose dimension is $m-1$, the required count is $m-2$. Its projection lies on $L\subset G_{j_0}$, so the processed label contributes one further old undecided label. The old count is therefore at least $m-1$. The cylinder projection has exactly the same remaining memberships as its base, so the side argument also covers all cylinder points. These are pointwise count statements and use no scalar estimate away from $D_E$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-packet-initialization"></a>
<div id="packet:initialization" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (Initialization and radial confinement). *Let the stage-zero stabilized scalar curvature be at least $.4$, and suppose that every color pair has separation $$\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_h(A_i,B_i)>T_0+4,\qquad
 T_0=2\pi\sqrt{2/\pi_0}.$$ Then the data of <a href="#packet:invariant" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:invariant">17</a> exist at stage zero with $\Pi_j=\pi_0$. On a noncompact $X$ the radial cores can be chosen as alternating separating subbands of a smooth proper function, with compact smooth barrier domains around their closures lying in the same pole plateaus. Every regular radial partition respecting these signs has locally finite compact wall components and compact closed-side components before collar attachment. On a compact $X$ a decided radial label with empty wall has the same properties.*

</div>

<a id="stmt-finite-cut"></a>
<div id="finite:cut" class="theorem">

**Theorem 20** (Finite compatible cutting). *Fix a finite number $N_*$ of labels and the stage constants in <a href="#packet:constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="packet:constants">[packet:constants]</a>. Suppose a stage-$r$ packet diagram is obtained by the regular splitting construction of <a href="#packet:split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:split">31</a>, with compatible smooth base metrics and a finite common list of positive circle warps, and satisfies <a href="#packet:invariant" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:invariant">17</a>. After the radial stage, require the complete compact-part/product-end geometry of [packet:category,packet:geometry] [packet:category](#packet:category), [packet:geometry](#packet:geometry). Let $j_0$ be the next label, and suppose every nonempty parent packet has base dimension at least two. Then $j_0$ can be processed as follows.*

*On every parent there is a compatible smooth regular defining function with the prescribed signs on the current forced core closures. Keep its zero wall as the new hit space. To each closed sign side attach the full product cylinder over its boundary. These pieces form a compatible smooth stage-$(r+1)$ packet diagram with the continuous projections described above. The wall on a parent $E$ lies in $\operatorname{int}G_{j_0}$. The remaining profiles may first be modified, their cores enlarged, and their potentials increased in the parents; these data are then carried to the new pieces by restriction and cylinder projection. There are compatible smooth metrics and potentials, and exactly $399$ new positive circle factors on each new packet, for which the entire stage-$(r+1)$ invariant holds. Earlier forced signs are preserved. The new packets have complete compact-part/product-end geometry. This assertion includes a possible one-dimensional output, on which its scalar lower bound holds everywhere.*

*The operation fixes every old warp identically equal to $1$. For a remaining label that is passive on the finite downward source diagrams and the face copies used in their comparisons—constant pole profile, full corresponding core, and spatially constant potential $c_j$ agreeing on compared faces—the construction can preserve the pole and core and choose its new potential to be exactly $(1+3\eta_r/2)c_j$, where $\eta_r=1/(200N_*)$.*

*At stage zero the single parent may instead be an arbitrary complete smooth manifold with no product-end or bounded-geometry assumption. Subject to the same scalar and separation assumptions, if its data are the radial initialization of <a href="#packet:initialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:initialization">19</a>, the same conclusion holds for the first split. Every resulting wall component and every closed-side component is compact before the cylinders are attached, and these components form locally finite families.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-finite-cut"><summary>Proof of stmt-finite-cut</summary>

*Proof.* Realize the processed profile by a smooth finite pressure and take a local absolute minimizer of weighted perimeter minus its pressure-volume term, as in [cut:pressure,cut:bv-compactness] [cut:pressure](#cut:pressure), [cut:bv-compactness](#cut:bv-compactness). The phase has the forced signs of <a href="#cut:barriers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:barriers">40</a>; its boundary, called the true cut, has the regular-part geometry of <a href="#cut:gmt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:gmt">42</a> but may still have singularities. After the radial cut, the generic pressure choice in <a href="#cut:generic-uniqueness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:generic-uniqueness">41</a> makes these true cuts converge to the corresponding face products at packet ends (<a href="#cut:end-convergence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:end-convergence">43</a>). On the initial radial parent use shift zero and a phase from compactness; compact barriers replace this post-radial growth and uniqueness argument.

The wall, and each retained side with its attached cylinder, carry the quadratic-form lower bounds of <a href="#cut:raw-domination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:raw-domination">46</a>. These bounds include a positive gradient reserve and, on a joined side, the scalar contribution of its internal attachment seam. They are not yet pointwise scalar bounds on smooth new packets. Keep these true cuts and their pressures fixed. The constructions of [local:anchors](#local:anchors), [local:sharp](#local:sharp), [shield:global](#shield:global) change only the remaining profiles, enlarge their cores, and add nonnegative potentials. Their form cost is paid by the gradient reserve and the gap $\lambda_r-\lambda_{r+1}$, and the updated guard $H_E^*$ of <a href="#packet:guard" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="packet:guard">[packet:guard]</a> misses the singular set. Thus the future required regions can be controlled through regular portions of the true cut. When the parent dimension is at most seven the singular set is empty, and no singular forcing is needed; compactness on the resulting smooth windows is the smooth case of <a href="#shield:compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:compactness">55</a>. More generally this smooth-window case applies whenever the limiting wall is smooth, including products of smooth faces with Euclidean factors even when their total dimension is at least eight. At the first radial split the same arguments are applied on the locally finite compact transition blocks, as specified in <a href="#shield:global" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:global">59</a>.

The guarded approximation in <a href="#regular:partition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="regular:partition">61</a> now replaces the true cut by a smooth regular wall. Its defining functions are exact products in the old ends. The comparison with the true cut on the guard, followed by the seam smoothing of <a href="#regular:seam" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="regular:seam">63</a>, transfers the form bound to the smooth split. The positive supersolutions of [regular:correction,regular:solutions] [regular:correction](#regular:correction), [regular:solutions](#regular:solutions) then supply $399$ compatible new circle factors; the scalar identity in <a href="#regular:step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="regular:step">69</a> converts that form bound into the pointwise stage-$(r+1)$ scalar inequality. Throughout this transfer the parent scalar inequality is used only over $D_E$: <a href="#packet:counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:counts">18</a> places every new required region inside that old region as well as inside the guard.

give the new geometry; <a href="#packet:initialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:initialization">19</a> gives the radial confinement. Unit entries are fixed by restriction, product extension, and seam smoothing. For a remaining label passive in the sense stated above, we omit its forcing updates, and [regular:constant-preservation,regular:profiles] [regular:constant-preservation](#regular:constant-preservation), [regular:profiles](#regular:profiles) reproduce its chosen constant potential exactly. The choice of $\eta_r$ satisfies the strict stage margins in <a href="#regular:eta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="regular:eta">[regular:eta]</a>. ◻

</details>

This theorem is a finite-label statement. Its common positive floor and finite list of warp factors do not provide a common floor or a global finite list for the construction that follows. The next section proves the stronger locality property actually needed there: on each whole packet and each directed comparison diagram, only finitely many labels and warp factors are nonconstant, and the remaining potential sum is a spatial constant reproduced exactly by the construction.

<a id="sec:countable"></a>
### Countably many compatible partitions

The separation widths in [rounds:wide](#rounds:wide) may depend on the label. We now use this freedom to construct the partitions. The analytic construction is performed at finite stages. Its essential finiteness property concerns each *whole packet*, including all its infinite product ends; local finiteness merely on compact subsets would not suffice. The finite-label procedure is stated in [finite:cut](#finite:cut), and its detailed proof is in . We adapt that proof using individual positive floors and finite exceptional data on each whole packet. This section establishes their hypotheses for the countable construction and proves that those hypotheses persist.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Initialization and the inductive data

Let $D$, the centers $x_i$, and the pairs $(A_i,B_i)$ be as in [rounds:wide](#rounds:wide). We assume every $B_i$ is nonempty, since otherwise $\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}_g M\le4D$. There is one label for each center, and an additional label $0$ used first for radial confinement. The set of labels is finite or countable; write it as $I=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$, stopping the enumeration if necessary. Choose <a id="countable:floors"></a>$$\label{countable:floors}
 p_i^*>0,\qquad \sum_{i\in I}p_i^*=\frac1{20}.$$ Use [rounds:wide](#rounds:wide) with <a id="countable:widths"></a>$$\label{countable:widths}
 L_i=10+2\pi\sqrt{2/p_i^*}\qquad(i\ne0).$$ We henceforth use the resulting complete metric $h\ge g$, its locally finite list of log warps $p_\alpha$, and its stabilized scalar lower bound $2/5$.

The initial weighted base measure is globally well defined: $$\omega\,\mathrm d\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_h,\qquad
 \omega=\exp\Bigl(\sum_\alpha p_\alpha\Bigr)>0.$$ The sum is locally finite. On a buffered compact set, only finitely many nonunit factors $e^{p_\alpha}$ occur, so ordinary finite warped-product formulas compute the stabilized scalar, weighted variations, and invariant test forms there. These formulas agree on overlaps: adjoining a unit factor changes none of them. In particular, the initial problem is a weighted problem on the $n$-dimensional base $M$.

Fix numbers $$0=t_0<t_1<t_2<\cdots<1,\qquad t_r\longrightarrow1,$$ and use as many stages as there are labels. These are budget parameters, unrelated to a spatial coordinate. Put <a id="countable:constants"></a>$$\label{countable:constants}
 \begin{gathered}
 a_r=.60+.03t_r,\qquad b_r=1.12-.05t_r,
 \qquad \lambda_r=\frac14-\frac{t_r}{8},\\
 \Delta_r=t_{r+1}-t_r,\qquad
 e_r=\frac{\Delta_r}{800},\qquad
 \eta_r=\frac{\Delta_r}{200},\qquad k=399,
 \qquad C=\frac{4k}{k+1}=3.99.
 \end{gathered}$$ Thus $\lambda_r\ge1/8$, $.60\le a_r<.63$, and $1.07<b_r\le1.12$. No positive infimum of the $\Delta_r$ is required. In a stagewise appendix lemma, its symbol $e_0$ denotes the current $e_r$.

<a id="countable:profile-invariant"></a>
<a id="countable:scalar-invariant"></a>
We use the packet notation and regular splitting operation introduced in [sec:finite-cuts](#sec:finite-cuts). The countable invariant below replaces the common finite-label floor by the individual positive floors $p_j^*$. At stage $r$, the processed labels are $0,\ldots,r-1$ and the remaining set is $J_r$. A packet with $s$ hit entries has base dimension $m=n-s$. Its continuous collapse map to $M$ is denoted by $q_E$. For each $j\in J_r$ retain an extended-real continuous profile $\mu_j$ with locally Lipschitz finite truncations, a smooth potential $\Pi_j\ge p_j^*$, and disjoint open cores $U_j^+,U_j^-$ with $$\label{countable:profile-invariant}
 \overline{U_j^\pm}\subset\operatorname{int}\{\mu_j=\pm\infty\},
 \qquad 2|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}\mu_j|\le a_r\mu_j^2+\Pi_j
 \quad\hbox{almost everywhere on the finite band}.$$ For unprocessed center labels require $q_E^{-1}(A_j)\subset U_j^+$ and $q_E^{-1}(B_j)\subset U_j^-$. All data are compatible products in the packet ends. With <a id="countable:required"></a>$$\label{countable:required}
 G_j=E\setminus(U_j^+\cup U_j^-),\qquad
 D_E=\{x:\#\{j\in J_r:x\in G_j\}\ge m-1\},$$ the scalar requirement is $$\label{countable:scalar-invariant}
 R_E\ge\lambda_r+b_r\sum_{j\in J_r}\Pi_j\quad\hbox{on }D_E.$$ Here $R_E$ denotes the stabilized scalar expression, and integrals and Laplacians use the product of the old warp lengths as weight. The finiteness statement below makes these meanings unambiguous. A nonpositive counting threshold means the whole packet.

<a id="stmt-countable-initial"></a>
<div id="countable:initial" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21** (Initial profiles and radial blocks). *The data in <a href="#countable:profile-invariant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="countable:profile-invariant">[countable:profile-invariant]</a>–<a href="#countable:scalar-invariant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="countable:scalar-invariant">[countable:scalar-invariant]</a> exist at stage zero with $\Pi_i=p_i^*$. The radial forced signs can be chosen so that every regular radial split preserving them has locally finite compact wall and closed-side components before cylinder attachment. On every compact radial block and buffer, only finitely many center labels fail to be identically the negative pole with full negative core, and only finitely many initial warps fail to be one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-countable-initial"><summary>Proof of stmt-countable-initial</summary>

*Proof.* For a positive number $p$, the decreasing crossing $$2|y'|=.5y^2+p$$ from $+\infty$ to $-\infty$ has length $$T(p)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{2\,\mathrm dy}{.5y^2+p}
      =2\pi\sqrt{2/p}.$$ Extend it by its poles. For center $i$, begin this crossing at stretched distance $2$ from $A_i$ and compose it with $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_h(A_i,\cdot)$. The distance is $1$-Lipschitz, so the resulting profile obeys the inequality with $.5$ in place of $a_0=.60$. Take cores $$U_i^+=\{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_h(A_i,\cdot)<1\},\qquad
 U_i^-=\{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_h(A_i,\cdot)>T(p_i^*)+3\}.$$ Their closures lie in the respective pole interiors. The stipulated width puts $B_i$ strictly inside the negative core. In particular, on the complement of the original closed ball $\overline B_g(x_i,2D)$, the profile is the negative pole and the negative core is full.

There exists a smooth proper function $\rho:M\to\mathbb R$, bounded below, with $|\rho-\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_h(o,\cdot)|<1$ and $|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}\rho|_h\le2$. Here no uniform geometric assumption is necessary. To construct it, use relatively compact coordinate balls in a locally finite cover, convolve the distance on slightly larger balls, and blend with a smooth partition of unity. The metric distortion and local convolution errors can be made arbitrarily small on each ball. In differentiating the blend, write each local approximation as the distance plus its error; the terms involving derivatives of the partition sum to zero on the common distance. Choose the value errors so that the remaining partition-derivative terms have sum less than a fixed small number at each point. A locally finite refinement and successively smaller positive errors achieve this, while keeping the total value error below one. Properness follows from the bounded value error and properness of the complete metric $h$.

Choose alternating positive and negative plateaus in the $\rho$-axis, with transition intervals wider than $2T(p_0^*)$. Rescale the preceding crossing to these intervals, reversing its sign on alternate intervals. The gradient bound for $\rho$ gives the profile inequality with coefficient $.5$ and potential $p_0^*$. Choose the core closures strictly inside the plateaus. Regular values in the remaining margins give compact smooth barrier annuli, and a compact central barrier, whose closures stay in the same pole plateaus. Their value bands tend to infinity and are locally finite.

A wall respecting the signs cannot cross a separating plateau band; each component is closed in one compact radius block and hence compact. A component of either closed side is trapped between two separator bands of the opposite sign, or in the initial compact block. Regularity of the defining function gives manifold-with-boundary charts for these sides. A compact block therefore contains only finitely many components. The same charts prove local finiteness of the component families. When $M$ is compact, use a radial label with constant positive pole and full positive core; its split has no wall and its only side is compact.

The scalar requirement initially holds everywhere because $$\lambda_0+b_0\sum_i p_i^*=.25+1.12/20=.306<.4.$$ Finally a compact block with a compact buffer meets only finitely many original closed outer balls and finitely many initial warp supports. Every other center label and warp has the asserted constant data there. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Finite exceptional data on entire packets

A *downward diagram* of a packet consists of it and all its further faces. An *upward diagram* consists of it and all its cofaces. Each is finite at a fixed stage, by [packet:split](#packet:split). A finite union of either type is finite. The constructions below make finitely many directed passes through such diagrams. They never ask for closure under arbitrary alternating face and coface steps.

<a id="countable:collapse-recursion"></a>
Radial confinement gives the geometric reason to expect finite data on a whole packet. At the first split, a compact radial side or wall component is prolonged by cylinders; its entire collapse image stays in that original compact component. At every later split a child $A$ of $E$ has $$\label{countable:collapse-recursion}
 q_A=q_E\circ p_A,\qquad
 p_A=\begin{cases}
 \text{inclusion},&\text{on the wall or bulk},\\
 \text{projection to the wall},&\text{on a fresh cylinder}.
 \end{cases}$$ Hence $q_A(A)\subset q_E(E)$, including in all simultaneous normal directions. A finite incidence diagram therefore has image in a finite union of compact original radial blocks. Only finitely many original outer balls and initial nonunit warp supports meet that image. Shielding changes no collapse map, and regularization chooses new walls inside old parents, so both preserve this confinement.

This argument controls the initial supports. Later smooth data need not factor through $q_A$: they also use data copied from faces. We must therefore retain finite exceptional lists through those directed dependencies, as well as preserve every other entry exactly.

<a id="stmt-countable-finite-data"></a>
<div id="countable:finite-data" class="definition">

<a id="countable:passive"></a>
**Definition 22** (Finite exceptional data). At stage $r\ge1$, on each whole packet $E$ there are finite sets $I_E\subset J_r$ of label indices and $F_E$ of old warp indices such that, for $j\notin I_E$, $$\label{countable:passive}
 \mu_j\equiv-\infty,\qquad U_j^-=E,\qquad U_j^+=\varnothing,
 \qquad \Pi_j\equiv c_rp_j^*.$$ For every old warp index outside $F_E$, its length is identically one. The number $c_r$ is the same on all packets and is prescribed by <a id="countable:passive-multiplier"></a>$$\label{countable:passive-multiplier}
 c_0=1,\qquad c_{r+1}=(1+3\eta_r/2)c_r.$$ For any finite diagram used in a comparison, take the union of its finite exceptional sets, adding the current label explicitly. This one finite union then works simultaneously on all its whole packets. At stage zero the analogous property is required on every compact radial block and buffer, with $c_0=1$.

</div>

The sets $I_E$ need not consist exactly of the nonconstant entries: an entry may be retained in the exceptional list even if it happens to be constant. This convention avoids any need to discard previous exceptions. Only the existence of a finite list matters.

<a id="stmt-countable-locality"></a>
<div id="countable:locality" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23** (Consequences and directed dependencies). *Suppose the packet geometry and [countable:finite-data](#countable:finite-data) hold at a finite stage. Then the following assertions hold.*

1.  *The sum of potentials on a whole packet is a smooth function with bounded derivatives of every fixed order. The undecided counts, required sets, and guards use only a finite list and are closed.*

2.  *On each whole packet and each finite comparison diagram, the old weight, stabilized scalar, stability forms, and weighted Laplacian are computed with one finite list of warps, after padding with unit factors. The bounded geometry and polynomial weighted growth conclusions of [packet:geometry](#packet:geometry) apply, including on any finite Euclidean product.*

3.  *Compatible choices with positive packetwise tolerances can be made in the following order: fix individual requests, propagate numerical requests down the finite coface chains, and then construct data upward from deeper faces. Enlarging a coface’s exceptional list by the union over its downward source diagram suffices for the last pass. No data are propagated back from a newly constructed coface to an already constructed face.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-countable-locality"><summary>Proof of stmt-countable-locality</summary>

<a id="countable:potential-sum"></a>
*Proof.* On $E$, the sum of potentials is exactly $$\label{countable:potential-sum}
 \sum_{j\in J_r}\Pi_j
 =\sum_{j\in I_E}\Pi_j+c_r\sum_{j\in J_r\setminus I_E}p_j^*.$$ The second term is a finite spatial constant. Indeed $$1\le c_r\le\exp\Bigl(\frac32\sum_{s<r}\eta_s\Bigr)
 \le e^{3/400}.$$ The first term is a finite sum; its derivative bounds follow from compatible smoothness and the compact-part/product-end decomposition in [packet:geometry](#packet:geometry). On a finite comparison diagram choose common product thresholds for its finite exceptional list. Every other entry is already a spatial constant with the same value on each copy. The full sum is therefore an exact face pullback beyond those thresholds; no supremum of infinitely many thresholds is taken. Passive entries have empty undecided set. Thus a set requiring at least $a\ge1$ undecided labels is the finite union, over $a$-element subsets of $I_E$, of the intersections of their closed $G_j$’s. This proves closedness, including for the guards with threshold $m-2$. Threshold zero gives $E$.

For (ii), take the union of the finitely many relevant old warp indices on the finite diagram and set every missing entry to one. Writing $u_\alpha=\log f_\alpha$ and $U=\sum_\alpha u_\alpha$, the scalar expression is $$R_E=\mathop{\mathrm{Scal}}_{h_E}-2\Delta_{h_E}U-|\mathrm dU|_{h_E}^2
                        -\sum_\alpha|\mathrm du_\alpha|_{h_E}^2.$$ A padded logarithm is zero; the product weight is unchanged. The weighted Laplacian is $\Delta_{h_E}+\langle\mathrm dU,\mathrm d\,\cdot\,\rangle_{h_E}$ and is unchanged as well. Weighted perimeter and its first and second variations depend on the same weight. Equivalently, a flat unit factor adds zero second fundamental form and zero curvature to the invariant variation calculation. Thus the wall and seam forms also agree. The finite-diagram geometric lemma now gives completeness, bounded geometry, positive lower and finite upper bounds for the weight, and center-uniform polynomial volume growth. It gives the same statements after taking a finite Euclidean product. The regularity problem for a minimizing phase remains on the base, with singular dimension at most $m-8$ by [cut:gmt](#cut:gmt); padding does not alter that dimension.

For (iii), a particular face receives requests from only its finitely many cofaces and coface chains. Allowing for the finitely many fixed product and cutoff bounds, the minimum of the requested positive errors is positive. Settle all these numbers before constructing the approximations. A request only decreases an error tolerance: it does not copy any coface value into the face. During the subsequent upward pass, the inputs on a packet are its own data and already constructed copies from its finite downward diagram. Their exceptional sets have a finite union. Outside that union every copy in <a href="#countable:passive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="countable:passive">[countable:passive]</a> agrees with every other copy. A newly constructed coface is not used to reconstruct its sources. The same argument applies to finitely many successive directed passes, and proves the stated dependency rule. With these numerical tolerances fixed, at each upward pass the output on $E$ depends only on its original data and the outputs on its strict faces. Its source set for copied functions is therefore contained in the finite downward diagram of $E$. The preceding downward pass changes only positive numbers, not the input functions. In particular two packets sharing a face do not copy one another’s newly constructed data through that face. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### One countable invariant step

<a id="stmt-countable-step"></a>
<div id="countable:step" class="proposition">

**Proposition 24** (Preservation of the invariant). *Suppose that all nonempty parents at stage $r$ have base dimension at least two and satisfy <a href="#countable:profile-invariant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="countable:profile-invariant">[countable:profile-invariant]</a>–<a href="#countable:scalar-invariant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="countable:scalar-invariant">[countable:scalar-invariant]</a>. At $r=0$ use the compact radial blocks of [countable:initial](#countable:initial); at $r\ge1$ suppose also that the packet geometry and [countable:finite-data](#countable:finite-data) hold. Processing label $r$ produces the compatible smooth split, preserves the forced signs, and gives the same assertions at stage $r+1$. Exactly $399$ new warp factors, with common indices, are appended at this stage. Each whole resulting packet has a finite relevant old warp list.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-countable-step"><summary>Proof of stmt-countable-step</summary>

*Proof.* We verify the choices in their order of dependence.

<a id="countable:shift"></a>
*The current pressure and minimizing phases.* The pressure construction of [cut:pressure](#cut:pressure) uses a positive floor only for the label being processed. Replace its finite-family floor by $p_r^*$. Compatible clipping heights can first be chosen above the local barrier bounds, with a uniform extra barrier margin of one. Then use local smoothing errors to obtain $$2|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}p_0|\le .65p_0^2+1.01\Pi_r.$$ All smoothing requests involve only this one profile and finite incidence diagrams. The elementary inequality $$.67(y+c)^2-.65y^2\ge-21.775c^2$$ shows that one common nonempty shift interval, for example $$\label{countable:shift}
 |c|<\min\{.01\sqrt{p_r^*},1/2\},$$ preserves both $2|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}(p_0+c)|\le .67(p_0+c)^2+1.02\Pi_r$ and the barrier inequalities. Floors for other labels do not enter this interval.

After the radial stage, [countable:locality](#countable:locality) supplies all bounded geometry and weighted polynomial-growth hypotheses of [cut:generic-uniqueness](#cut:generic-uniqueness). There are countably many packets, Euclidean product dimensions, and tests in that lemma’s exhaustion. Remove the countable exceptional shift sets and choose a single $c$ in <a href="#countable:shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="countable:shift">[countable:shift]</a>. The resulting true minimizers have the common product limits of [cut:end-convergence](#cut:end-convergence); choose them before any shielding update. At the initial radial step no uniqueness or polynomial growth at spatial infinity is used. The smooth global positive weight and locally bounded smooth pressure give local BV existence and strict compactness by [cut:bv-compactness](#cut:bv-compactness). The relatively compact radial barriers force the phases by compact comparisons. All variations and tests lie in buffered compact windows with finite warp lists, so the formulas in [cut:raw-domination](#cut:raw-domination) apply. The GMT and shielding dimensions are the base dimensions at this first step as well.

*Anchors, guards, and shielding.* On each post-radial packet retain its finite exceptional list, enlarged by its finite downward source diagram and by the current label. Labels outside it are already forced everywhere and require no shell addition. Choose compatible smooth clipped anchors as in [local:anchors](#local:anchors), retaining the exact value $-1$ for a passive profile. For each packet of dimension $m\ge8$ and each set of $m-7$ remaining labels, the image of its singular set under the corresponding smooth anchor map has $(m-7)$-dimensional Lebesgue measure zero. There are countably many such conditions, even if all finite subsets of the countable label set are included. In the countable product of uniform probability intervals $(-.3,.3)$, each bad cylinder has measure zero by its finite-dimensional marginal. A threshold vector outside their union therefore exists. At most $m-8$ anchors equal their thresholds at any singular point. The sign exclusions at poles follow from the $.1$ anchor approximation, exactly as in [local:anchors](#local:anchors).

Process the finitely many face depths from deepest upward, keeping the true cuts fixed. Suppose all strict faces of $E$ have been shielded. Its remaining risky set $$K_E=\Sigma_E\cap
 \{x:\#\{j\in J_r\setminus\{r\}:x\in G'_j\}\ge m-2\}$$ is closed. It is compact: an escaping sequence would, by [packet:escape](#packet:escape), have a product limit over a further face with compact convergent base coordinates. The finite exceptional list and closed core complements pass the undecided count to that limit. The face has a smaller dimension, so the limit lies in its guard. The product convergence of true cuts forces the limiting point to be singular, since regular-point graphical convergence excludes nearby singular points. This contradicts the completed shielding of that face. This argument also treats escape in several normal coordinates.

Apply [local:flat-pieces](#local:flat-pieces), [local:shell](#local:shell), [local:sharp](#local:sharp) on this compact risky set, only for the finite list which can still be undecided. Enumerate the signed compact flat pieces. Choose each shell scale by [shield:maximum](#shield:maximum), hold it fixed, and then send its coface fronts far enough out by [shield:extension](#shield:extension). In the cap construction of [shield:caps](#shield:caps), the crossing for label $j$ satisfies $$2|w'|\le .58w^2+p_j^*.$$ Its length is finite; the inequality is paid by $\Pi_j\ge p_j^*$. The minimum-of-branches formula in that lemma adds no simultaneous normal derivative cost. Its cutoff finishes before the cap crossing starts, so whenever the shell branch is active the whole shell potential is available. Opposite signs remain in disjoint strict anchor regions, and cores only enlarge.

Only finitely many additions are required on $E$. Indeed, every point of the initial compact $K_E$ eventually belongs to an open forced core for each of its strict anchors among the finite exceptional list. At most $m-8<m-2$ labels can then remain undecided there. The residual risky subsets of the initial $K_E$ are nested and closed, and their intersection is empty. Compactness gives a finite index at which the residual is empty. Stop then. Each specified coface receives additions from finitely many source faces through finitely many chains. Thus only finitely many additions and finite minima or maxima affect any whole packet. In particular no infinite limiting profile is taken on that packet.

<a id="countable:shield-form"></a>
To control all the countably many packets at once, enumerate each possible local addition and assign positive allowances with total less than $.12$. Split each allowance between its shell-scale choice and its simultaneous finite coface extension choices. Starting the bookkeeping form bound at $.78$, the maximum estimate and the extension estimate give one $\theta_*<.9$ such that $$\label{countable:shield-form}
 \int1.16\sum_{j\ne r}D_jv^2
 \le\theta_*\bigl(\mathcal N(v)+e_r\|v\|_2^2\bigr).$$ Here $\mathcal N$ has coefficient $1.7$ on a wall and coefficient $.8$ on bulk plus $1.7$ on its attached cylinder. These are the same coefficients on every packet and product. Only a finite list of additions occurs on each post-radial packet, so the bound persists after a whole depth has been treated.

Initially, instead work in separated compact transition shells with compact buffers between radial barriers. Each has finitely many exceptional labels and warps. Its risky set is compact, and the same finite termination applies; there is no coface extension yet. Choose supports within the shell buffers. Their local finiteness makes all updates locally finite on $M$, and a compact test meets only finitely many of them. The summable allowances prove <a href="#countable:shield-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="countable:shield-form">[countable:shield-form]</a> for every such test. Every compact radial side block meets only finitely many shell buffers. This supplies the whole-packet finite lists after the first attachment.

*Counts and the quantitative transfer.* Core enlargement gives $G'_j\subset G_j$. Only the finite exceptional list can contribute to the counts on a post-radial packet; the analogous statement holds on buffered compact blocks at the radial step. Thus [packet:counts](#packet:counts) places every new required set in both the old required set and the shielded guard under projection, including along attached cylinders. The finite lists and closed core complements also pass these count inclusions to all further-face product limits.

The guarded tube and comparison constructions of [regular:guards](#regular:guards), [regular:partition](#regular:partition) use these closed guards, regular graphical convergence on product limits, and finite packetwise derivative bounds. All are now available. Numerical errors are allocated down coface chains before the defining functions are constructed upward.

Apply the quantitative transfer of [regular:transfer](#regular:transfer) with the current $t_r$, $\Delta_r=t_{r+1}-t_r>0$, and $e_0=e_r=\Delta_r/800$. Its stage margins <a href="#regular:eta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="regular:eta">[regular:eta]</a> were proved for these general adjacent parameters, so they do not require equally spaced stages. The potential calculation also applies: on each finite comparison diagram, <a href="#countable:potential-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="countable:potential-sum">[countable:potential-sum]</a> expresses the old potential sum as finitely many smooth functions plus one spatial constant. Only finitely many shielding additions occur there. Thus, with $\widetilde\Pi_j=\Pi_j+D_j$, the same raw form and comparison estimates control <a id="countable:raw-target"></a>$$\label{countable:raw-target}
 \lambda_{r+1}+(1+3\eta_r)b_{r+1}
                         \sum_{j\ne r}\widetilde\Pi_j$$ near the new required set, with the positive mass and gradient reserves proved there. The constant tail has no derivative error; potential approximation errors concern only the finite exceptional list. At the radial step these estimates are made on buffered compact blocks, as in that proof. The seam and potential smoothing can consequently retain an $e_r$ local mass gap. This positive number is fixed for the current stage; all geometric tolerances may depend on the packet. No infimum over future stages or over all packets is taken.

<a id="countable:smooth-potentials"></a>
*Exact passive constants in regularization.* Restrictions, bulk inclusions, and cylinder projections preserve identically constant functions. Seam smoothing of an old identically unit warp leaves it one. For the remaining potentials, smooth $(1+3\eta_r/2)\widetilde\Pi_j$ as in [regular:profiles](#regular:profiles), [regular:constant-preservation](#regular:constant-preservation), so that $$\label{countable:smooth-potentials}
 (1+\eta_r)\widetilde\Pi_j\le\Pi_j^{\rm new}
                     \le(1+2\eta_r)\widetilde\Pi_j.$$ On a finite exceptional list, allocate the required relative errors separately, using $p_j^*>0$ for its $j$th entry. If an aggregate error is prescribed, subdivide it among that finite list. If an entry is passive on the whole source diagram, set its output *exactly* to $c_{r+1}p_j^*$. This is compatible with the smoothing procedure: local convolution of a constant is that constant, partition weights sum to one, and every copied end value is the identical constant. Equivalently this entry can simply be omitted from all smoothing and restored as that constant afterward. We only impose this rule on whole constant inputs and their agreeing copies, so it makes no claim about preserving the edge of an arbitrary nonsmooth plateau.

Before blending on a coface, enlarge its exceptional list by the union over its entire finite downward source diagram. Thus a face-active entry is recorded even if the coface’s individual input was passive. The remaining copies agree identically. Coface requests merely adjust numeric tolerances; they do not introduce new source values on faces. This proves exact passive reproduction on the completed diagram and precludes a cascade through infinitely many same-depth neighbors. At the radial step choose a compact buffer for each compact radial block, enlarged to contain the finitely many chart closures and shell supports used by its approximations. Only finitely many original outer balls and initial nonunit warp supports meet this buffer. Enlarge the exceptional list by precisely these indices. Every other entry is the same constant on every averaging neighborhood and is therefore reproduced exactly before cylinder attachment.

The local-slope argument of [regular:profiles](#regular:profiles) justifies restriction of profiles to the wall: an almost-everywhere gradient bound by a continuous function gives the corresponding local Lipschitz slope bound at every point of each finite band. Restriction and cylinder projection then preserve it. The small metric change increases slopes by at most $1+\eta_r$, and the first inequality in <a href="#regular:eta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="regular:eta">[regular:eta]</a> together with <a href="#countable:smooth-potentials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="countable:smooth-potentials">[countable:smooth-potentials]</a> gives the next profile inequality. Continuous pullback and core enlargement preserve all pole-neighborhood and forced-preimage conditions.

*Corrections, supersolutions, and the new warp group.* The sum in <a href="#countable:potential-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="countable:potential-sum">[countable:potential-sum]</a> is now smooth, bounded, and exactly compatible, as are its finite-order derivatives. On every required finite diagram, pad the old warps by units. In a product end over $B$ the operator of [regular:correction](#regular:correction) therefore satisfies exactly $$L_A=L_B-C\Delta_{\rm normal},\qquad
 L_A=-C\Delta_{\rm old}+R_{\rm old}-V_A^*+K_A,
 \qquad
 V_A^*=\lambda_{r+1}+b_{r+1}\sum_{j\ne r}\Pi_j^{\rm new}.$$ In particular the potential formula does not depend on the base dimension or on how many unit factors were padded. The correction is zero near the new required set and produces a positive form gap on each whole packet. The proof of [regular:correction](#regular:correction) uses finite minima of the gaps on the packet’s face diagram. Its bounded resolvent and the compatible positive supersolutions in [regular:resolvent](#regular:resolvent), [regular:solutions](#regular:solutions) apply with the packetwise bounded geometry and polynomial weighted volume already proved. Their tolerances are chosen after the operators and their individual exact solutions are fixed, and before the final upward blending pass. Thus their finite-dependency hypothesis is precisely (iii) of [countable:locality](#countable:locality).

Let $v_A>0$ be the resulting supersolution with $L_Av_A\ge1/2$. Append the common group of $k=399$ circle lengths $f=v_A^{2/(k+1)}=v_A^{1/200}$. The scalar identity in [regular:step](#regular:step) gives $$R_{\rm new}=R_{\rm old}-C\frac{\Delta_{\rm old}v_A}{v_A}
           =V_A^*-K_A+\frac{L_Av_A}{v_A}
           \ge V_A^*\quad\hbox{on }D_A.$$ Only the number of *new* factors enters the coefficient $C$. The old finite list may vary from packet to packet; unit padding has no effect. Each stage adds just this one finite group, with the same indices in every status. All its lengths are positive and bounded above and away from zero on a whole packet, and exactly compatible. The initial lists and their finite propagated exceptions remain finite, so the complete old list at stage $r+1$ is still finite on each whole packet and each finite comparison diagram.

Finally the regular split preserves compact truncations and finite incidence by [packet:split](#packet:split). Initially the radial separators make all components compact before prolongation. Compatible smooth metrics and finite relevant warps then give the complete packet geometry by [packet:geometry](#packet:geometry). The recursion <a href="#countable:collapse-recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="countable:collapse-recursion">[countable:collapse-recursion]</a> preserves confinement in original radial blocks. All exceptional-list enlargements just described are finite, and all passive constants have multiplier $c_{r+1}$. This completes the induction step, including the first radial step. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Termination in dimension and finite-stage walls

<a id="stmt-countable-dimension-one"></a>
<div id="countable:dimension-one" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (The one-dimensional obstruction). *No nonempty one-dimensional packet can satisfy the invariant at a finite stage of the construction.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-countable-dimension-one"><summary>Proof of stmt-countable-dimension-one</summary>

*Proof.* Such a packet is complete by [packet:geometry](#packet:geometry) and has only finitely many relevant positive smooth warps $f_1,\ldots,f_q$ on its whole base. Its arclength universal cover is $\mathbb R$, including when the packet itself is a circle. On this line put $u_\alpha=\log f_\alpha$ and $P=\sum_{\alpha=1}^q u_\alpha$. The warped-product scalar formula reduces to $$R=-2P''-(P')^2-\sum_{\alpha=1}^q(u_\alpha')^2.$$ The required count in dimension one is zero, so the invariant holds everywhere and gives $R\ge\lambda_r>0$. Consequently $$-2P''\ge\lambda_r+(P')^2,\qquad
 \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm ds}\arctan\frac{P'}{\sqrt{\lambda_r}}
 \le-\frac{\sqrt{\lambda_r}}2.$$ Integration over a line interval longer than $2\pi/\sqrt{\lambda_r}$ contradicts the range of the arctangent. If $q=0$, the scalar formula is simply $R=0$. The argument takes place at one finite stage and uses an ordinary finite sum. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-countable-construction"></a>
<div id="countable:construction" class="proposition">

**Proposition 26** (The countable compatible construction). *All labels can be processed successively. At every finite stage the packet diagram has the invariant above and no nonempty packet of dimension at most one. After the radial stage it has finite exceptional data on whole packets; at stage zero this assertion holds on buffered compact radial blocks. Its finite reconstruction $X_r$ has a proper collapse $q_r:X_r\to M$ and closed signed sides $P_i^{(r)},N_i^{(r)}$ for each processed center label, with $$P_i^{(r)}\cup N_i^{(r)}=X_r,\qquad
 P_i^{(r)}\cap N_i^{(r)}=W_i^{(r)},$$ where $W_i^{(r)}$ is its wall. The forced preimages satisfy $$q_r^{-1}(A_i)\subset P_i^{(r)}\setminus W_i^{(r)},\qquad
 q_r^{-1}(B_i)\subset N_i^{(r)}\setminus W_i^{(r)}.$$ These inclusions hold in open phases and persist at every subsequent finite stage. No $n-1$ distinct walls, even including the radial wall, have a common point. Each center wall lies over its original open outer ball, and its components are compact and locally finite.*

*Old horizons can be enlarged by identifications preserving collapse, all walls, and all signed statuses. For any prescribed sequence $\varepsilon_r>0$ with $\sum_r\varepsilon_r<1$, the next finite collar compression can be chosen with collapse displacement less than $\varepsilon_r$, preserving the previously decided wall strata and signed sides. These are finite-stage conclusions; the limiting fixed-copy construction is made in the next section.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-countable-construction"><summary>Proof of stmt-countable-construction</summary>

*Proof.* Initialization is [countable:initial](#countable:initial). If the construction has reached stage $r$, its parents have dimension at least two: a first nonempty output of smaller dimension would have dimension exactly one, since a hit decreases dimension by one and a sign preserves it. [countable:step](#countable:step) produces the next invariant, and [countable:dimension-one](#countable:dimension-one) excludes that first output. Thus the induction continues through every finite stage. There is no passage to an infinite-stage analytic metric, weight, or torus.

Apply [packet:reconstruction](#packet:reconstruction) to each finite initial segment. Its hypotheses are the regular compatible splits and the compact radial components already established. Its status pieces are exactly the corresponding compact packet truncations. If $n-1$ walls met, assign those labels hit status and choose a closed sign status for each other processed label containing the point. The corresponding nonempty truncation would have dimension one, a contradiction. Forced signs persist under the continuous collapse recursion and enlargement of the cores. In particular $$q_r(W_i^{(r)})\subset M\setminus(A_i\cup B_i)
                         \subset B_g(x_i,2D).$$ Properness of $q_r$ and compactness of the original closed outer ball make each wall component compact; regular local wall charts give local finiteness, as in [packet:reconstruction](#packet:reconstruction).

The horizon identifications in [packet:reconstruction](#packet:reconstruction) preserve collapse, walls, and signed statuses. Its collar compressions preserve the old strata and their sides, and [packet:small-displacement](#packet:small-displacement) allows their collapse oscillations to be bounded by the prescribed $\varepsilon_r$. These choices are made separately on the locally finite compact component family; no uniform collar width or horizon at infinity is required. The next section chooses these finite-stage homeomorphisms coherently on one fixed copy of $M$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:reconstruction"></a>
### Reconstruction and the map to a complex

All distances in this section are measured in the original metric $g$; write $d_0$ for that distance. Put $D=WR_0$, as in <a href="#rounds:wide" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rounds:wide">14</a>. The centers $x_i$ form a maximal $D$-separated set, and the color pairs are $$A_i=\overline B_0(x_i,D),\qquad
 B_i=M\setminus B_0(x_i,2D).$$ The $A_i$ cover $M$. Every family of balls with these centers and a fixed finite radius is locally finite: a compact set has a compact neighborhood of that radius by completeness, and a compact metric space contains only finitely many $D$-separated points. The family is countable, since $M$ is second countable. If some $B_i$ is empty, $\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}_{g}M\le 4D$ and a constant map proves the theorem. We therefore assume that all $B_i$ are nonempty.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### A limit on the original manifold

The analytic packets have infinite product ends. Their finite reconstructions in <a href="#packet:reconstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:reconstruction">35</a> are manifolds homeomorphic to $M$; only finite intervals of the product ends are used in these reconstructions. We now choose those homeomorphisms coherently. This is the point at which local finiteness of the color supports is essential.

<a id="stmt-topology-partitions"></a>
<div id="topology:partitions" class="proposition">

<a id="topology:displacement"></a>
<a id="topology:sides"></a>
<a id="topology:forced"></a>
**Proposition 27** (Reconstructed partitions). *The compatible cuts of <a href="#countable:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="countable:construction">26</a> give a continuous proper map $q:M\to M$, and for every color $i$ closed sets $P_i,N_i,W_i$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 &d_0(q(x),x)<1,\label{topology:displacement}\\
 &P_i\cup N_i=M,\qquad P_i\cap N_i=W_i,
   \qquad W_i\subset q^{-1}\bigl(B_0(x_i,2D)\bigr),
   \label{topology:sides}\\
 &q^{-1}(A_i)\subset P_i\setminus W_i,
   \qquad q^{-1}(B_i)\subset N_i\setminus W_i.
   \label{topology:forced}
\end{aligned}$$ The wall family $(W_i)$ is locally finite, and no $n-1$ distinct walls have a common point.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-topology-partitions"><summary>Proof of stmt-topology-partitions</summary>

<a id="topology:finite-error"></a>
*Proof.* Choose positive numbers $\varepsilon_0,\varepsilon_1,\ldots$ with $\sum_{r\ge0}\varepsilon_r<1$. Here $\varepsilon_0$ is assigned to the single radial stage; the other numbers are assigned to successive color stages. In this proof only, index the post-radial reconstruction by $r=0$. For a finite reconstruction write $$q_r:X_r\longrightarrow M,\qquad
 H_r:X_r\longrightarrow M,\qquad
 Q_r=q_r\circ H_r^{-1}.$$ The map $q_r$ is the proper collapse of <a href="#packet:reconstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:reconstruction">35</a>, whereas $H_r$ is a homeomorphism to a fixed copy of the original manifold. We maintain $$\label{topology:finite-error}
 d_0(Q_r(x),x)\le E_r,
 \qquad E_r\le\sum_{j=0}^{r}\varepsilon_j<1.$$ Walls and their two closed sides will also be viewed on this fixed copy using $H_r$.

For the radial stage, the cut has a locally finite family of compact components. Choose pairwise disjoint, locally finite collars of these components on which the collapse varies by less than $\varepsilon_0$ in each normal segment. Compactness of each component and continuity of the collapse permit the collars to be this small, independently from one component to another. Compress the finite inserted cylinders into these collars. Since their supports are disjoint, the displacement bound is $\varepsilon_0$, rather than a sum over components. This constructs $H_0$ and proves <a href="#topology:finite-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topology:finite-error">[topology:finite-error]</a> initially. The finite length of an inserted cylinder may be arbitrarily large; its length does not impose a lower bound on the width of the collar into which it is compressed.

<a id="topology:horizon"></a>
We describe a subsequent color stage $i$. First raise any old finite horizons needed for the new compatible defining functions. Denote the resulting reconstruction by $\widehat X_r$. The horizon reparameterization $$\Phi_r:\widehat X_r\longrightarrow X_r$$ preserves collapse and every old wall and signed side. Indeed, on an old product end the collapse is independent of the normal coordinate, and an increasing change of that coordinate preserves its sign and its zero section. Compatible products of these changes agree at corners. Thus we may set $$\label{topology:horizon}
 \widehat q_r=q_r\circ \Phi_r,\qquad
 \widehat H_r=H_r\circ \Phi_r,
 \qquad
 \widehat q_r\circ\widehat H_r^{-1}=Q_r.$$ In particular, increasing a horizon contributes no displacement on the fixed copy.

<a id="topology:strip"></a>
The new wall lies over $B_0(x_i,2D)$. Choose its collar strip $S_i$ in $\widehat X_r$ so that $$\label{topology:strip}
 S_i\subset\widehat q_r^{-1}\bigl(B_0(x_i,2D+1)\bigr),$$ and so that collapse oscillation along every segment used by the compression is less than the error assigned to this stage. These choices are possible because the relevant wall components in a finite reconstruction are compact and locally finite. More specifically, their union lies in the inverse image of the compact ball $\overline B_0(x_i,2D)$ under the proper finite collapse; hence only finitely many components are involved in this color stage.

The strips can be chosen to preserve every old stratum, including the radial strata. To check this assertion, work near a point of a new wall on an old stratum, and denote the new defining function by $u$. The horizons were chosen beyond the product thresholds, so the terminal collars glued at each old hit wall lie in the product range. In the resulting product chart, $u$ is pulled back from the corresponding face and is independent of the old signed normal coordinates. Its restriction to that face is regular at zero. In the corresponding product chart choose a vector field $V$ in the face directions with $V(u)=1$, and with zero component in every old normal direction. The chart may avoid the old walls not passing through the point. A partition of unity combines these fields: the linear identity $V(u)=1$ and tangency to every old wall both survive. Its short flow supplies a collar preserving the old walls and their sides. On a fresh cylinder, each old stratum is the product of its intersection with the new wall and the cylinder interval. The compression uses the same increasing normal reparameterization on all these products. It consequently preserves every old signed side as well as all their intersections.

<a id="topology:fixed-support"></a>
Let $$c_r:X_{r+1}\longrightarrow\widehat X_r
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad
 h_r:X_{r+1}\longrightarrow\widehat X_r$$ be, respectively, the new cylinder collapse and the homeomorphism that compresses those cylinders into the chosen strips. They agree off the inverse image of $S_i$. Figure [fig:topology-compression] [fig:topology-compression](#fig:topology-compression) shows the two maps schematically in a normal coordinate: $c_r$ collapses the inserted interval, while $h_r$ compresses it into the chosen old collar. Define <a id="topology:recursion"></a>$$\label{topology:recursion}
 q_{r+1}=\widehat q_r\circ c_r,
 \qquad H_{r+1}=\widehat H_r\circ h_r,
 \qquad
 Q_{r+1}=\widehat q_r\circ c_r\circ h_r^{-1}
                         \circ\widehat H_r^{-1}.$$ The oscillation choice gives $d_0(Q_{r+1}(x),Q_r(x))<\varepsilon_{r+1}$ wherever they differ, proving <a href="#topology:finite-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topology:finite-error">[topology:finite-error]</a> at the next stage. Their difference is supported in $\widehat H_r(S_i)$. If $x=\widehat H_r(z)$ belongs to this set, then <a href="#topology:horizon" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topology:horizon">[topology:horizon]</a>, <a href="#topology:strip" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topology:strip">[topology:strip]</a>, and <a href="#topology:finite-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topology:finite-error">[topology:finite-error]</a> imply $$\label{topology:fixed-support}
 d_0(x,x_i)
 \le d_0(x,Q_r(x))+d_0(\widehat q_r(z),x_i)
 <1+(2D+1)=2D+2.$$ This support radius is independent of the stage.

<figure id="packet:collar-figure">

<figcaption>The two maps used in a finite reconstruction, shown schematically in local normal coordinates: $s$ on the reconstructed domain $X_{r + 1}$ and $t$ on the old target $X̂_{r}$. The target strip $|t| < δ$ represents the chosen collar $S_{i}$. The collapse is constant on the inserted cylinder; the homeomorphism squeezes it into an arbitrarily narrow old collar. Where the maps differ, both images lie in the target strip. Infinite analytic ends are truncated before this topological operation.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-topology-compression"></a>

```latex
\end{tikzpicture}
```


The balls in <a href="#topology:fixed-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topology:fixed-support">[topology:fixed-support]</a> form a locally finite family. Consequently, on every relatively compact neighborhood in the fixed copy, the maps $Q_r$ are exactly unchanged after finitely many stages. Their locally stationary limit $q$ is continuous and satisfies <a href="#topology:displacement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topology:displacement">[topology:displacement]</a>. It is proper: the inverse image of a compact set is a closed subset of its compact closed $1$-neighborhood in $(M,d_0)$.

At each finite stage the signed sides are the closed reconstructed sides, with their intersection equal to the central wall. The stratum-preserving identifications just constructed preserve every previously installed side and wall exactly. Newly installed color walls are confined to the fixed balls in <a href="#topology:fixed-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topology:fixed-support">[topology:fixed-support]</a>. Thus all the partition data are locally eventually unchanged. Their final closed sets satisfy <a href="#topology:sides" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topology:sides">[topology:sides]</a> and <a href="#topology:forced" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topology:forced">[topology:forced]</a>, since these are the finite-stage forced-sign properties of <a href="#countable:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="countable:construction">26</a>, and both collapse and statuses are stationary near each point. The wall family is locally finite by the same support bound. Finally, a point on $n-1$ distinct final color walls would, on a neighborhood where these data have stabilized, lie on the same $n-1$ finite-stage walls. This contradicts <a href="#countable:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="countable:construction">26</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Thickening the walls without increasing their multiplicity

Put $k=n-2$. We need open neighborhoods of the whole walls, rather than independently chosen neighborhoods of their individual compact pieces.

<a id="stmt-topology-thicken"></a>
<div id="topology:thicken" class="lemma">

<a id="topology:thickening"></a>
**Lemma 28** (Simultaneous wall neighborhoods). *There are locally finite open sets $T_i\supset W_i$ such that $$\label{topology:thickening}
 T_i\subset q^{-1}\bigl(B_0(x_i,2D)\bigr)
                  \setminus q^{-1}(A_i\cup B_i),
 \qquad
 \#\{i:x\in T_i\}\le k\quad(x\in M).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-topology-thicken"><summary>Proof of stmt-topology-thicken</summary>

<a id="topology:cover-property"></a>
*Proof.* Set $O_i=q^{-1}(B_0(x_i,2D))$ and $F_i=q^{-1}(A_i\cup B_i)$. By <a href="#topology:partitions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="topology:partitions">27</a>, $W_i$ is disjoint from the closed set $F_i$ and lies in the open set $O_i$. At a point $x$, local finiteness and closedness of the walls give an open neighborhood meeting only walls that contain $x$. Shrink it to a neighborhood $U_x$ contained in $O_i\setminus F_i$ for every such wall. Therefore $U_x$ meets at most $k$ walls, and $$\label{topology:cover-property}
 U_x\cap W_i\ne\varnothing\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 U_x\subset O_i\setminus F_i.$$

Choose a locally finite open star refinement $\mathcal V$ of this cover: the union of the members of $\mathcal V$ meeting any fixed member is contained in some $U_x$. For completeness, such a refinement can be obtained directly with the metric $d_0$. The function $$\rho(x)=\sup_U\min\{1,d_0(x,M\setminus U)\},
 \qquad U\in\{U_x:x\in M\},$$ is positive and $1$-Lipschitz, with distance to the empty set truncated to $1$. The balls $V_x=B_0(x,\rho(x)/8)$ star-refine the original cover. Indeed, if $V_y$ meets $V_x$, the Lipschitz inequality gives $d_0(x,y)<2\rho(x)/7$, and hence $V_y\subset B_0(x,25\rho(x)/56)$. The latter ball is contained in $B_0(x,\rho(x)/2)$, which lies in a member of the original cover. Fix $o\in M$. Obtain a locally finite open refinement of $(V_x)$ by covering each compact annulus $\overline B_0(o,m)\setminus B_0(o,m-1)$ with finitely many open subsets of members of $(V_x)$ lying in $\{m-2<d_0(o,\cdot)<m+1\}$, for $m\ge1$. These sets cover $M$ and are locally finite. A refinement of a star refinement is still a star refinement of the original cover, which proves the assertion about $\mathcal V$.

Define $$T_i=\bigcup\{V\in\mathcal V:V\cap W_i\ne\varnothing\}.$$ It contains $W_i$, and <a href="#topology:cover-property" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topology:cover-property">[topology:cover-property]</a> implies the containment in <a href="#topology:thickening" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topology:thickening">[topology:thickening]</a>. If $z$ belongs to several $T_i$, choose $V_0\in\mathcal V$ containing $z$. For each such $i$ there is a $V_i\in\mathcal V$ containing $z$ and meeting $W_i$. All the $V_i$ meet $V_0$, so their union lies in a single $U_x$. That $U_x$ meets each of these walls, and therefore there are at most $k$ of them. Finally, $(T_i)$ is locally finite because $T_i\subset O_i$ and the family $(O_i)$ is locally finite. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Coordinates, triangulation, and entire fibers

<a id="topology:coordinates"></a>
For each $i$ the closed sets $$C_i^+=P_i\setminus T_i,\qquad C_i^-=N_i\setminus T_i$$ are disjoint and cover $M\setminus T_i$. For a closed set $C$ write $\delta_C(x)=\min\{1,d_0(x,C)\}$, with $\delta_\varnothing=1$, and put $$\label{topology:coordinates}
 t_i(x)=\frac{\delta_{C_i^-}(x)}
                    {\delta_{C_i^-}(x)+\delta_{C_i^+}(x)}.$$ The denominator is positive because the two closed sets are disjoint. These continuous coordinates have the following properties:

1.  $t_i=1$ on $q^{-1}(A_i)$ and $t_i=0$ on $q^{-1}(B_i)$;

2.  $0<t_i(x)<1$ implies $x\in T_i$;

3.  $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}t_i\subset q^{-1}(\overline B_0(x_i,2D))$, so the coordinate supports are locally finite;

4.  at every point at least one coordinate equals $1$, and at most $k$ coordinates lie strictly between $0$ and $1$.

Here the last assertion uses the inner-ball cover and <a href="#topology:thicken" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="topology:thicken">28</a>.

<a id="topology:staircase"></a>
Consider the cubical set of finitely supported coordinate vectors described by these properties. Its cubes are $$Q(S,A)=\{t:t_i=1\ (i\in S),\ 0\le t_i\le1\ (i\in A),
                    \ t_i=0\ (i\notin S\cup A)\},$$ where $S,A$ are disjoint finite sets of labels, $S\ne\varnothing$, and $|A|\le k$. Triangulate each cube by the staircase triangulation: for every ordering $a_1,\ldots,a_j$ of $A$, take the simplex with vertices $$\label{topology:staircase}
 \mathbf1_S,\quad \mathbf1_{S\cup\{a_1\}},\quad\ldots,\quad
 \mathbf1_{S\cup A}.$$ The triangulations agree on faces: setting a free coordinate to zero removes it, while setting it to one moves it to $S$. They therefore define a simplicial complex $K$ of dimension at most $k$.

We use the usual polyhedral topology on $|K|$. To verify continuity without placing a product topology on the infinite coordinate space, fix a point of $M$ and a neighborhood meeting only finitely many coordinate supports. On that neighborhood all other coordinates are identically zero. The vector $(t_i)$ thus takes values in the finite cubical complex using this finite set of labels, and is a continuous map to that complex. Its staircase triangulation is a finite geometric simplicial complex, whose inclusion in $|K|$ is continuous. The coordinate map consequently defines a continuous map <a id="topology:final-map"></a>$$\label{topology:final-map}
 F:M\longrightarrow |K|,\qquad \dim K\le n-2.$$ The triangulation retains the entire coordinate vector: the affine map from each simplex in <a href="#topology:staircase" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topology:staircase">[topology:staircase]</a> to its coordinate simplex agrees on faces and is injective on their union. One can see injectivity by restricting any two finite supports to their finite union; there it is the usual triangulation of a cubical complex. Thus $F(x)=F(y)$ implies $t_i(x)=t_i(y)$ for every $i$.

Choose, for any two points of the same fiber, a coordinate that equals $1$ at the first point. It also equals $1$ at the second. By (i), both collapse images lie in the same open outer ball, and hence $$d_0(x,y)
 \le d_0(x,q(x))+d_0(q(x),x_i)
       +d_0(x_i,q(y))+d_0(q(y),y)
 <4D+2.$$ This applies to every pair of points in the fiber, regardless of its number of components. Consequently <a id="topology:fiber-bound"></a>$$\label{topology:fiber-bound}
 \sup_{z\in|K|}\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}_{g}F^{-1}(z)\le4D+2=4WR_0+2.$$ Together with [rounds:wide,countable:construction] [rounds:wide](#rounds:wide), [countable:construction](#countable:construction), this proves <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="sec:closed-covers"></a>
### Universal covers and the uniform simplex metric

We use the continuous macroscopic dimension of a metric space $X$: $\dim_{\mathrm{mc}}X\le d$ means that a continuous map from $X$ to a simplicial complex of dimension at most $d$, with its uniform simplex metric, has uniformly bounded entire fibers. Here each simplex is a standard Euclidean simplex with the same edge length. This is the continuous convention in Dranishnikov (2013, Definitions 1.1–1.2) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[dranishnikov2014](#ref-dranishnikov2014)]. We first convert the map already constructed to this target metric. We then supply the two lower dimensions using the classical geodesic index argument and the width theorem of Liokumovich and Wang.

<a id="stmt-cover-uniform"></a>
<div id="cover:uniform" class="lemma">

**Lemma 29** (Changing the simplex metric without changing fibers). *Let $k\ge0$ be an integer. Suppose continuous functions $t_i:Y\to[0,1]$ have locally finite supports, at least one equals $1$ at each point, and at most $k$ are strictly between $0$ and $1$. Their coordinate map admits an injective conversion to a simplicial complex of dimension at most $k$ with its uniform simplex metric. The converted map is continuous, and two of its values agree if and only if all the original coordinates agree.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cover-uniform"><summary>Proof of stmt-cover-uniform</summary>

*Proof.* Use the cubes $Q(S,A)$ and staircase simplices of <a href="#topology:staircase" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topology:staircase">[topology:staircase]</a>, where $S,A$ are disjoint finite sets, $S\ne\varnothing$, and $|A|\le k$. Realize the abstract simplices as standard simplices, and send each coordinate simplex to its new realization with the same barycentric coordinates. The rules agree on common faces. Equality of two images identifies their minimal simplex and its positive barycentric coordinates; the affine combination of the corresponding finite indicator vectors recovers the same original coordinate vector. Thus the conversion is injective.

For $k\ge1$, give each simplex edge length $1$ and define $d_K$ as the infimum of the total Euclidean lengths of finite piecewise straight paths in the simplices. This infimum is finite: vertices $\mathbf1_S$ and $\mathbf1_T$ are joined by adding the coordinates in $T\setminus S$ and then removing those in $S\setminus T$, always retaining a nonempty set of coordinates equal to $1$. Every point is joined to a vertex in its simplex. For a vertex $v$, its barycentric coordinate $b_v$, set to zero on simplices not containing $v$, agrees on faces and is $\sqrt2$-Lipschitz on each unit-edge simplex. Indeed such a simplex is realized by the vectors $e_v/\sqrt2$ in a Euclidean coordinate space. Hence $$|b_v(x)-b_v(y)|\le\sqrt2\,d_K(x,y).$$ The barycentric coordinates distinguish points, so $d_K$ is a genuine finite metric. For $k=0$ use the discrete vertex metric.

Near a point of $Y$, only a finite set $F$ of coordinates can be nonzero. The coordinate map there lands continuously in the finite cubical subcomplex with all other coordinates zero. The conversion is continuous on each of its closed simplices and hence on the finite complex. Inclusion into the full uniform realization is continuous on a simplex because distance there is at most the length of its straight segment. Finite pasting therefore proves continuity on the entire finite subcomplex, and thus near every point of $Y$. The same local finite argument applies for $k=0$. No equality between two topologies on an arbitrary infinite complex is required. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cover-reeb"></a>
<div id="cover:reeb" class="lemma">

**Lemma 30** (Level components of a proper Morse function). *Let $u:N\to\mathbb R$ be a proper Morse function on a connected smooth manifold without boundary. There is a locally finite simplicial graph $K$ and a continuous map $q:N\to K$, with $K$ carrying its uniform simplex metric, whose point fibers are precisely the connected components of the levels of $u$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cover-reeb"><summary>Proof of stmt-cover-reeb</summary>

*Proof.* Choose an auxiliary smooth Riemannian metric on $N$ to define gradients. The critical set is closed and its points are isolated. Properness therefore makes the number of critical points in any compact slab $u^{-1}([a,b])$ finite. Thus the critical values form a locally finite subset of $\mathbb R$, even if several critical points have the same value. Every level is compact and locally path connected: regular charts give a hypersurface, while the Morse normal form at a critical point gives the zero set of a nondegenerate quadratic form, which is a cone or an isolated point. A compact locally path connected space has finitely many components, since its components are open and form an open cover.

Let $S$ consist of the critical values and the integers. It is locally finite. At each $a\in S$ put a vertex for every component of $u^{-1}(a)$. Between consecutive values $a<b$ of $S$, the flow of $\nabla u/|\nabla u|^2$ identifies $u^{-1}((a,b))$ with a regular level times $(a,b)$. The flow exists for each compact subinterval because its trajectory stays in a compact slab. Each of the finitely many connected product components defines an open edge, parametrized by its $u$-value. The image of a proper map to $\mathbb R$ is closed; any finite endpoint of $u(N)$ is an attained extremum and hence a critical value. Thus no further finite endpoints are missing.

An edge component $E$ has a unique attachment at each end. At its lower end, the nonempty compact connected sets $$\overline{E\cap u^{-1}((a,a+\epsilon])},
 \qquad 0<\epsilon<b-a,$$ are nested as $\epsilon\downarrow0$. Their intersection equals $\overline E\cap u^{-1}(a)$: a point of this latter set is approached by points of $E$ whose heights tend to $a$, and therefore belongs to every displayed closure. The intersection is nonempty, connected and contained in $u^{-1}(a)$, hence in exactly one of that level’s components. Attach the edge to its vertex. The same argument applies at $b$. There are finitely many edges in either adjacent slab, so every vertex has finite valence. The resulting graph is countable and locally finite. Its points correspond exactly to the connected components of all levels, using full level components as vertices when critical values coincide.

Define $q$ by this correspondence. On an open edge it is continuous by the product description. At a vertex corresponding to a compact level component $C\subset u^{-1}(a)$, an adjacent edge not attached to $C$ has no sequence approaching $C$: such a sequence would put a point of $C$ into its endpoint intersection, contrary to its unique attachment. The finitely many other components of $u^{-1}(a)$ are disjoint compact sets. A neighborhood of $C$ can therefore exclude them and the finitely many nonincident edges. Requiring also that $|u-a|$ be sufficiently small places its remaining points in any prescribed short tails of the finitely many incident edges. This proves continuity at the vertex and shows that its whole inverse image is $C$. The graph is connected, since $q$ is a continuous surjection and $N$ is connected.

Subdivide every edge once with a different midpoint vertex for each edge. This removes parallel edges from the abstract realization; loops do not occur because the endpoint heights differ. The result is a simplicial graph. Give each new edge the same length. Finite valence implies that its ordinary graph topology equals the topology of this uniform simplex metric: vertex neighborhoods contain short segments on each of finitely many incident edges. The subdivision and change of edge lengths preserve points and fibers. Thus $q$ has the asserted continuity and exact fibers. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-cover-reeb-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-cover-reeb</summary>

*Proof of <a href="#cover:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cover:main">3</a>.* Put $s=\min_M\mathop{\mathrm{Scal}}_h>0$ and $X=\widetilde M$. The lifted metric is complete. If $X$ is compact, use a constant map to a vertex, whose only nonempty fiber has finite diameter.

For $n\ge4$, the complete metric $s\widetilde h$ has scalar curvature at least $1$. Apply [thm:main](#thm:main) using the coordinates of <a href="#topology:coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topology:coordinates">[topology:coordinates]</a>, then [cover:uniform](#cover:uniform). Local finiteness of their supports was proved in [sec:reconstruction](#sec:reconstruction), and the conversion preserves their entire fibers. Scaling back gives the bound $C_n/\sqrt{s}$ in the original metric.

For $n=2$, the Gaussian curvature of the cover is at least $s/2$. If a unit-speed minimizing geodesic had length $L>\pi\sqrt{2/s}$, take a parallel unit normal $E$ along it and the endpoint-zero variation field $V(t)=\sin(\pi t/L)E(t)$. Its index form obeys $$I(V,V)=\int_0^L\bigl(|\nabla_tV|^2-K|V|^2\bigr)\,dt
 \le \frac{\pi^2}{2L}-\frac{sL}{4}<0,$$ contradicting minimality. Completeness supplies minimizing geodesics, so the cover has diameter at most $\pi\sqrt{2/s}$ and is compact by Hopf–Rinow. The constant map therefore proves this case.

Finally let $n=3$ and $X$ be noncompact. Apply Liokumovich and Wang (2026, Theorem 1.1, arXiv version 1) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[liokumovichWang2023](#ref-liokumovichWang2023)] to the complete metric $(s/6)\widetilde h$, whose scalar curvature is at least $6$. It gives a proper Morse function such that every connected component of every level, including critical levels, has ambient diameter less than $18\pi$ in that metric. Apply [cover:reeb](#cover:reeb). Each whole fiber of its graph map is one such level component, so in the original lifted metric it has diameter less than $18\pi\sqrt{6/s}$. This is a continuous map to a one-dimensional uniform simplicial complex, as required. ◻

</details>

The low-dimensional conclusions use their stated classical and public inputs. The quantitative theorem in dimensions at least four has the separate proof given above. All map statements here are continuous; changing the simplex metric does not supply a Lipschitz bound.

<a id="sec:packets"></a>
### Packets, finite horizons, and the induction invariant

This appendix gives the geometry and bookkeeping for a finite number of partitions. The minimization, shielding, and regularization arguments in the following appendices construct the regular cuts to which this geometry applies. The passage to countably many labels is proved in <a href="#countable:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="countable:construction">26</a>.

The packet diagrams, finite stage constants, analytic invariant, and radial initialization are stated in [sec:finite-cuts](#sec:finite-cuts). We prove here their geometric properties and the compatibility of finite reconstructions.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Product ends and regular splitting

<a id="stmt-packet-split"></a>
<div id="packet:split" class="lemma">

**Lemma 31** (Compatible splitting and finite truncations). *Suppose a packet diagram after the radial stage has the properties in <a href="#packet:category" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:category">16</a>. On every packet let $g_E$ be a smooth compatible function, with $0$ a regular value, including on every face. Keep $L_E=g_E^{-1}(0)$ as the new hit space.
