To each closed side $\{\pm g_E\ge0\}$ attach $L_E\times[0,\infty)$ along its boundary. Use smooth normal collars across the attachment, chosen as products in all sufficiently far old tails. Connected components of these spaces, with the appended statuses, form a new packet diagram.*

*It has finite face and coface incidence, properly embedded closed tails, and compact remainders. Each packet admits a compact truncation which is a smooth manifold with corners and is a strong deformation retract of the full packet. Truncations can be chosen simultaneously on every finite downward diagram, with their faces equal to the corresponding truncated packets. If thresholds are in the product range of $g_E$, the old retractions preserve its zero set and both closed sides. These conclusions also hold at the first radial stage provided that, before attachment, its wall and closed-side components are compact and form a locally finite family in $X$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-packet-split"><summary>Proof of stmt-packet-split</summary>

*Proof.* First work in the finite downward diagram of one old packet. Raise all entry thresholds until every overlap is in a commuting quadrant, and until all the functions in this diagram are products there. For an elementary normal coordinate $t_i$ choose a larger horizon $h_i$. On its tail use $t_i\mapsto\min(t_i,h_i)$ and the homotopy $t_i\mapsto(1-s)t_i+s\min(t_i,h_i)$; use the identity elsewhere. The formulas agree continuously at $t_i=h_i$. In an overlap the other normal coordinates are unchanged and the formulas commute. One may therefore perform these clamps in any order. Their joint image is compact: remove the far elementary tails, and then inductively truncate each of the finitely many face tails. This gives a finite union of compact products. Its boundary strata have precisely the claimed product descriptions. Each clamp fixes the cutting function, since that function is a face pullback throughout the moving region.

The regular zero set in this compact manifold with corners is transverse to every corner stratum. Indeed on a far old face the derivative in face directions is the derivative of the regular face function. Consequently the compact zero set and the two compact closed sides are manifolds with corners. Each has finitely many components: every point has a connected neighborhood in the relevant manifold with corners, and compactness supplies a finite such cover. Clamping shows that a full zero or side component has a nonempty connected intersection with the truncation. Conversely a connection in the full set clamps to a connection in the truncation. Thus no additional components or connections occur only at infinity.

On an old tail the whole operation is literally the same operation on its face times the old normal intervals. In particular the cylinder attached to a face zero set is the same subset in every order of passing to that face. For the new sign-to-hit relation the tail is exactly the newly attached cylinder. These observations prove all product and commutation rules, including the unique occurrence for a prescribed coarser status: the outgoing product of a connected face packet is connected and belongs to a unique coarser component. A new packet has finitely many faces by the compact truncation just constructed. A face has at most one incident packet for each coarser status, of which there are finitely many. Applying these assertions through the finite status depth proves finiteness in both directions.

For clarity, properness of tails is part of this argument, not an inference from a metric completeness assertion. Fresh closed cylinder tails are proper by the attachment topology. The continuous collapse of a new space to its old parent sends a compact set to a compact set. On an inherited tail, old properness then bounds its old parameters in a compact subset of the old tail; the fresh cylinder length is also bounded on the given compact set. The remaining zero and side conditions are closed. Thus the inverse image of that compact set in the inherited closed tail is compact. Equivalently, it is contained in the union of closed old pieces and bounded fresh cylinders over compact zero pieces. This also shows that the complement of all new far tails is compact. Clamp the fresh cylinder coordinate as well to obtain the asserted new compact truncations and retractions.

At the radial stage there are initially no old tails. The hypotheses give compact wall and side pieces, with finite incidence on each compact piece, and the fresh cylinders start the preceding induction. All diagrams remain countable, because all manifolds involved are second countable and the radial family is locally finite.

For later metric uses, normal collars can be obtained from any compatible smooth parent metric. In an old product tail, both the regular defining function and the metric are pulled back from the face, so the unit normal and its normal geodesics split off the old normal coordinates. On a compact truncation of the zero set choose a positive tubular radius by finitely many normal-coordinate charts. Induction through its finite face diagram extends this radius to a positive uniform radius on that parent packet: any failure would be in a product over a face where the radius was already chosen. Thus these collars are products on all old ends; at the radial step the same choice is made separately on each incident compact block. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Completeness, growth, and product limits

<a id="stmt-packet-geometry"></a>
<div id="packet:geometry" class="lemma">

<a id="packet:growth"></a>
**Lemma 32** (Metric geometry of a packet). *Equip a packet diagram after the radial stage with compatible smooth base metrics and finitely many compatible smooth positive warp functions. Each connected packet $E$, separately, is complete and has bounded geometry: positive injectivity radius and bounds on curvature and every covariant derivative. Every warp and all its covariant derivatives are bounded on $E$, and every warp has a positive lower bound. For $\omega_E$ the product of the warps there are constants $A_E<\infty$ and an integer $d_E\le\dim E$ such that $$\label{packet:growth}
 \int_{B_E(x,R)}\omega_E\,\mathrm d\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_E
       \le A_E(1+R)^{d_E}
       \qquad(x\in E,\ R\ge0).$$ The constant is uniform over centers in this packet. The same assertions hold on $E\times\mathbb R^a$, with the product metric, pulled-back warps, and growth exponent at most $d_E+a$, for every finite $a$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-packet-geometry"><summary>Proof of stmt-packet-geometry</summary>

*Proof.* Induct upward from packets with no faces, which are compact. A full elementary product tail over a complete face has no finite-distance exit through its face coordinate. More precisely, a finite-length curve in that product whose normal coordinate stays above the entry level has Cauchy face and normal coordinates. Completeness of the face and properness of the closed tail keep its limit in the product. It follows that, after fixing a closed-tail entry level $T_i$, the function $$\rho_i=\max(t_i-T_i,0)\quad\hbox{on that tail},\qquad
 \rho_i=0\quad\hbox{outside it}$$ is continuous and globally $1$-Lipschitz. The only exit is through $t_i=T_i$, and its differential has norm at most one wherever defined. A Cauchy sequence has all the finitely many $\rho_i$ bounded. The compact-truncation assertion of <a href="#packet:split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:split">31</a> puts such a sequence in a compact set. Hence the packet is complete.

<a id="packet:sector"></a>
Recursively subdividing each coordinate into a bounded interval and its far range gives a finite cover of $E$ by compact sets and sectors $$\label{packet:sector}
 K_B\times[T_1,\infty)\times\cdots\times[T_d,\infty),$$ where $K_B$ is a compact subset of a further face and the metric and warps are products. The recursion terminates because a face decreases base dimension. On such a sector each displayed normal coordinate is also the corresponding elementary coordinate on $E$, by commutation. In an $R$-ball its range therefore has length at most $2R$, by the Lipschitz functions $\rho_i$ (enlarge by a fixed bounded interval at an entry if necessary). The sector’s weighted volume in that ball is bounded by its compact base volume times a constant multiple of $(1+R)^d$. Summing the finitely many bounds proves <a href="#packet:growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:growth">[packet:growth]</a>, for all centers, including centers outside the sector. Notice that $d\le\dim E$.

Bounds on smooth data follow from the same finite sector decomposition: on compact pieces they are finite, and on a product they are precisely face bounds. For bounded geometry one can equivalently use uniform charts. Any failure of a uniform chart radius or of a fixed-order chart bound has an escaping sequence; a subsequence lies in a far product over a compact part of a further face, as detailed in <a href="#packet:escape" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:escape">33</a>. Fixed-radius neighborhoods there are product neighborhoods with uniform smooth chart bounds and a positive injectivity radius. Compact remaining regions have the same properties by a finite chart cover. This proves bounded geometry and all warp bounds. The product assertions follow either by the same proof or from $B_{E\times\mathbb R^a}((x,z),R)\subset B_E(x,R)\times B_{\mathbb R^a}(z,R)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-packet-escape"></a>
<div id="packet:escape" class="lemma">

**Lemma 33** (Escaping sequences and product windows). *If a sequence escapes all compact sets of a fixed packet $E$, a subsequence has coordinates $(x_\nu,t_{1,\nu},\ldots,t_{d,\nu})$ in one further-face product, where $d\ge1$, $x_\nu$ converges in a compact subset of the face $B$, and every $t_{i,\nu}\to\infty$. After translating these normal coordinates, the product charts contain each fixed metric neighborhood of the sequence for all sufficiently large $\nu$. They exhaust $B\times\mathbb R^d$ locally. All compatible smooth data equal the corresponding product data on these windows.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-packet-escape"><summary>Proof of stmt-packet-escape</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a sector from the finite cover in <a href="#packet:sector" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:sector">[packet:sector]</a> containing a subsequence. Pass to a subsequence so each of its normal coordinates either converges finitely or tends to infinity. The compact base coordinate also converges. Absorb the bounded normal coordinates into the base of the intermediate face specified by the remaining, unbounded coordinates. Commutation identifies this with the corresponding full product occurrence. At least one unbounded coordinate remains, since otherwise the sequence lies in a compact truncation. A path of length at most $R$ changes each normal coordinate by at most $R$. It cannot escape through a face coordinate, by completeness of the face as in <a href="#packet:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:geometry">32</a>. Thus a fixed-radius neighborhood lies in the product once all its normal coordinates exceed the entry levels by more than $R$. Translating yields the stated exhaustion. This argument uses only the completeness part of <a href="#packet:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:geometry">32</a>, which was established before its bounded-geometry conclusion. ◻

</details>

The initial metric and warps need not have bounded geometry on $X$. At the radial split each bulk piece and each wall piece is compact before cylinder attachment. The smooth data have finite derivative bounds there, the positive warps have positive minima, and the cylinders freeze those data. This initializes <a href="#packet:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:geometry">32</a>. For a locally finite initial warp list, a compact radial block and its compact buffer meet only finitely many nonunit factors; the same argument applies after omitting the unit factors. Polynomial growth is a conclusion for a single post-radial packet, never an assumption on the original complete manifold.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Compatible choices

<a id="stmt-packet-approximation"></a>
<div id="packet:approximation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 34** (Compatible choices and finite tolerance allocation). *On a packet diagram after the radial stage, choices of smooth cutoffs and approximations can be made from deeper faces to cofaces, retaining exact products outside finite thresholds. More precisely, fix a finite derivative order and, on each packet, positive error tolerances for any finite collection of smooth data or approximations. A datum on a face may additionally be required to meet the tolerances of all its incident cofaces, including through chains. These requirements can be replaced in advance by strictly positive face tolerances, and then met by compatible blending, provided the individual local approximations and their required convergence to the face data are available. In particular there exist compatible smooth positive functions arbitrarily small in any prescribed finite derivative norm on each packet. Also, if compatible closed sets $F_E$ and compatible open sets $O_E$ satisfy $F_E\subset O_E$ on every packet, then $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_E(F_E,E\setminus O_E)>0$ on each fixed packet whenever both sets are nonempty.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-packet-approximation"><summary>Proof of stmt-packet-approximation</summary>

*Proof.* There are only finitely many requests on a particular face, because there are only finitely many coface chains. Starting with the given requests, propagate stricter tolerances down these chains, allowing for the finitely many product, derivative, and cutoff constants. Take finite minima; no minimum over an infinite adjacency component is taken. This finishes all tolerance choices before construction begins. A countable collection of packets causes no problem, since each one has finitely many faces and cofaces and the depth is finite.

For the construction on one packet, enumerate its elementary tails. Choose smooth cutoffs $f_i(t_i)$ rising from zero to one sufficiently far out, with bounded derivatives through the chosen order, and extend them by zero off their tails. Write $u_0$ for an individual approximation and $u_i$ for the already chosen face copy. The weights <a id="packet:blend"></a>$$\label{packet:blend}
 w_0=\prod_i(1-f_i),\qquad
 w_i=f_i\prod_{a<i}(1-f_a),\qquad
 w_0+\sum_iw_i=1$$ produce $w_0u_0+\sum_iw_iu_i$. Put the transitions beyond all relevant intersection thresholds. On an overlap the participating face copies then agree by their own product rules. In particular, wherever some $f_i=1$, every nonzero term is the same face copy. The result is an exact copy there. Leibniz’ rule bounds the derivative error by a finite multiple of the preassigned errors. A perturbation on the remaining compact region changes none of these end identities. This proves the conditional approximation assertion. It does not assert convergence for a temporary nonproduct object; whenever such an object is used below, its convergence is proved separately.

For positive small functions start with positive constants on the compact deepest packets, take positive small individual choices on compact parts, and use the positive weights above. Decrease the preassigned tolerances to cover all coface derivative requests. This gives strict positivity at every point and any stipulated finite norm bounds, including the $C^2$ bounds used for seam widths. For the separation assertion, a sequence of distances tending to zero either stays in a compact region, where closedness and inclusion in the open set give a contradiction, or escapes. In the latter case <a href="#packet:escape" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:escape">33</a> supplies a common product window over a compact part of a deeper face. The two sequences project to the same limit point, lying both in $F_B$ and in $B\setminus O_B$, a contradiction. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Finite reconstruction

<a id="stmt-packet-reconstruction"></a>
<div id="packet:reconstruction" class="proposition">

**Proposition 35** (Finite-horizon reconstruction). *Starting with $X$, perform finitely many compatible regular splits as in <a href="#packet:split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:split">31</a>. For the first split require locally finite compact radial wall and side components. At the later split for color $i$, require the defining functions to be positive on the inverse image of $A_i$ and negative on the inverse image of $B_i$, with those inverse images contained in open regions of the stipulated signs. After any $r\ge1$ of these splits, the diagram has a reconstruction $X_r$, obtained by finite collar truncations and gluing at hit faces, with the following properties:*

1.  *$X_r$ is a smooth manifold homeomorphic to $X$. Its $r$ labelled walls are closed smooth hypersurfaces with simultaneous transverse intersection charts. Each wall component is compact, the component families are locally finite, and the closed sign/hit pieces are exactly the compact cornered truncations of the analytic packets, by components.*

<a id="packet:displacement"></a>
2.  *The packet maps glue to a proper continuous map $q_r:X_r\to X$. There is a homeomorphism $H_r:X_r\to X$ such that $$\label{packet:displacement}
     d_0\bigl(H_r(z),q_r(z)\bigr)\le r\qquad(z\in X_r).$$*

3.  *Horizons can be enlarged to exceed any already specified compatible product thresholds on the full analytic packets. A horizon is constant for all occurrences of a normal coordinate belonging to one connected global wall component. Such enlargement has an identification with the previous reconstruction which preserves $q_r$, every old wall, and every old side status.*

4.  *Once color $i$ has been processed, $q_r^{-1}(A_i)$ remains in its open positive phase and $q_r^{-1}(B_i)$ remains in its open negative phase at every subsequent stage. In particular its wall misses $q_r^{-1}(A_i\cup B_i)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-packet-reconstruction"><summary>Proof of stmt-packet-reconstruction</summary>

*Proof.* We prove the assertions in their order of dependence. At stage zero use $X_0=X$ and $q_0=H_0=\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}$. At the radial split, the assumed compact pieces give the first finite reconstruction by inserting cylinders of finite length on the two sides of each wall component. The properness and homeomorphism argument below applies to this locally finite compact family. Its compact pieces also initialize the analytic diagram by <a href="#packet:split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:split">31</a>.

Suppose the reconstruction through stage $r$ has been made. Fix all product thresholds for the next cutting functions and their collar metrics in the *full analytic coordinates* first. Consider a connected component $W$ of an existing global wall. A packet with the corresponding hit status has a nonempty connected truncation, by the commuting retractions of <a href="#packet:split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:split">31</a>; it therefore lies in a single such $W$. The same assignment holds at all incidences through further faces, since the corresponding truncations glue along those faces. Compactness of $W$ and local finiteness of the reconstructed pieces imply that only finitely many such pieces meet $W$. Thus there are only finitely many threshold requests for its normal coordinate. Choose one horizon exceeding all of them, with a positive buffer. Do this for each existing wall component and coordinate.

This is a direct choice from previously fixed finite lists. Increasing a horizon cannot introduce a new analytic threshold: the functions and the incidence diagram were fixed on the full infinite tails. On a tail where topology and $q_r$ are already products, choose an increasing smooth reparameterization from the enlarged finite normal interval to the old one, equal to the identity before the old product range and sending the new endpoint to the old endpoint. Choose the same reparameterization at all occurrences of $W$, on both sides of its central hit wall. It preserves those sides and the hit wall. At corners the separately labelled normal reparameterizations commute. Their product gives an identification $\Phi_r:\widehat X_r\to X_r$ preserving every wall and sign status. The glued position maps satisfy $$\widehat q_r=q_r\circ\Phi_r,
 \qquad \widehat H_r=H_r\circ\Phi_r,
 \qquad
 d_0(\widehat H_r,\widehat q_r)\le r,$$ because each position map is independent of the moved normal variables. Properness is preserved by this homeomorphism. Thus horizon enlargement introduces no error or change of position data. For the rest of the step, relabel these enlarged representatives as $X_r,q_r,H_r$.

The compatible cutting functions now glue to a single smooth function on this enlarged $X_r$. Near any old wall stratum the function is a pullback from the hit piece, independent of all its signed normal coordinates. Its zero set $L$ is therefore regular and transverse to every old wall stratum. More explicitly, in a chart $(b,s_1,\ldots,s_d)$ at an old stratum, the old walls are $s_j=0$ and the new function is $g_B(b)$. The face hypothesis says $\mathrm dg_B\ne0$ at its zeros. Hence the old normal covectors $\mathrm ds_1,\ldots,\mathrm ds_d$ together with $\mathrm dg_B$ are independent. This also covers a stratum involving the radial wall. At this point $L$ has been formed without choosing a new horizon. For a color split its forced signs give $$q_r(L)\subset X\setminus(A_i\cup B_i).$$ In the present one-ball-per-label setup, $L\subset q_r^{-1}(B_{d_0}(x_i,2D))$. The latter set has compact closure because $q_r$ is proper and the original closed outer ball is compact. Since $L$ is closed in $X_r$, the whole color wall $L$ is compact. In particular each component is compact, and there are only finitely many components. More generally, a closed regular hypersurface with compact components has a locally finite component family: a regular hypersurface chart meets only its own component, and compact subsets of the hypersurface have finite such covers. The first radial wall need not be compact as a whole; its stated locally finite compact-component hypothesis gives exactly what is needed for the same construction.

Only now assign a finite new horizon to each component of $L$. Cut along $L$, attach a cylinder on each side, and identify the two outer cylinder boundaries as the new central hit wall. Near an old wall this is the product of the operation on its hit face with the old signed normal coordinate. The same statement holds at multiple old intersections. Thus old walls extend smoothly through the new cylinders, and all walls have transverse intersection charts. The resulting pieces are exactly the truncations of the newly appended statuses: the old clamps preserve $L$ and its signs and extend across fresh cylinders keeping their new coordinate fixed; the fresh-coordinate clamp commutes with all of them. In particular every full new packet retracts onto one nonempty connected piece in the reconstruction. This verifies the component assertion needed to assign its coordinate at the next stage.

Let $c:X_{r+1}\to X_r$ collapse the inserted cylinders to $L$ and be the identity on the bulk. This map is proper. A compact subset of $X_r$ meets only finitely many new wall components; its inverse image is contained in its compact bulk copy together with finitely many finite cylinders over compact subsets of these compact components. The glued map is $q_{r+1}=q_r\circ c$, and so is proper. Every old compact wall meets finitely many new components, and its transverse intersection with them is compact. Its extension adds only finitely many compact finite cylinders; the old wall therefore remains compact. Local finiteness of all pieces follows locally from the finite collection of transverse wall charts, together with the compact truncation descriptions. This proves (i) and the properness assertion without using any later wall.

We next choose collars that preserve all old strata. The compatible packet metrics glue on the enlarged reconstruction: near an old hit stratum they have the form $h_B+\sum_j\mathrm ds_j^2$. On a neighborhood of $L$, write $u$ for the glued cutting function and use the transverse field $$V=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}u}{|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}u|^2},\qquad V(u)=1.$$ In each old product chart, $u=g_B(b)$ and this field has no old normal component. Thus it is tangent to every old wall meeting that chart and is the pullback of the corresponding face field. Equivalently, one can patch such local fields with a compatible partition of unity: $V(u)=1$ and tangency are linear conditions, so they survive patching. The flow supplies product collars at old intersections. It preserves every old wall and each side, by uniqueness of the flow and continuity from the identity. This explains why a transverse ordinary collar chosen without the product condition would not suffice here.

Choose pairwise disjoint locally finite strips around the compact components of $L$ using this flow. A positive strip width exists on each component by compactness; it may be decreased to keep strips disjoint. For each component, continuity of $q_r$ and compactness permit the width to be so small that $q_r$-images of any two points on the same flow segment have $d_0$-distance less than one. A monotone normal reparameterization compresses the two finite inserted intervals and the old strip into that old strip, fixing its outer boundary. It produces a homeomorphism $h:X_{r+1}\to X_r$, equal to the identity off the strips, with $$d_0\bigl(q_r(h(z)),q_r(c(z))\bigr)<1.$$ Different strip widths and cylinder lengths are allowed on different components. Local finiteness makes these definitions a global homeomorphism. Set $H_{r+1}=H_r\circ h$. The triangle inequality and <a href="#packet:displacement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:displacement">[packet:displacement]</a> give the new bound $r+1$. The compression follows the stratum-preserving collar, so it also preserves every old wall and its two side statuses. The old signs are pulled back under $c$, including across the cylinders, and the newly imposed signs hold on the prescribed inverse images. Pullbacks of their open phases stay open. This proves (iv) and finishes the induction. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-packet-small-displacement"></a>
<div id="packet:small-displacement" class="remark">

*Remark 36* (Prescribed reconstruction errors). The number $1$ in the last collar comparison can be replaced at stage $r$ by any prescribed $\varepsilon_r>0$. Each compact wall component has its strip chosen separately, with the same error bound $\varepsilon_r$ on every component. Disjoint strips give a supremum bound, rather than a sum over components. The resulting error after finitely many stages is at most $\sum_{j<r}\varepsilon_j$. This freedom concerns the original position map and is independent of the analytic lengths of the inserted finite collars. The spatial localization and limit of such reconstructions are treated in the main reconstruction argument.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Initialization

<details id="proof-stmt-packet-small-displacement"><summary>Proof of stmt-packet-small-displacement</summary>

*Proof of <a href="#packet:initialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:initialization">19</a>.* The decreasing solution of $2|y'|=.5y^2+\pi_0$, from $+\infty$ to $-\infty$, has finite crossing length $$\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{2\,\mathrm dy}{.5y^2+\pi_0}
       =2\pi\sqrt{2/\pi_0}=T_0.$$ Extend it by $+\infty$ before the crossing and by $-\infty$ after it. This is continuous as an extended-real-valued function, and all its finite truncations are locally Lipschitz. Composing the transition, begun at distance $2$ from $A_i$, with the $1$-Lipschitz function $d_h(A_i,\cdot)$ gives $$2|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}_h\mu_i|\le .5\mu_i^2+\pi_0
                    \le a_0\mu_i^2+\pi_0$$ almost everywhere on the finite band. Take $$U_i^+=\{d_h(A_i,\cdot)<1\},\qquad
 U_i^-=\{d_h(A_i,\cdot)>T_0+3\}.$$ Their closures lie strictly inside the respective pole interiors; they contain $A_i$ and $B_i$. The requested finite separations are available from <a href="#rounds:wide" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rounds:wide">14</a> after $N_*$, and hence $T_0$, are fixed. Their size does not change the original radius $D$.

We give the radial construction without a bounded-geometry hypothesis. Assume first that $X$ is noncompact, fix $o\in X$, and put $d(x)=d_h(o,x)$. Choose a countable locally finite cover by relatively compact coordinate neighborhoods on which the metric oscillation is small, and a subordinate smooth partition of unity $\{\chi_j\}_{j\ge1}$ whose supports are compact in those neighborhoods. Convolution in a slightly larger coordinate neighborhood gives smooth $d_j$ with $$|d_j-d|<\epsilon_j,\qquad |\mathrm dd_j|_h\le\tfrac32
       \quad\hbox{near }\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\chi_j.$$ The derivative estimate follows from the local Lipschitz bound for $d$ and the small metric oscillation; the convolution radius can then be reduced to make its value error arbitrarily small. Let $C_j=\sup|\mathrm d\chi_j|_h<\infty$, and choose $0<\epsilon_j<\min\{1/2,2^{-j-2}/(1+C_j)\}$. The locally finite sum $\rho=2+\sum_j\chi_jd_j$ is smooth and $|\rho-(2+d)|<1/2$. Moreover, since $\sum_j\mathrm d\chi_j=0$, $$\mathrm d\rho=\sum_j\chi_j\mathrm dd_j
             +\sum_j(d_j-d)\mathrm d\chi_j,
 \qquad |\mathrm d\rho|_h\le\tfrac32+\sum_j2^{-j-2}<2.$$ Completeness makes $d$ proper, so $\rho$ is proper and bounded below. Thus a smooth proper radius with bounded gradient has been constructed using only local smoothness and completeness.

Choose successive plateau intervals of the values of $\rho$, with alternating signs and positive-width cores strictly inside each plateau. Separate successive plateaus by transition intervals of width greater than $2T_0$. On such an interval, a copy of the crossing $y$, parametrized by $(\rho-a)/L$ with $L>2$ and with sign reversed on alternate intervals, obeys the same profile inequality because $|\mathrm d\rho|_h/L\le1$. Include an initial plateau extending below the minimum of $\rho$. Core closures have positive value margins to both ends of their plateaus, except at the harmless lower end of the initial plateau. Sard’s theorem supplies regular values in those margins. The sublevel domain at the initial plateau, and the domains between two such regular levels at later plateaus, are compact smooth barriers enclosing the corresponding core closures and lying in the same pole interiors. These barriers and the transition blocks form locally finite families, since their value intervals tend to infinity. Connectedness and properness imply that the image of $\rho$ contains all sufficiently large values, so the separating subbands are nonempty.

A zero wall respecting the signs misses every separating core subband. On any connected wall component, the continuous function $\rho$ therefore takes values in one bounded intervening block. The component is closed in $X$, and properness of $\rho$ makes it compact. For a fixed sign, successive core subbands of the opposite sign bound the $\rho$-values of every connected component of the closed side; the central component is bounded by the first opposite separator. These closed-side components are compact as well. In a compact block, regularity gives a finite cover by charts in which the wall is a single sheet and each closed side is a half-space or the full chart. Only finitely many components can meet that block. The blocks are locally finite, which proves the required local finiteness of wall and side components.

If $X$ is compact, set the radial profile identically $+\infty$, with $U_{\mathrm{rad}}^+=X$ and $U_{\mathrm{rad}}^-=\varnothing$. The constant positive defining function realizes its empty wall; the single nonempty closed side is $X$, which is compact. All radial profile and confinement assertions then hold directly.

Finally, the initial scalar requirement holds everywhere, since $$\lambda_0+b_0\sum_{j\in J_0}\Pi_j
 =\frac14+1.12N_*\frac{1}{10N_*}
 =.362<.4\le R_X.$$ The profile, core, and forced-preimage conditions have already been verified. This proves the invariant at stage zero. ◻

</details>

The geometric lemmas require compatible regular defining functions. The minimizing boundaries used to obtain them may initially be singular. The remaining appendices first construct these minimizing partitions, enlarge the remaining cores until the required guards avoid the singular sets, and then produce the regular compatible functions to which <a href="#packet:split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:split">31</a> applies. The curvature restriction to the required sets is respected precisely by <a href="#packet:counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:counts">18</a>.

<a id="sec:cut"></a>
### Weighted minimizing cuts and the forms on retained sides

Prescribed-curvature perimeter minimization and convergence of its boundaries have classical precedents in Massari and Massari–Pepe (Massari 1975; Massari and Pepe 1975) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[massari1975](#ref-massari1975), [massariPepe1975](#ref-massariPepe1975)]. For scalar-curvature applications, prescribed-mean-curvature minimization is the $\mu$-bubble method described by M. Gromov (1996§$5\frac56$). We prove here the weighted, varying-metric and product-end versions required by the cutting construction, including the forms on both retained sides.

Fix a finite stage $r$ and the label $j_0$ to be processed. The packet and profile hypotheses are those of [packet:category,packet:invariant] [packet:category](#packet:category), [packet:invariant](#packet:invariant). For the finite-horizon formulation in this appendix, only finitely many labels and finitely many nonunit old warps occur in each incident diagram under consideration. The processed potential has a positive lower bound $$\Pi_{j_0}\ge\pi_{\rm cut}>0.$$ A common lower bound for all labels at all stages is not required. The constants satisfy $a_r\le .63$, $b_r\ge1.07$, and $\lambda_r>\lambda_{r+1}$. The extension to a countable collection of labels is proved in the main text; every argument below uses only the current pressure and the indicated finite lists.

Throughout, $E$ denotes a parent packet of base dimension $m\ge2$. After the radial split it has the compact truncations, finite incidence, and exact product ends of <a href="#packet:category" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:category">16</a>. At the initial split we instead work on the complete original base, and use the locally finite compact radial barriers of <a href="#packet:initialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:initialization">19</a>. We assume no volume growth or bounded geometry on that initial base.

On a finite incident diagram, insert unit warps where necessary so that the old warp lists agree. Their metric is $$\widehat g=g+\sum_{i=1}^q f_i^2\,\mathrm d\theta_i^2,
 \qquad \omega=\prod_{i=1}^q f_i,
 \qquad \mathrm d\mu=\omega\,\mathrm d\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g.$$ Empty products are one. Fiber volume constants are suppressed throughout. Adding a unit warp changes neither $\omega$ nor any formula below. At the initial radial stage the list need only be finite on each compact window; the compatible local expressions then agree on overlaps. No infinite-dimensional torus is used. In particular all minimizing sets, BV compactness, tangent cones, and singular-dimension bounds concern the $m$-dimensional base, regardless of the length of the warp list. Write $R=\mathop{\mathrm{Scal}}_{\widehat g}$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_\omega Y=\omega^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_g(\omega Y)$. For a set $A$ of locally finite perimeter define its weighted perimeter by $$P_\omega(A;U)=\int_{U\cap\partial^*A}\omega\,\mathrm d\mathcal H_g^{m-1}.$$ The reduced-boundary representation is the framework developed by De Giorgi (De Giorgi 1954, 1955) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[deGiorgi1954](#ref-deGiorgi1954), [deGiorgi1955](#ref-deGiorgi1955)]; the current formulation and slicing originate in Federer and Fleming (Federer and Fleming 1960) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[federerFleming1960](#ref-federerFleming1960)]. We use the precise representation and slicing statements in Simon (2014, chap. 3, Section 4, and Chapter 6, Section 4) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[simonGMT](#ref-simonGMT)]. All set equalities and inclusions involving measurable phases are understood up to $\mu$-null sets. The phase representative used for geometry will be specified below. Constants in local estimates may depend on a compact window; after the radial split they can be chosen uniformly on each fixed packet, without any uniformity between packets. No scalar-curvature lower bound is used to construct the cuts or to prove their local regularity. The parent scalar bound enters only at the end of this appendix, and only on the required region $D_E$ of <a href="#packet:required-set" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:required-set">[packet:required-set]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Smooth finite pressures and absolute minimizers

<a id="stmt-cut-pressure"></a>
<div id="cut:pressure" class="lemma">

<a id="cut:pressure-gradient"></a>
<a id="cut:barrier-pressure"></a>
**Lemma 37** (Realizing the processed profile). *There is a smooth finite compatible function $p_0$ and a number $c_*>0$ such that every $p=p_0+c$, $|c|<c_*$, satisfies $$\label{cut:pressure-gradient}
             2|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}p|\le .67p^2+1.02\Pi_{j_0}.$$ There are compatible smooth barrier domains $D^+,D^-$ whose interiors contain the corresponding forced-core closures and whose closures lie in the respective pole interiors. Each admits a smooth vector field $Y^\pm$ with $|Y^\pm|\le1$, equal to the outward unit normal on its boundary, such that, for all these shifts, $$\label{cut:barrier-pressure}
 p\ge1+\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_\omega Y^+\quad\hbox{on }D^+,
 \qquad
 -p\ge1+\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_\omega Y^-\quad\hbox{on }D^-.$$ After the radial split all this data has bounded derivatives on each packet. At the initial radial split the barriers are the separate relatively compact domains in the radial pole bands, and the choices are locally finite with locally prescribed bounds.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cut-pressure"><summary>Proof of stmt-cut-pressure</summary>

*Proof.* The nesting of the core closures inside the pole interiors and the compatible approximation construction of <a href="#packet:approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:approximation">34</a> give smooth separating cutoff functions. A level which is regular on every packet gives the required domains: there are only countably many packets, so Sard’s theorem permits a common level. Their far collars are exact copies of the face domains. On a fixed packet, compact parts and the finite face diagram give a positive tubular radius and bounded geometry for the resulting boundary. Extend its outward normal in a smaller tube and multiply by a cutoff of absolute value at most one. This supplies $Y^\pm$ and a bound for its weighted divergence. The radial case uses the relatively compact pole barriers individually.

Fix the barriers and their vector fields first. By <a href="#packet:approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:approximation">34</a>, choose a compatible positive smooth function $h$ with sufficiently small value and first derivative, and put $M=h^{-1}$. Require $$M\ge3+|\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_\omega Y^\pm|\quad\hbox{on }D^\pm,
 \qquad 2|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}h|\le a_r.$$ Only finitely many coface requests reach a given packet; at the radial step the requests are locally finite on compact sets. The choices therefore have the stated compatibility. The derivative inequality implies $2|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}M|\le a_rM^2$. The clipped function $$z=\max(-M,\min(\mu_{j_0},M))$$ is finite and locally Lipschitz. At almost every point its derivative is that of one active branch, and consequently $$2|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}z|\le a_r z^2+\Pi_{j_0}.$$ This assertion also holds near the original poles, since the clipping levels are locally bounded and finite truncations of $\mu_{j_0}$ are locally Lipschitz. On $D^\pm$ one has $z=\pm M$.

Here is the quantitative smoothing fact being used. If a locally Lipschitz function $z$ obeys $|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}z|\le F$ almost everywhere for a continuous positive $F$, then, for arbitrary positive continuous error targets, local convolution in sufficiently small metric charts followed by a partition of unity gives a smooth $z_1$ with prescribed value error and $|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}z_1|\le F$ plus the prescribed error. Indeed convolution averages the gradient inequality, the oscillation of $F$ and the metric on its support tends to zero, and differentiating the partition of unity produces only the value errors times its fixed derivatives. A locally finite cover permits the errors to be assigned summably at each point. For compatible smoothing, start on deepest faces. On overlaps in far collars the clipped functions and all face copies agree. Blend with the cutoffs in <a href="#packet:approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:approximation">34</a>; the same value-error estimate pays the additional cutoff derivatives. Preallocate smaller errors on faces to meet their finitely many coface requests. Thus the gradient estimate is always compared with the single continuous function $(a_rz^2+\Pi_{j_0})/2$, rather than with unrelated bounds for the pieces.

Choose the value error at most $\min\{1/4,10^{-3}\sqrt{\Pi_{j_0}}\}$ and the gradient error so that $$2|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}p_0|\le a_rz^2+1.005\Pi_{j_0}.$$ These are positive continuous error targets. For $e=p_0-z$, $$.65(z+e)^2-.63z^2\ge-20.475e^2,$$ so $a_r\le .63$ gives $$2|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}p_0|\le .65p_0^2+1.01\Pi_{j_0}.$$ On $D^\pm$ the value error and the earlier choice of $M$ give $\pm p_0\ge 11/4+|\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_\omega Y^\pm|$. Finally set $$c_* =\min\{1/2,.01\sqrt{\pi_{\rm cut}}\}.$$ The elementary identity $$.67(y+c)^2-.65y^2\ge-21.775c^2\qquad(y,c\in\mathbb R)$$ and $\Pi_{j_0}\ge\pi_{\rm cut}$ prove <a href="#cut:pressure-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:pressure-gradient">[cut:pressure-gradient]</a> for every $|c|<c_*$; the barrier margin also persists. Thus the domains, clipping height, smoothing, and open shift interval have all been fixed before selecting a minimizing phase. On a post-radial packet every smooth datum just constructed has bounded derivatives, by its compact parts and exact products. At the radial step the construction gives the corresponding bounds on each compact window, which is all that is used. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cut-minimizer-definition"></a>
<div id="cut:minimizer-definition" class="definition">

<a id="cut:local-minimality"></a>
**Definition 38** (Local absolute minimizer). For smooth $p$ a phase $A$ is a local absolute minimizer if for every relatively compact open $U\subset E$ and every locally finite-perimeter set $B$ with $A\mathbin\triangle B\Subset U$, $$\label{cut:local-minimality}
 P_\omega(A;U)-\int_{A\cap U}p\,\mathrm d\mu
 \le P_\omega(B;U)-\int_{B\cap U}p\,\mathrm d\mu.$$ The notation $A\mathbin\triangle B\Subset U$ means that the discrepancy is contained, modulo null sets, in a compact subset of $U$.

</div>

<a id="stmt-cut-bv-compactness"></a>
<div id="cut:bv-compactness" class="lemma">

**Lemma 39** (Existence and strict local compactness). *Every smooth positive weight and smooth locally bounded pressure on a smooth manifold admit a local absolute minimizer. Suppose on exhausting coordinate windows that metrics and positive weights converge smoothly and pressures converge locally uniformly. Any sequence of corresponding local absolute minimizers has a subsequence whose indicators converge in $L^1_{\rm loc}$ to a local absolute minimizer of the limit data. Along that subsequence the weighted perimeter measures converge weakly locally, as do the unweighted perimeter measures and their associated unoriented perimeter varifolds. The assertion includes smooth pointed rescalings when the rescaled data have the stated convergence.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cut-bv-compactness"><summary>Proof of stmt-cut-bv-compactness</summary>

*Proof.* We first establish the comparison formulas that will show both minimality of a limiting phase and absence of perimeter loss. We then construct exhaustion minimizers and apply these formulas to locally convergent sequences.

<a id="cut:lattice"></a>
*Comparison formulas.* Local $L^1$ compactness under local perimeter bounds and lower semicontinuity are Simon (2014, chap. 2, Theorem 2.6) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[simonGMT](#ref-simonGMT)]; positive smooth weights and smooth metrics on a relatively compact chart give equivalent variation norms. The further BV inputs are the perimeter lattice inequality and the trace and gluing formulas, both across a fixed smooth face and across almost every admissible level of a Lipschitz slicing function. The lattice inequality is $$\label{cut:lattice}
 P_\omega(A\cap B;U)+P_\omega(A\cup B;U)
          \le P_\omega(A;U)+P_\omega(B;U).$$ For smooth functions $u,v$, the gradients of $\min(u,v)$ and $\max(u,v)$ are the two original gradients, possibly interchanged, almost everywhere. Strictly approximate the two characteristic functions, apply this identity, and use lower semicontinuity on the minimum and maximum. This gives <a href="#cut:lattice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:lattice">[cut:lattice]</a>, with its smooth metric and weight.

We record the trace argument for a fixed smooth comparison face, since such a face need not be an almost-everywhere selected level. Work first in a flattened coordinate collar $Q\times(-a,a)\subset\mathbb R^{m-1}\times\mathbb R$, with the face $T=Q\times\{0\}$ and the plus side given by $t>0$. All collars used here are buffered inside a relatively compact ambient chart. The functions under consideration are integrable and have finite total variation on each smaller collar reaching $T$; for the restrictions of our finite-perimeter phases this follows from local finite perimeter in the ambient chart. Local BV regularity only on sets bounded away from $T$ would not suffice.

<a id="cut:fixed-face-slices"></a>
Let $w$ be such a BV function on the plus half-collar, and let $D_t w$ denote its distributional derivative in the $t$ direction. Choose a compact tangential box $K$ and an open box $K'$ with $K\Subset K'\Subset Q$, and fix $a_0<a$. Mollify on the open half-collar, using kernels small enough to remain inside it on each fixed strip. Fix a countable exhaustion by buffered tangential boxes and compact height intervals contained in $(0,a)$. Choose one subsequence whose local $L^1$ errors are summable on every member of this exhaustion, by making its $k$th error at most $2^{-k}$ on each of the first $k$ members. Fubini then gives convergence in $L^1$ on horizontal slices for all heights in one common set of full measure, simultaneously on the exhaustion boxes. Restriction gives the same convergence on $K'$. For a smooth mollification the fundamental theorem of calculus, followed by integration over $K$, bounds the difference of two slices by the integral of the absolute vertical derivative on the intervening strip. Convolution bounds this integral by the variation of $D_t w$ on a slightly enlarged strip; the tangential enlargement lies in $K'$ when the kernel is small enough. In the limit the enlarged tangential box lies in $K'$ and the height interval is contained in $[s,t]$; thus the limiting bound is at most $|D_t w|(K'\times[s,t])$. Excluding heights at which $|D_t w|(K'\times\{t\})>0$, gives $$\label{cut:fixed-face-slices}
 \int_K |w(y,t)-w(y,s)|\,\mathrm dy
 \le |D_t w|\bigl(K'\times(s,t)\bigr),
 \qquad 0<s<t<a_0,$$ for all $s,t$ in a common set of full measure. There are at most countably many excluded atom heights, since the variation on the buffered collar is finite.

The right-hand side of <a href="#cut:fixed-face-slices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:fixed-face-slices">[cut:fixed-face-slices]</a> tends to zero uniformly for good heights $s,t\downarrow0$: the sets $K'\times(0,\delta)$ decrease to the empty set inside the open half-collar and have finite variation measure. The good slices are therefore Cauchy in $L^1(K)$ and have a limit, denoted $\operatorname{Tr}_+w$. Traces obtained on nested tangential boxes agree by uniqueness of the $L^1$ limit, giving a locally integrable trace on $T$. Reflection gives the analogous trace $\operatorname{Tr}_-w$ from the minus side. For characteristic functions these traces are themselves characteristic functions almost everywhere, since an $L^1$ limit of characteristic functions has an almost-everywhere convergent subsequence. In particular the phase traces take values in $\{0,1\}$.

The trace also gives the required integration-by-parts formula. On the plus side, multiply a smooth vector field, compactly supported in the tangential directions and away from the outer end of the collar, by a cutoff which is zero for $t\le\varepsilon$ and one for $t\ge2\varepsilon$. Apply the distributional derivative identity inside the open half-collar and let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. The cutoff derivative term tends to the pairing of $\operatorname{Tr}_+w$ with the vector field’s $t$ component at $T$: its derivative has integral one, and the good slices converge in $L^1$ uniformly as their heights approach zero. The other terms converge by integrability and finite variation. This gives the boundary term with outward normal $-\partial_t$ on the plus side; on the minus side the outward normal is $\partial_t$. Undoing the smooth coordinate change gives the corresponding Riemannian formula. More explicitly, the smooth coordinate volume density is included in the divergence test; its boundary flux is exactly $\langle X,\nu\rangle\,\mathrm d\mathcal H_g^{m-1}$. Thus, if $D_g w$ denotes the intrinsic distributional gradient measure, with convention $\int w\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_g X\,\mathrm d\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g=-\int\langle X,\mathrm dD_g w\rangle$ for tests supported in the open side, then $$\int_{U_\pm}w\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_g X\,\mathrm d\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g
 =-\int_{U_\pm}\langle X,\mathrm dD_g w\rangle
   +\int_T\operatorname{Tr}_\pm w\,
                   \langle X,\nu_{\mathrm{out},\pm}\rangle
                         \,\mathrm d\mathcal H_g^{m-1}.$$ The vector field here has compact support in the collar, so no other boundary contribution occurs.

Now orient $\nu_T$ from the minus side to the plus side, and glue a BV function $u$ on the minus side to a BV function $v$ on the plus side. Adding their integration-by-parts identities shows that the gradient measure of the glued function is $$(D_g u)\lfloor U_-+(D_g v)\lfloor U_+
 +\bigl(\operatorname{Tr}_+v-\operatorname{Tr}_-u\bigr)
                  \nu_T\,\mathcal H_g^{m-1}\lfloor T.$$ These three vector measures are concentrated on the disjoint measurable sets $U_-$, $U_+$, and $T$. Their variations therefore add. Multiplying the variation measures by the smooth positive weight $\omega$ gives the weighted formula, whose extra interface cost is precisely $$\int_T\omega\,
       |\operatorname{Tr}_+v-\operatorname{Tr}_-u|
                         \,\mathrm d\mathcal H_g^{m-1}.$$ We are weighting the variation, not differentiating $\omega$ times the glued function. For phases the last integrand is the indicator of the trace-disagreement set, multiplied by $\omega$.

We will also glue across almost every level of a locally Lipschitz slicing function $f$, whose level sets need not be smooth. For this distinct use, the reduced-boundary representation identifies a phase locally with its integer-multiplicity integration current and identifies boundary mass with perimeter (Simon 2014, chap. 3, Section 4, Equation (4.1) and Theorem 4.3) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[simonGMT](#ref-simonGMT)]. In a locally oriented chart the phase current has locally finite mass and locally finite boundary mass. Lipschitz coarea and the current slicing and restriction formulas therefore apply (Simon 2014, chap. 3, Section 2, Equations (2.8)–(2.9); Chapter 6, Section 4, Lemmas 4.1 and 4.5 and Definition 4.4) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[simonGMT](#ref-simonGMT)]. In particular, writing $J$ for either phase current, for almost every level $\tau$ one has $$\langle J,f,\tau\rangle
   =\partial\bigl(J\lfloor\{f<\tau\}\bigr)
             -(\partial J)\lfloor\{f<\tau\}.$$ Apply this identity to both phases, excluding also the null set of levels charged by their local volume or perimeter measures. For a top-dimensional phase current the slice has multiplicity one on the sliced phase and zero elsewhere. Indeed the slicing definition restricts its phase indicator to the noncritical part of the level; coarea shows that critical points and any changes of the ambient almost-everywhere representative affect almost no level in positive hypersurface measure. The two phase slices have the same orientation induced by $f$ in the locally oriented chart, so their difference has multiplicity equal to the difference of the two sliced indicators. The boundary of their gluing has the two restricted boundary currents and the difference of their level slices as its interface term. Its extra weighted mass is bounded by the weighted hypersurface measure of the set where the sliced phase values differ. This is the almost-everywhere interface-cost bound used in the annular comparisons, distance-ball comparisons, and level-set deletions below. A countable collection of phases, including a converging sequence and its limit, can use one common set of admissible levels by intersecting their full-measure sets. If the phase indicators converge locally in $L^1$, coarea on a fixed buffered slicing annulus makes the integrated sliced disagreement tend to zero. Pass to a subsequence for which these integrals are summable. Fubini then makes the disagreement tend to zero at almost every level, still within the common admissible set. This supplies a fixed gluing level with vanishing interface cost in the compactness argument below. All orientations in this argument are local; global orientability is not required.

*Exhaustion minimizers and local compactness.* Choose a smooth relatively compact exhaustion $U_k\Subset E$. Minimize over phases vanishing outside $\overline U_k$, taking the total perimeter, including the interface on $\partial U_k$, and the pressure integral inside $U_k$. The empty phase is admissible. Because the pressure is bounded on $\overline U_k$, an energy bound gives a perimeter bound. The class is closed under $L^1$ convergence, the volume term is continuous, and perimeter is lower semicontinuous. The direct method thus gives a minimizer, locally absolute minimizing in $U_k$.

The same local bound applies to arbitrary local absolute minimizers. In a coordinate ball $B_t$ compactly contained in their comparison domain, comparison with the phase cleared inside $B_t$ gives, for almost every $t$, $$P_\omega(A;B_t)\le\int_{\partial B_t}\omega\,\mathrm d\mathcal H_g^{m-1}
                         +\int_{B_t}|p|\,\mathrm d\mu.$$ Choose $t$ in a fixed annulus around the smaller window to be bounded. Coarea bounds the first term for a suitable slice. Smoothly convergent metrics and positive weights and locally uniformly bounded pressures make this a uniform local bound. Diagonal BV compactness therefore produces a locally $L^1$ convergent subsequence, both for the exhaustion minimizers and for any sequence in the statement.

*Minimality and perimeter convergence.* The gluing formulas now apply to the locally convergent subsequence. Choose nested relatively compact windows $U\Subset V$ with a Lipschitz foliation of the intervening annulus. The integral over slicing parameters of the trace discrepancy between $A_i$ and $A$ tends to zero by $L^1$ convergence and coarea. One can therefore choose a slicing hypersurface between $U$ and $V$ whose discrepancy tends to zero along a subsequence. Glue $A$ inside this surface to $A_i$ outside. Minimality of $A_i$ bounds its interior perimeter from above by that of $A$, the vanishing trace cost, and a vanishing difference of volume terms. The last difference vanishes by the convergence of the data and indicators. First use windows whose limiting perimeter gives zero mass to their boundaries; then shrink the annulus. Together with lower semicontinuity this proves weak local convergence of the perimeter measures. Replacing the interior copy of $A$ by any compact competitor $B$ gives the same upper comparison with $B$, since $B=A$ on the gluing annulus. Lower semicontinuity on the left proves <a href="#cut:local-minimality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:local-minimality">[cut:local-minimality]</a> for $A$. Smooth metric and weight changes make only errors tending uniformly to zero on each fixed window, so the same argument proves the convergent-data assertion.

*Changing metrics and varifold convergence.* For the varifold assertion, account explicitly for the changing metric. In a coordinate chart let $D_0\chi_{A_i}$ be the Euclidean distributional derivative and let $(g_i)$ denote the positive metric matrices. The vector measures $$\mathbf v_i=\sqrt{\det g_i}\,g_i^{-1/2}D_0\chi_{A_i}$$ have Euclidean total variation equal to the $g_i$-perimeter measures. The matrices and their derivatives converge smoothly, so distributional convergence and the local mass bounds give $\mathbf v_i\rightharpoonup
\mathbf v$. The perimeter conclusion gives $|\mathbf v_i|\rightharpoonup|\mathbf v|$ as well.

This strict convergence also controls the unit polar vectors. To see this, on a compact window approximate the limiting polar vector in $L^2(|\mathbf v|)$ by a continuous bounded vector field $e$. Expand $$\int|\mathbf v_i/|\mathbf v_i|-e|^2\,\mathrm d|\mathbf v_i|
 =\int(1+|e|^2)\,\mathrm d|\mathbf v_i|-2\int e\cdot\mathrm d\mathbf v_i.$$ Weak convergence gives its limit, and then the approximation makes that limit arbitrarily small. Uniform continuity consequently gives convergence for every continuous compactly supported integrand of position and polar vector. The unoriented $g_i$-orthogonal tangent hyperplane is a continuous function of this vector and $g_i$. This proves varifold convergence. Multiplication or division by the smooth positive weight on a fixed compact window gives the asserted weighted and unweighted versions. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Barriers, ordering, and exact product limits

<a id="stmt-cut-barriers"></a>
<div id="cut:barriers" class="lemma">

**Lemma 40** (Barriers hold for every minimizer). *Every local absolute minimizer for a pressure of <a href="#cut:pressure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:pressure">37</a> contains $D^+$ and excludes $D^-$. Its boundary support consequently misses a neighborhood of each current core closure. After the radial split this neighborhood can be chosen with a positive uniform radius on each packet. At the radial split the assertion is local on the corresponding compact radial blocks.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cut-barriers"><summary>Proof of stmt-cut-barriers</summary>

<a id="cut:barrier-volume"></a>
*Proof.* We first prove the positive assertion. Put $D=D^+$, $Y=Y^+$ and $G=D\setminus A$. For almost every distance radius $R$ about a fixed point, $U=B_R$ is a finite-perimeter set, its boundary has zero mass for the relevant perimeter measures, and BV traces are available. Write $$V(R)=\mu(G\cap B_R),\qquad
 S_R=\int_{\partial^*B_R}\operatorname{Tr}(\chi_G)\omega\,
                  \mathrm d\mathcal H^{m-1}.$$ Distance coarea, with $|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}d|=1$ almost everywhere off the center, gives local absolute continuity and $V'(R)=S_R$ for almost every $R$. Weighted Gauss–Green, $Y=\nu_D$ on $\partial D$, and $|Y|\le1$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 P_\omega(D;U)-P_\omega(A\cap D;U)
 &\le\int_{G\cap U}\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_\omega Y\,\mathrm d\mu\\
 &\quad-\int_{\partial^*U}
             \operatorname{Tr}(\chi_G)\,Y\cdot\nu_U\,
                         \omega\,\mathrm d\mathcal H^{m-1}.
\end{aligned}$$ By <a href="#cut:lattice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:lattice">[cut:lattice]</a> this bounds the interior perimeter increase on replacing $A$ by $A\cup D$ in $U$. Glue back to $A$ outside $U$; the extra perimeter is at most $S_R$. This is a compact comparison in a slightly larger relatively compact open set. By <a href="#cut:barrier-pressure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:barrier-pressure">[cut:barrier-pressure]</a> and minimality, $$\label{cut:barrier-volume}
                         V(R)\le2S_R=2V'(R).$$ The weighted ball volume is at most polynomial by <a href="#packet:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:geometry">32</a>, since $\omega$ is bounded on the packet. If $V(R_0)>0$, <a href="#cut:barrier-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:barrier-volume">[cut:barrier-volume]</a> would imply $V(R)\ge V(R_0)e^{(R-R_0)/2}$, a contradiction. Thus $G$ is null. For a relatively compact radial barrier, simply add all of $D$; no slicing terms occur, and the same comparison gives $\mu(G)=0$ directly. The complement of $A$ minimizes pressure $-p$, proving the negative assertion. The domains contain the core closures in their interiors; the packetwise positive separation follows from exact products and the compact-part property. A phase equal to one or zero on an open set has no perimeter support there. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cut-generic-uniqueness"></a>
<div id="cut:generic-uniqueness" class="proposition">

**Proposition 41** (Generic uniqueness on all required products). *After the radial split one common shift $c$, with $|c|<c_*$, can be chosen such that there is exactly one local absolute minimizer, modulo null sets, on every packet $E$ and every product $E\times\mathbb R^d$, $d\ge0$, for the pulled-back weight and pressure $p_0+c$. The unique product phase is exactly $A_E\times\mathbb R^d$ modulo null sets, where $A_E$ is the unique phase on $E$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cut-generic-uniqueness"><summary>Proof of stmt-cut-generic-uniqueness</summary>

*Proof.* On any one such space, consider *arbitrary* minimizers $A$ for $p_0+c_1$ and $B$ for $p_0+c_2$, with $c_1<c_2$. Put $G=A\setminus B$, $\delta=c_2-c_1$, and use the same distance-ball slicing as in the preceding proof. Replace $A$ by $A\cap B$ inside the ball and $B$ by $A\cup B$ there, gluing each to its own exterior phase. The two gluing costs total at most $2S_R$. Their interior perimeter increments have nonpositive sum by <a href="#cut:lattice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:lattice">[cut:lattice]</a>; their pressure increments sum to precisely $-\delta\mu(G\cap B_R)$. Adding the two minimality inequalities gives <a id="cut:ordering-volume"></a>$$\label{cut:ordering-volume}
                 \delta V(R)\le2V'(R).$$ Polynomial weighted growth, also on $E\times\mathbb R^d$, forces $V=0$. Thus *every* minimizer at the smaller shift is contained in *every* minimizer at the larger shift.

Choose countably many nonnegative compactly supported continuous test functions $\varphi_a$ which separate locally integrable functions. For each $a$ and shift $c$, let $$I_a(c)=\left(\inf_{A\text{ minimizes at }c}
                 \int\varphi_a\chi_A\,\mathrm d\mu,
              \ \sup_{A\text{ minimizes at }c}
                 \int\varphi_a\chi_A\,\mathrm d\mu\right).$$ Existence is supplied by <a href="#cut:bv-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:bv-compactness">39</a>. The preceding ordering says that the nonempty open intervals $I_a(c)$ for distinct $c$ are disjoint. Each contains a rational number, so only countably many can be nonempty. If minimizers differ, some $\varphi_a$ distinguishes them and $I_a(c)$ is nonempty. There are therefore at most countably many exceptional shifts on this space. The packet collection and the set of integers $d$ are countable, so their exceptional shifts have a countable union. Choose $c$ in the remaining part of $(-c_*,c_*)$.

A Euclidean translation of the unique minimizer on $E\times\mathbb R^d$ is again a minimizer. Uniqueness implies translation invariance of its indicator. It suffices to impose the countable dense group of rational translations: continuity of translations in $L^1_{\rm loc}$ then gives all translations, so its Euclidean distributional derivatives vanish. The indicator is therefore $\chi_F(x)$ for a base phase $F$. BV slicing shows that $F$ has locally finite perimeter. If a compact base competitor improved its energy by $\eta>0$, use that competitor over $(-L,L)^d$ and the phase $F$ outside, retaining the original phase outside the compact base discrepancy. The energy gain is $\eta(2L)^d$. The interfaces at the Euclidean box boundary cost at most $O(L^{d-1})$ times the weighted volume of that compact discrepancy. For large $L$ this contradicts minimality. Thus $F$ minimizes on $E$, and base uniqueness identifies it with $A_E$. The case $d=0$ needs no product argument. ◻

</details>

At the radial split set $c=0$ and choose any minimizer supplied by <a href="#cut:bv-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:bv-compactness">39</a>. Its compact barriers confine its boundary to the locally finite compact transition shells. No uniqueness or polynomial-growth assertion on the initial manifold is used. We call the selected minimizing boundary a *true cut*; it will later be replaced by a compatible regular wall near the required region.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### The precise local regularity input

<a id="stmt-cut-gmt"></a>
<div id="cut:gmt" class="theorem">

<a id="cut:base-mean-curvature"></a>
**Theorem 42** (Local GMT properties used here). *Let $A$ satisfy <a href="#cut:local-minimality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:local-minimality">[cut:local-minimality]</a> on a smooth $m$-manifold, $m\ge2$, with smooth positive $\omega$ and smooth $p$. Put $S=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}|D\chi_A|$. Then $S=S_{\rm reg}\cup\Sigma$, where $S_{\rm reg}$ is a smooth embedded hypersurface, $\Sigma$ is relatively closed, and <a id="cut:singular-dimension"></a>$$\label{cut:singular-dimension}
                         \dim_{\mathcal H}\Sigma\le m-8.$$ A negative dimension bound means that $\Sigma$ is empty. The outward measure-theoretic normal extends smoothly on $S_{\rm reg}$. With $B(u,v)=\langle\nabla_u\nu,v\rangle$, its base mean curvature satisfies $$\label{cut:base-mean-curvature}
                    H_g=p-\partial_\nu\log\omega.$$ The unweighted multiplicity-one perimeter varifold has locally bounded generalized mean curvature. Its density exists at every support point; monotonicity gives local uniform area upper bounds and positive area lower bounds at support points. Under the convergences of <a href="#cut:bv-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:bv-compactness">39</a>, densities are upper semicontinuous at converging points, provided the local generalized-mean-curvature bounds are uniform. The supports converge locally, and on every compact subset of the regular limiting hypersurface convergence is smooth and single-sheeted when the data converge smoothly. On compact subsets of the interiors of the limiting phases there is eventually no boundary.*

*Every tangent at a point of $S$ is a multiplicity-one locally perimeter-minimizing boundary cone in the Euclidean tangent space. A flat tangent implies regularity. A tangent at a singular point is therefore nonflat, and its translation-invariance spine has dimension at most $m-8$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cut-gmt"><summary>Proof of stmt-cut-gmt</summary>

*Proof by first variation, strict blowups, and regularity.* Put $d=m-1$. The classical inputs are the bounded-mean-curvature monotonicity theorem and Allard’s graphical theorem (Simon 2014, chap. 4, Theorem 3.17; Chapter 5, hypotheses 5.1 and Theorem 5.2) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[simonGMT](#ref-simonGMT)], and the singular-dimension theorem for *exactly* minimizing boundary currents (Simon 2014, chap. 7, Theorem 5.8) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[simonGMT](#ref-simonGMT)]. The minimizing-boundary dimension bound follows from the work of Simons and Federer (Simons 1968; Federer 1970) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[simons1968](#ref-simons1968), [federer1970](#ref-federer1970)]. The last theorem will be applied only to constant-metric blowup limits. We verify the transfer to the present smooth weighted problem, including the singular-set compactness needed for the Hausdorff-dimension conclusion.

*First variation and local area bounds.* First variation is available before any regularity conclusion. The flow of a compactly supported smooth field $Z$ is an admissible comparison in <a href="#cut:local-minimality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:local-minimality">[cut:local-minimality]</a>, so $$\int_{\partial^*A}\omega
   \bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_S Z+\langle\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}\log\omega,Z\rangle\bigr)
       \,\mathrm d\mathcal H_g^d
 =\int_{\partial^*A}p\omega\langle Z,\nu\rangle
       \,\mathrm d\mathcal H_g^d.$$ Here tangent planes and $\nu$ are the measure-theoretic ones. Substituting $Z=W/\omega$ cancels the tangential weight derivatives and gives $$\int_{\partial^*A}\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_S W\,\mathrm d\mathcal H_g^d
  =\int_{\partial^*A}
       (p-\partial_\nu\log\omega)\langle W,\nu\rangle
          \,\mathrm d\mathcal H_g^d.$$ Thus the unweighted perimeter varifold has intrinsic generalized mean-curvature vector $-(p-\partial_\nu\log\omega)\nu$, locally bounded by $|p|+|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}\log\omega|$. Its multiplicity is one almost everywhere.

On a relatively compact coordinate patch, first pass to a smaller patch and extend its metric to a closed manifold. Explicitly, place its coordinate closure inside a coordinate ball of a flat torus and blend the given positive definite metric with the flat metric outside the smaller patch; the convex combination stays positive definite. The resulting metric is smooth and the ambient manifold is compact, so the smooth isometric embedding theorem applies (Nash 1956, Theorem 2) <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[nash1956](#ref-nash1956)]. The preceding intrinsic first variation then gives Euclidean generalized mean curvature with the additional bounded term $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_{TS}\mathrm{II}$, where $\mathrm{II}$ is the second fundamental form of this fixed embedding. Indeed decompose a Euclidean test field into its ambient-manifold tangent and normal parts and differentiate that decomposition along $TS$. Its normal contribution is exactly the trace of $\mathrm{II}$. Consequently Euclidean monotonicity applies locally. Intrinsic and embedded density limits agree, since the embedding is isometric to first order. Monotonicity gives density existence and upper semicontinuity, including at nonreduced support points; their densities are at least one, by approximation by reduced points. Together with the clearing-ball upper bound from <a href="#cut:bv-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:bv-compactness">39</a>, it gives uniform upper and positive lower area bounds on compact patches.

*A common ambient model for varying metrics.* We record carefully the uniform version when the metric changes. Suppose $g_i\to g$ smoothly on a fixed patch, and all other data have the local bounds in the theorem. Any claimed failure of regular-point persistence or support convergence has a subsequence on which the failure persists. Pass further so that, on a slightly larger compact patch, $$\|g_i-g\|_{C^i}\le 2^{-i^2}.$$ Set $t_i=2^{-i}$ and take disjoint smooth bump functions $\beta_i(t)$, equal to one on a neighborhood of $t_i$, supported in intervals of width comparable to $t_i$, and satisfying $|\beta_i^{(b)}|\le C_b t_i^{-b}$. After omitting finitely many terms, $$g_t=g+\sum_i\beta_i(t)(g_i-g),\qquad G=\mathrm dt^2+g_t$$ is a smooth positive metric on a product neighborhood, with $g_0=g$ and $g_{t_i}=g_i$. For each pair of fixed orders $a,b$, the summands after $a$ spatial and $b$ temporal derivatives are bounded by $C_b2^{ib-i^2}$ for all sufficiently large $i$. These bounds, divided by any fixed power of $t_i$, still tend to zero. Thus all mixed derivatives extend at $t=0$. The plateaus also give $\partial_tg_t=0$ at $t_i$, so the slices carrying $g_i$ are totally geodesic in $G$.

Extend this *one* smooth metric $G$ across the edge of a smaller product patch to a closed manifold and isometrically embed it once. The images of the slices $t=t_i$ converge smoothly to the slice $t=0$, and the second fundamental form of this single embedding is bounded on their common compact neighborhood. The preceding first-variation calculation therefore gives one Euclidean mean-curvature bound for all the embedded perimeter varifolds. Strict perimeter convergence from <a href="#cut:bv-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:bv-compactness">39</a> becomes varifold convergence in this same Euclidean space. This construction proves the needed sequential uniformity; it does not assume that independently chosen isometric embeddings have uniform bounds.

*Exact minimizing tangent cones.* Next consider a blowup of one phase at $x\in S$. In smooth coordinates, at scale $\rho$, the normalized data are $$A_{x,\rho}=\rho^{-1}(A-x),\quad
 g_{x,\rho}(y)=g(x+\rho y),\quad
 \omega_{x,\rho}(y)=\omega(x+\rho y),\quad
 p_{x,\rho}(y)=\rho p(x+\rho y).$$ The metric here has been divided by the square of the physical scale: perimeter scales by $\rho^d$, volume by $\rho^{d+1}$, and this accounts for the factor $\rho$ in the pressure. On every fixed window the data converge smoothly to $g(x),\omega(x),0$. The strict compactness lemma therefore identifies every subsequential tangent varifold with the unit-multiplicity perimeter varifold of a phase minimizing perimeter in the constant metric $g(x)$. A constant weight can be divided out. After one linear isometry this is an ordinary Euclidean perimeter-minimizing phase. It is also a minimizing boundary current. Indeed, orient this Euclidean chart. A compactly supported integral current filling a boundary competitor has top dimension and is given by an integer-valued BV function $q$. The competitor is the boundary of $\chi_A+q$. Clipping that integer-valued function to $[0,1]$ leaves it unchanged outside the compact discrepancy and gives a characteristic function without increasing variation. Any current competitor with smaller mass would therefore give a smaller-perimeter set competitor. This verifies the precise minimizing-boundary hypothesis of Simon (2014, chap. 7, Theorem 5.8) <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[simonGMT](#ref-simonGMT)]. All orientations in this argument are local.

The support lower bound makes this limit nonzero at the origin. Monotonicity based at $x$ makes its mass ratio constant on all radii; the equality case, or the stationary radial identity in Simon (2014, chap. 4, Equation (3.6)) <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[simonGMT](#ref-simonGMT)], makes it conical. Equivalently, after a fixed local isometric embedding, dilations of the embedded ambient manifold converge smoothly to its tangent space, its second fundamental form is multiplied by $\rho$, and the same blowups give this cone. Its multiplicity is one as a perimeter varifold, although its density at the vertex need not be one.

*Regularity and persistence under convergence.* We now apply Allard’s graphical regularity theorem (Allard 1972) <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[allard1972](#ref-allard1972)]. In its Euclidean form used here, fix an exponent $q>d$. For a rectifiable $d$-varifold of multiplicity at least one, on a ball centered at a support point and contained in the first-variation domain, sufficiently small excess of the normalized mass above one and sufficiently small $$\rho^{\,1-d/q}
       \left(\int_{B_\rho}|\mathbf H|^q\,\mathrm d\mu_V\right)^{1/q}$$ give a single $C^{1,1-d/q}$ graph on a smaller ball (Simon 2014, chap. 5, Theorem 5.2) <a id="pm-cite-50"></a>[[simonGMT](#ref-simonGMT)]. A unit flat tangent supplies the small mass excess. The bounded mean curvature supplies the other small quantity, since it is $O(\rho)$ under the local area bound. Thus a flat tangent makes the original point regular as a $C^{1,\alpha}$ graph. The graph satisfies the weak prescribed-curvature equation $H_g=p-\partial_\nu\log\omega$; its coefficients and right-hand side are smooth functions of position, graph height, and graph gradient. The bounded slope gives uniform ellipticity. We spell out the passage from a weak graph to a smooth one. In a relatively compact chart write the equation as $$\partial_i A^i(x,u,Du)=B(x,u,Du).$$ Here $A$ is the gradient, in the slope variables, of the smooth positive weighted graph-area integrand. Thus $A_p$ is symmetric and uniformly positive on the bounded slope range; $B$ is smooth in its arguments. For a compactly supported cutoff $\eta$, test translated differences with $\eta^2\delta_hu$. The difference of $A$ has the form $a_hD\delta_hu+c_h\delta_hu+b_h$, where $a_h$ is uniformly elliptic and all three coefficients are uniformly bounded. Since $u$ is Lipschitz, $\delta_hu$ is bounded. On the right move the difference quotient from $B$ to the test function, using $\|\delta_{-h}\phi\|_2\le\|D\phi\|_2$. Ellipticity and Cauchy’s inequality then give $$\int\eta^2|D\delta_hu|^2\le C_\eta$$ independently of small $h$. Hence $u\in H^2_{\mathrm{loc}}$. For $v=\partial_ku$, weak differentiation now gives $$\partial_i(a^{ij}\partial_jv)=\partial_iF^i,\qquad
 a^{ij}=A^i_{p_j},\qquad
 F^i=\delta_{ik}B-A^i_{x_k}-A^i_uv,$$ with coefficients evaluated at $(x,u,Du)$. Both $a$ and $F$ are $C^{0,\alpha}$ because $u$ is $C^{1,\alpha}$.

To justify the divergence Schauder estimate for this weak $H^1$ solution, mollify $a,F$ on a slightly larger ball and solve the coercive linear Dirichlet problems on the ball with the same trace as $v$. Their solutions $v_\epsilon$ satisfy $$\lambda\|D(v_\epsilon-v)\|_2
 \le \|F_\epsilon-F\|_2
       +\|a_\epsilon-a\|_\infty\|Dv\|_2,$$ where $\lambda>0$ is a common ellipticity constant. Thus they converge in $H^1$. Smooth-coefficient Sobolev regularity makes each approximant smooth in the interior; the local $L^2$–$L^\infty$ estimate and then the a priori divergence Schauder estimate give uniform interior $C^{1,\alpha}$ bounds (Simon 2015, Lecture 6, Theorem 1 and Corollary 1; Lecture 18, Theorem 2; Lecture 12, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-51"></a>[[simonPDE](#ref-simonPDE)]. These bounds use only the common ellipticity and Hölder bounds, not higher derivatives of the mollified coefficients. Passing to the limit gives $v\in C^{1,\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}$, hence $u\in C^{2,\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}$. Repeating the difference-quotient and weak Schauder argument for differentiated equations makes the graph smooth. The estimates are uniform for families with common $C^{1,\alpha}$ graph bounds and uniformly smooth geometric data.

Likewise, near a regular point of a limiting phase choose a sufficiently small ball with mass ratio as close to one as needed, with strict slack in the Allard threshold, and with small scaled mean-curvature bound.
