Choose its radius also so that its sphere has zero limiting perimeter measure; almost every sufficiently small radius has this property. For smoothly convergent data, use the single ambient embedding construction above. Weak perimeter-measure convergence now passes the mass bound on this ball to the approximating phases. To center at a nearby approximating support point, decrease the radius by a fixed arbitrarily small factor: the recentered ball lies in the chosen ball for all large indices, and the strict slack pays the change in the normalizing power of the radius. Allard therefore gives one graph in each smaller ball for all sufficiently large indices. The graph equation and smooth data convergence then give smooth convergence on smaller compact patches. This also proves $$x_i\in\Sigma_i,\quad x_i\longrightarrow x
                    \quad\Longrightarrow\quad x\in\Sigma$$ for interior limits: a regular limiting point has just been excluded, and a point outside the limiting support is excluded by the uniform support lower bound and perimeter convergence. The same lower bound and weak convergence prove local support convergence; they also exclude stray support in compact subsets of phase interiors. The regular set is open in the support, so its complement $\Sigma$ is relatively closed.

*Transfer of the singular-dimension bound.* For the singular-dimension transfer we use the content argument in Simon (2014, chap. 1, Theorem 3.26(2), and Appendix A, proof of Theorem A.4, step (1)) <a id="pm-cite-52"></a>[[simonGMT](#ref-simonGMT)]. Its hypotheses in the present application have all been established: on every compact coordinate patch and at every center, each vanishing scale sequence has a strict tangent subsequence; these tangents are homogeneous minimizing boundaries; singular sets commute exactly with translations and dilations; and, on compact windows of every such convergent sequence, singular sets eventually lie in every open neighborhood of the limiting singular set. For the last assertion, a contrary sequence of points has a convergent subsequence, contradicting regular-point persistence or support convergence. The data on the rescaled windows have uniform local derivative bounds, with derivatives of order $j$ acquiring factors $\rho^j$ and pressure derivatives acquiring $\rho^{j+1}$. Thus the compactness and regularity argument applies to all these sequences. We use only the cited content-transfer step, and spell it out next; no identification with the scalar-function family of Appendix A is needed.

Suppose $m\ge8$ and, for some $s>m-8$, the singular set in an interior window had positive $\mathcal H^s$ measure. Let $\mathcal H^s_\infty$ denote Hausdorff content, defined by covering with balls and summing their radii to the power $s$; normalization constants are immaterial. The Hausdorff-content density selection in the proof of Theorem A.4 provides an interior $x\in\Sigma$, scales $\rho_i\downarrow0$, and $c>0$ such that $$\rho_i^{-s}\mathcal H^s_\infty
               (\Sigma\cap\overline B_{\rho_i}(x))\ge c.$$ Pass to a strict tangent limit $C$ along these scales. The exact minimizing-boundary theorem gives $\mathcal H^s(\Sigma_C)=0$, because its hypersurface dimension is $d=m-1$ and its bound is $d-7=m-8$. Cover $\Sigma_C\cap\overline B_{3/2}$ by finitely many open balls whose sum of $s$th powers of radii is less than $c/2$; the cover can first be chosen countable with this bound and then reduced to a finite subcover by compactness. Singular-set persistence puts every $\rho_i^{-1}(\Sigma-x)\cap\overline B_1$ in that same union for large $i$: otherwise a convergent sequence in its compact complement would give a singular limiting point outside the cover. Scaling of Hausdorff content now contradicts the preceding lower bound. This proves $\mathcal H^s(\Sigma)=0$ for every $s>m-8$. It is the density selection and this finite-cover passage, in addition to tangent information, that transfer the dimension bound.

If $m\le7$, the exact minimizing-boundary theorem says that every tangent cone is regular at its vertex and hence is a hyperplane. Flat-tangent regularity then makes $\Sigma$ empty. Finally a nonflat minimizing cone is singular at its vertex, and translation invariance carries that vertex to each point of its spine. The spine is therefore contained in its singular set, giving the asserted spine bound. The preceding monotonicity argument, applied in the common embeddings for convergent data, also gives upper semicontinuity of densities at moving points by first passing to the limit at a fixed radius and then letting the radius decrease to zero. The smooth graph’s coorientation comes from the phase, and its first-variation equation is <a href="#cut:base-mean-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:base-mean-curvature">[cut:base-mean-curvature]</a>. All constructions are local in the base and require no ambient orientability or torus-dimensional regularity theorem. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cut-end-convergence"></a>
<div id="cut:end-convergence" class="corollary">

**Corollary 43** (True cuts converge at packet ends). *For the common generic shift, let $A_E$ be the true phase on each packet and let $A_B$ be that on a face. On translated windows escaping in a product collar over a compact part of $B$, the phases $A_E$ converge to $A_B\times\mathbb R^d$ in $L^1_{\rm loc}$, their perimeter measures and varifolds converge, and their regular boundaries converge smoothly on compact sets off the limiting singular set. Their boundary supports converge locally as well. The same conclusions hold after passage to any deeper product furnished by <a href="#packet:escape" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:escape">33</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cut-end-convergence"><summary>Proof of stmt-cut-end-convergence</summary>

*Proof.* The metric, weights, and shifted pressure in the translated expanding windows are exactly the data pulled from $B$. Local compactness produces a subsequential limiting phase on $B\times\mathbb R^d$, and it is an absolute minimizer by <a href="#cut:bv-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:bv-compactness">39</a>. The uniqueness and factorization of <a href="#cut:generic-uniqueness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:generic-uniqueness">41</a> identify it with $A_B\times\mathbb R^d$. Every subsequence has this same possible limit, which proves convergence of the full sequence. Apply <a href="#cut:gmt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:gmt">42</a> for the support and smooth assertions. The deeper-face statement follows by using the commuting product coordinates in <a href="#packet:escape" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:escape">33</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Stability on a wall and the retained-side estimate

We now choose the representative of each phase which is open away from $S$ and agrees near $S_{\rm reg}$ with the corresponding smooth closed side when a trace is required. Write $S=S_{\rm reg}$ in integrals below. Every test has compact support away from $\Sigma$; joined-side tests also avoid the cylinder over $\Sigma$. The old torus variables remain invariant, and $\mathrm d\mu_S=\omega\,\mathrm d\mathcal H_g^{m-1}$ is the wall measure. The intrinsic scalar curvature $R_S$ and the second fundamental form $B$ refer to $S\times\mathbb T^q$ with its induced old stabilized metric. Its torus principal curvature in direction $i$ is $\partial_\nu\log f_i$, so <a href="#cut:base-mean-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:base-mean-curvature">[cut:base-mean-curvature]</a> gives $$H=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}B=H_g+\partial_\nu\log\omega=p$$ for the outward normal of the plus phase. The invariant wall Laplacian is $\omega^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_S(\omega\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}_S\,\cdot\,)$, so its integration by parts uses precisely $\mathrm d\mu_S$.

<a id="stmt-cut-wall-stability"></a>
<div id="cut:wall-stability" class="lemma">

<a id="cut:wall-form"></a>
**Lemma 44** (The wall stability form). *For every compactly supported smooth invariant test $v$ on $S$, $$\label{cut:wall-form}
 \int_S\bigl(2|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v|^2+R_Sv^2\bigr)\,\mathrm d\mu_S
 \ge\int_S\bigl(R+p^2+|B|^2+2\partial_\nu p\bigr)v^2\,\mathrm d\mu_S.$$ In particular its right side is at least $\int_S(R-1.02\Pi_{j_0})v^2\,\mathrm d\mu_S$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cut-wall-stability"><summary>Proof of stmt-cut-wall-stability</summary>

*Proof.* Weighted perimeter is the area of the old torus product for invariant phases. The invariant normal variation with speed $v$ is compactly supported in the smooth regular part and comes from an admissible base variation. Thus its energy has a minimum at parameter zero. For completeness the variation identities hold in every dimension: differentiating the induced metric gives $\dot g_{ab}=2vB_{ab}$, differentiating the unit normal gives $\dot\nu=-\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}_Sv$, and commuting the two ambient covariant derivatives in the derivative of $B_{ab}$ gives, after tracing and differentiating the inverse metric, $$\dot H=-\mathop{\mathrm{\Delta}}_S v-(\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}_{\widehat g}(\nu,\nu)+|B|^2)v.$$ The area derivative is $Hv\,\mathrm d\mu_S$, while the pressure-volume variation is $pv\,\mathrm d\mu_S$. The first energy derivative is therefore $\int_S(H-p)v\,\mathrm d\mu_S$. At $H=p$ the derivatives of the area measure and of the chosen speed multiply zero, and $\dot p=v\partial_\nu p$. Hence the nonnegative second derivative is $$\int_S\left[-v\mathop{\mathrm{\Delta}}_Sv-
 (\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}_{\widehat g}(\nu,\nu)+|B|^2+\partial_\nu p)v^2\right]
                         \,\mathrm d\mu_S.$$ Weighted integration by parts gives $$\int_S|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v|^2\,\mathrm d\mu_S
 \ge\int_S(\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}_{\widehat g}(\nu,\nu)+|B|^2+
                           \partial_\nu p)v^2\,\mathrm d\mu_S.$$ Compare the first variation in Chodosh and Li (2024, Lemma 13) <a id="pm-cite-53"></a>[[chodoshLi](#ref-chodoshLi)] and the second-variation calculation in the proof of its Lemma 14. The displayed calculation proves the identity in the present stabilized setting; the stability and Gauss-equation mechanism is that of Schoen and Yau (1979, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-54"></a>[[schoenYau1979Structure](#ref-schoenYau1979Structure)]. The derivation here uses neither Chodosh and Li’s dimension restriction for smooth existence nor a regularity assertion for the torus product. Only the already smooth base patch and its invariant variations enter. The Gauss identity $$R_S=R-2\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}_{\widehat g}(\nu,\nu)+H^2-|B|^2$$ gives <a href="#cut:wall-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:wall-form">[cut:wall-form]</a>. By <a href="#cut:pressure-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:pressure-gradient">[cut:pressure-gradient]</a>, $$p^2+2\partial_\nu p
       \ge p^2-2|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}p|
       \ge .33p^2-1.02\Pi_{j_0},$$ which proves the final statement. ◻

</details>

For either phase side $\Omega$, put $p_s=p$ on the plus side and $p_s=-p$ on the minus side. The side is itself an absolute minimizing phase for the signed pressure $p_s$, and its outward stabilized mean curvature is $H_{\rm out}=p_s$.

<a id="stmt-cut-side-estimate"></a>
<div id="cut:side-estimate" class="lemma">

<a id="cut:deletion"></a>
<a id="cut:side-form"></a>
**Lemma 45** (Deletion and the seam estimate). *Every nonnegative compactly supported smooth function $f$ on the closed regular side, with support avoiding the singular set, satisfies $$\label{cut:deletion}
       \int_S f\,\mathrm d\mu_S
       \le\int_\Omega\bigl(|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}f|+p_sf\bigr)\,\mathrm d\mu.$$ For every smooth real test $v$ of the same type, $$\label{cut:side-form}
 3.05\int_\Omega|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v|^2\,\mathrm d\mu
       +2\int_S H_{\rm out}v^2\,\mathrm d\mu_S
       \ge-1.02\int_\Omega\Pi_{j_0}v^2\,\mathrm d\mu.$$ Both statements extend to compactly supported Lipschitz tests by approximation.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cut-side-estimate"><summary>Proof of stmt-cut-side-estimate</summary>

*Proof.* For almost every $t>0$, delete $\Omega\cap\{f>t\}$ from the minimizing phase. The removed boundary area is the area on $S\cap\{f>t\}$; the newly introduced boundary is the level surface of $f$ in the interior of $\Omega$. The pressure-volume term increases by $\int_{\Omega\cap\{f>t\}}p_s\,\mathrm d\mu$. Minimality gives the corresponding perimeter inequality. Integrating in $t$, the layer-cake formula and weighted coarea give <a href="#cut:deletion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:deletion">[cut:deletion]</a>. All comparisons are compact and occur on regular patches; the perimeter slicing formula justifies the same argument at exceptional critical levels by discarding a null set of levels.

Apply <a href="#cut:deletion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:deletion">[cut:deletion]</a> to $f=2(-p_s)_+v^2$, initially by Lipschitz approximation. Its right side is bounded above by $$\int_{\Omega\cap\{p_s<0\}}
       \bigl[(2|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}p_s|-2p_s^2)v^2
                         +4|p_s|\,|v|\,|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v|\bigr]\,\mathrm d\mu.$$ Young’s inequality gives $$4|p_s|\,|v|\,|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v|
        \le3.05|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v|^2+\frac4{3.05}p_s^2v^2.$$ The pressure estimate holds unchanged for $p_s$, and <a id="cut:young-margin"></a>$$\label{cut:young-margin}
                2-\frac4{3.05}-.67=\frac{113}{6100}>0.$$ Discard the resulting nonpositive multiple of $p_s^2v^2$. Thus $$2\int_S(-p_s)_+v^2\,\mathrm d\mu_S
 \le3.05\int_\Omega|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v|^2\,\mathrm d\mu
                  +1.02\int_\Omega\Pi_{j_0}v^2\,\mathrm d\mu.$$ Since $2p_s\ge-2(-p_s)_+$ on the boundary and $H_{\rm out}=p_s$, this is <a href="#cut:side-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:side-form">[cut:side-form]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### Raw joined forms and the numerical reserve

Attach $S\times[0,\infty)_t$ to the regular boundary of either side, freezing all induced metric and old warp data on the cylinder. This is a raw joined space: it is smooth on its two pieces, and the seam retains its scalar corner contribution. An admissible test is a pair $v=(v_b,v_c)$, smooth separately up to regular seam patches, compactly supported away from singularities and their cylinders, satisfying $v_b|_S=v_c|_{t=0}$. Compact Lipschitz versions are allowed by local approximation in regular seam charts. For the wall alone the tests are compact smooth functions on $S$ away from $\Sigma$.

<a id="cut:raw-form"></a>
Set $C=3.99$. On a wall define $$Q_S(v)=\int_S(C|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v|^2+R_Sv^2)\,\mathrm d\mu_S.$$ On a joined side define $$\begin{aligned}
\label{cut:raw-form}
 Q_\Omega(v)
  ={}&\int_\Omega(C|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v_b|^2+Rv_b^2)\,\mathrm d\mu
       +2\int_S H_{\rm out}v_b^2\,\mathrm d\mu_S\notag\\
    &+\int_0^\infty\!\int_S
        \bigl(C(|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}_Sv_c|^2+|\partial_tv_c|^2)
                         +R_Sv_c^2\bigr)\,\mathrm d\mu_S\,\mathrm dt.
\end{aligned}$$ The sign in the seam term can also be checked directly: in outward arclength $s$ the mean curvature changes from $H_{\rm out}$ on the bulk side to zero on the frozen cylinder, and the scalar expression contains $-2\partial_sH$. Its distributional contribution is $+2H_{\rm out}$ at the seam. The smooth realization and its form convergence will be established in <a href="#sec:regularization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:regularization">13</a>.

<a id="stmt-cut-raw-domination"></a>
<div id="cut:raw-domination" class="proposition">

<a id="cut:side-domination"></a>
**Proposition 46** (Raw scalar domination). *Let $V=R-1.02\Pi_{j_0}$, restricted to the wall and pulled back to the cylinder when necessary. Then <a id="cut:wall-domination"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
\label{cut:wall-domination}
 Q_S(v)&\ge\int_S Vv^2\,\mathrm d\mu_S
                    +1.99\int_S|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v|^2\,\mathrm d\mu_S,\\
 Q_\Omega(v)&\ge\int_\Omega Vv_b^2\,\mathrm d\mu
       +\int_0^\infty\!\int_S Vv_c^2\,\mathrm d\mu_S\,\mathrm dt\notag\\
 &\quad+.94\int_\Omega|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v_b|^2\,\mathrm d\mu
       +1.99\int_0^\infty\!\int_S|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v_c|^2
                                      \,\mathrm d\mu_S\,\mathrm dt.
                                      \label{cut:side-domination}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v_c|^2=|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}_Sv_c|^2+|\partial_tv_c|^2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cut-raw-domination"><summary>Proof of stmt-cut-raw-domination</summary>

*Proof.* The wall assertion follows by adding $(C-2)|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v|^2$ to <a href="#cut:wall-stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:wall-stability">44</a>. For the bulk use <a href="#cut:side-estimate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:side-estimate">45</a>; the remaining gradient coefficient is $C-3.05=.94$. Apply the wall assertion to every cylinder slice and integrate in $t$. The horizontal coefficient is $C-2=1.99$, while the vertical coefficient is still $C=3.99$, so lowering the latter to $1.99$ gives <a href="#cut:side-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:side-domination">[cut:side-domination]</a>. The seam trace in the bulk estimate is precisely the matching trace in <a href="#cut:raw-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:raw-form">[cut:raw-form]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cut-reserve"></a>
<div id="cut:reserve" class="definition">

**Definition 47** (Reserved form and test completion). For the wall or a joined side define, respectively, $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal N_S(v)&=1.7\int_S|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v|^2\,\mathrm d\mu_S,\\
 \mathcal N_\Omega(v)
   &=.8\int_\Omega|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v_b|^2\,\mathrm d\mu
       +1.7\int_0^\infty\!\int_S|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v_c|^2
                                            \,\mathrm d\mu_S\,\mathrm dt.
\end{aligned}$$ We write $\mathcal N$ when the tested space is understood. The squared norm $\|v\|_2^2$ is the sum of the corresponding piecewise weighted integrals. With $$e_0=(\lambda_r-\lambda_{r+1})/100>0,
 \qquad \mathfrak h(v)=\mathcal N(v)+e_0\|v\|_2^2,$$ the test completion means completion of the admissible compact tests just specified in the norm $\mathfrak h^{1/2}$. In particular its seam condition is matching traces, with no zero trace imposed there.

</div>

<a id="cut:localized-potential"></a>
The coefficients have a reserve even after the later shielding charge $.9\mathfrak h$. Specifically, <a id="cut:remaining-gradient"></a>$$\label{cut:remaining-gradient}
                       .94-.9(.8)=.22,
             \qquad 1.99-.9(1.7)=.46.$$ The potential term in <a href="#cut:raw-domination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:raw-domination">46</a>, combined with the parent inequality, satisfies $$\label{cut:localized-potential}
 R-1.02\Pi_{j_0}
 \ge\lambda_r+b_r\sum_{j\ne j_0}\Pi_j
                         +(b_r-1.02)\Pi_{j_0}
                       \qquad\hbox{on }D_E.$$ Here $b_r\ge1.07>1.02$. The raw form inequalities themselves hold for all admissible compact tests; the replacement of their potential by the right side of <a href="#cut:localized-potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:localized-potential">[cut:localized-potential]</a> is permitted only where the test is supported over the old required region. The count inclusions of <a href="#packet:counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:counts">18</a> supply exactly this localization for the later required regions. These are the forms and constants used in shielding and regularization. The minimizing cut, its pressure, and the old weight remain fixed while the other profiles and their cores are changed.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
### Local shielding of singular sets

Shielding makes each singular point irrelevant to the next curvature requirement by enlarging enough remaining pole cores near that point. The forcing costs a potential concentrated in a thin annulus. We first pay for this potential from the reserved gradient form; the next appendix makes the choices compatible throughout the diagram.

We work at one fixed stage with a finite set of remaining labels. The countable extension is proved in <a href="#countable:step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="countable:step">24</a>. The local estimates use only $.60\le a_r\le .63$ and the positive reserve of <a href="#cut:reserve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:reserve">47</a>. Smooth geometric bounds are taken on the specified compact windows, so no bounded-geometry assumption on the original manifold is involved.

<a id="local:norm"></a>
Fix a true cut in a parent packet $E$ of base dimension $m$, as in <a href="#cut:gmt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:gmt">42</a>. Its boundary support is $S$, its singular set is $\Sigma$, and its regular part is $S_{\rm reg}$. The old torus volume factor is $\omega$. All measures below include this factor; on a cylinder it is pulled back from $S_{\rm reg}$. For either phase $\Omega$, write $$J_\Omega=\overline\Omega_{\rm reg}\mathbin{\cup}_{S_{\rm reg}}
             (S_{\rm reg}\times[0,\infty)).$$ Here the notation retains only regular boundary points when forming the join. Tests on $J_\Omega$ are compactly supported, piecewise smooth functions with matching values at the seam, whose supports avoid $\Sigma$ and the cylinders above it. Lipschitz tests with the same properties follow by approximation in regular charts. Tests on the wall have the analogous meaning. Use the reserved energy from <a href="#cut:reserve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:reserve">47</a>, namely $$\label{local:norm}
 \mathcal N(v)=
 \begin{cases}
  1.7\displaystyle\int_{S_{\rm reg}}|\nabla v|^2,&\text{on the wall},\\[3pt]
  .8\displaystyle\int_\Omega|\nabla v|^2+
  1.7\displaystyle\int_{S_{\rm reg}\times[0,\infty)}|\nabla v|^2,
       &\text{on a joined side}.
 \end{cases}$$ The notation $\|v\|_2^2$ denotes the sum of the corresponding weighted $L^2$ integrals. An ambient function is restricted to the wall and frozen in the cylinder coordinate. In particular, these conventions specify every potential integral in this section. The true cut remains fixed throughout: only profiles of labels other than the processed label $j_0$ will change.

<a id="stmt-local-anchors"></a>
<div id="local:anchors" class="lemma">

**Lemma 48** (Choice of anchors). *There are compatible smooth functions $h_j$, for the remaining labels, within $.1$ of $\max(-1,\min(1,\mu_j))$, and common numbers $t_j\in(-.3,.3)$ such that, on the singular set in each parent of dimension $m$, at most $m-8$ of the equalities $h_j=t_j$ hold. The assertion is vacuous in dimensions below eight. Moreover $$\{h_j>t_j\}\cap\{\mu_j=-\infty\}=\varnothing,
 \qquad
 \{h_j<t_j\}\cap\{\mu_j=+\infty\}=\varnothing.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-local-anchors"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-anchors</summary>

*Proof.* Compatible smoothing by <a href="#packet:approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:approximation">34</a> gives the stated approximations: smooth the continuous clipped functions on compact parts and blend with their already prepared exact face copies. Allocate the error $.1$ beforehand over the finitely many requests on each face, and reproduce constants exactly on passive copies. For a packet of dimension $m\ge8$ and a set $I$ of $m-7$ remaining labels, the smooth map $h_I=(h_j)_{j\in I}$ is locally Lipschitz. Since $\dim_{\rm H}\Sigma\le m-8$, its image of $\Sigma$ has zero $(m-7)$-dimensional Lebesgue measure. This follows on each relatively compact chart from the defining Hausdorff covers and then on the packet by a countable union. The set of threshold vectors for which $t_I\in h_I(\Sigma)$ is null by Fubini. For measurability, intersect $\Sigma$ with a compact exhaustion first: each image is compact, and their countable union is measurable. Give each interval $(-.3,.3)$ its normalized Lebesgue measure. There are countably many packets and finitely many relevant subsets per packet, so choose a threshold vector outside the union of all exclusions. The same argument also works in a countable product of the threshold intervals: each exclusion is a null cylinder set depending on only $m-7$ coordinates. If fewer than $m-7$ labels remain, the asserted count holds without a condition. At a negative pole $h_j\le-.9<t_j$, and at a positive pole $h_j\ge.9>t_j$, proving the last assertions. ◻

</details>

The guard $H_E^*$ in <a href="#packet:guard" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="packet:guard">[packet:guard]</a> requires at least $m-2$ remaining undecided labels. At $x\in\Sigma$, enlarging the positive core for every label with $h_j(x)>t_j$ and the negative core for every label with $h_j(x)<t_j$ to contain $x$ would leave at most $m-8<m-2$ labels undecided. These pointwise decisions would therefore remove $x$ from the guard.

We need quantitative pieces of $\Sigma$, rather than just its Hausdorff dimension. A coordinate chart in the following lemma is chosen with compact buffer and with its metric as close to Euclidean as required. Such charts can be chosen around every point. The plane condition is expressed in Euclidean chart coordinates. Replacing Euclidean balls by metric balls changes only fixed factors, which can be absorbed by requesting a smaller flatness constant.

<a id="stmt-local-flat-pieces"></a>
<div id="local:flat-pieces" class="lemma">

<a id="local:flatness"></a>
**Lemma 49** (Compact pieces with one-sided flatness). *Let $O$ be an open subset of a parent packet and let $\eta>0$. The set $\Sigma\cap O$ is covered by countably many compact sets $F\subset\Sigma\cap O$, each contained in a buffered coordinate chart, with the following property. There is $r_F>0$ such that, for every $x\in F$ and $0<r<r_F$, some affine $(m-8)$-plane $L_{x,r}$ satisfies $$\label{local:flatness}
 F\cap B_r(x)\subset\{z:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_{\rm Eucl}(z,L_{x,r})<\eta r\}.$$ The plane may depend on both the center and the radius.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-local-flat-pieces"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-flat-pieces</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\Theta(x)$ for the unweighted boundary density. First consider a tangent minimizing boundary cone $C$ at a singular point. It is nonflat, since a flat tangent gives regularity. For any $z\in C$, the density ratio centered at $z$ has limit $\Theta_C(0)$ as its radius tends to infinity: balls centered at $z$ are sandwiched between balls centered at zero whose radii differ by $|z|$. Consequently $$\Theta_C(z)\ge\Theta_C(0)
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \text{every density ratio centered at $z$ equals $\Theta_C(0)$}.$$ Equality in monotonicity says that $C$ is also a cone centered at $z$. Composing a dilation about $z$ with the inverse dilation about zero shows translation invariance along $z$. Conversely, translation invariance along $z$ gives equality of the two densities. The translation invariance directions form a vector subspace, the spine. Every spine point is singular: otherwise translation gives regularity at the vertex, and the cone is a plane. The minimizing-cone singular dimension estimate therefore bounds the spine dimension by $m-8$. These tangent-cone and regularity inputs are precisely those in <a href="#cut:gmt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:gmt">42</a>.

Fix a singular point $x$ and a small number $\eta'>0$. There are $d_x>0$ and $r_x>0$ such that, at every radius $r<r_x$, the points <a id="local:high-density"></a>$$\label{local:high-density}
 \{z\in\Sigma\cap B_r(x):\Theta(z)\ge\Theta(x)-d_x\}$$ lie within $\eta'r$ of some $(m-8)$-plane through $x$. If not, choose failing radii $r_i\downarrow0$ with density tolerances tending to zero. Rescale at $x$ and pass to a tangent cone $C$. Fix a plane containing its spine. Failure for this particular plane gives points $z_i$ in the rescaled unit ball a fixed positive distance from it. A convergent subsequence has limit $z$. Upper semicontinuity of densities under these convergent blowups gives $\Theta_C(z)\ge\Theta(x)=\Theta_C(0)$. The preceding paragraph puts $z$ in the spine, a contradiction. Smooth metric errors and bounded mean-curvature errors vanish in this rescaling.

Here is the countable decomposition, including its closure step. Group singular points according to lower bounds $r_x\ge1/a$ and $d_x\ge1/b$, with positive integers $a,b$, and according to a density interval of length less than $1/(2b)$. Any two points $x,z$ in the same group satisfy $\Theta(z)>\Theta(x)-d_x$. Thus the group itself has the plane containment at every one of its centers and every radius less than $1/a$. Work within one buffered chart at a time. Intersect these groups with a countable family of compact subpatches $Q\Subset O$ exhausting that chart’s portion of $O$, and take $F=\overline{Q\cap\text{group}}$. Relative closedness of $\Sigma$ and the compact buffer ensure $F\subset\Sigma\cap O$. These compact sets still cover $\Sigma\cap O$.

To check the geometric condition at a closure center $x$, let $x_i$ be group points tending to $x$ and fix $r<1/(3a)$. Apply the group estimate in $B_{2r}(x_i)$. The planes pass through $x_i$, so a subsequence converges to a plane through $x$. Every group point in $B_r(x)$ is in these larger balls eventually. Passing first to the limit of the planes and then to closure points gives containment with width $2\eta'r$. Taking, for example, $\eta'<\eta/3$ proves <a href="#local:flatness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:flatness">[local:flatness]</a>; choose $r_F$ below $1/(3a)$ and the buffered chart radius. This argument transfers the containment itself; no density continuity at a closure point is required. ◻

</details>

The next construction fixes the pressure and potential to be estimated. Distances denoted by $\delta$ from now on are ambient base distances.

<a id="stmt-local-shell"></a>
<div id="local:shell" class="lemma">

<a id="local:shell-gradient"></a>
<a id="local:shell-potential"></a>
**Lemma 50** (A shell forcing profile). *Let $F$ be a compact subset of $\Sigma\cap\{h_j>t_j\}$. For every sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$ there is an extended-real profile $u_\varepsilon$, equal to $+\infty$ for $\delta\le\varepsilon$ and $-\infty$ for $\delta\ge R_\varepsilon$, where $$\delta(x)=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,F),\qquad R_\varepsilon=e^{5\pi}\varepsilon,$$ whose finite truncations are locally Lipschitz and which satisfies $$\label{local:shell-gradient}
 2|\nabla u_\varepsilon|
       \le .58u_\varepsilon^2+2\delta^{-2}
       \quad (\varepsilon<\delta<R_\varepsilon).$$ There is a smooth nonnegative ambient function $D_\varepsilon$ with $$\label{local:shell-potential}
 \begin{aligned}
 D_\varepsilon&\ge2\delta^{-2}
       &&\text{on }\{\varepsilon\le\delta\le R_\varepsilon\},\\
 D_\varepsilon&\le2.12\delta^{-2}
       &&\text{everywhere off }F,\\
 \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}D_\varepsilon&\subset
       \{\varepsilon/2<\delta<2R_\varepsilon\}
       \Subset\{h_j>t_j\}.
 \end{aligned}$$ Replacing $\mu_j$ by $\max(\mu_j,u_\varepsilon)$, adding $D_\varepsilon$ to $\Pi_j$, and adding $\{\delta<\varepsilon/2\}$ to $U_j^+$ preserves the profile inequality with coefficient $a_r$. There is an identical negative construction in $\{h_j<t_j\}$, using $\min(\mu_j,-u_\varepsilon)$ and enlarging $U_j^-$. All supports and newly forced regions are compactly contained in the stated strict anchor region.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-local-shell"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-shell</summary>

*Proof.* The polynomial $q(y)=.29y^2-y+1$ is strictly positive, and $$\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{dy}{q(y)}=5\pi.$$ Let $y:(0,5\pi)\longrightarrow\mathbb R$ solve $y'=q(y)$, with the two infinite endpoint limits, and put $$u_\varepsilon(x)=\frac{y(\log(R_\varepsilon/\delta(x)))}{\delta(x)}.$$ At differentiability points of $\delta$, its gradient has norm at most one. Differentiating in $\delta$ gives $$\left|\frac{d}{d\delta}\frac{y(\log(R_\varepsilon/\delta))}{\delta}\right|
   =\frac{y'+y}{\delta^2}
   =.29u_\varepsilon^2+\delta^{-2},$$ which proves <a href="#local:shell-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:shell-gradient">[local:shell-gradient]</a>. Extending by the stated poles is continuous in the extended real line. Each finite clipping has a finite Lipschitz constant locally, including across either clipping interface.

Choose a continuous cutoff of $\delta$, equal to one on $[\varepsilon,R_\varepsilon]$ and supported strictly in $(\varepsilon/2,2R_\varepsilon)$. Multiplying it by $2.06\delta^{-2}$ gives a continuous nonnegative function supported away from $F$. Smooth it by nonnegative local convolutions and a partition of unity, with sufficiently small value error on this compact annulus and with support retained in a slightly larger annulus of the indicated size. The positive margins $.06$ on the central annulus and $.06$ below the upper bound give <a href="#local:shell-potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:shell-potential">[local:shell-potential]</a>. The annuli lie in the strict anchor region for all small $\varepsilon$, by compactness of $F$.

At almost every point where the new maximum is finite, its gradient is the gradient of an active branch. The old branch obeys the old inequality. The new one obeys it because $.58<a_r$ and $D_\varepsilon\ge2\delta^{-2}$ wherever that branch is finite. The same reasoning applies at branch equality, using the elementary weak derivative rule for maxima of locally Lipschitz functions. The new core has closure inside the open positive pole region $\{\delta<\varepsilon\}$. By <a href="#local:anchors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:anchors">48</a> no old negative pole occurs in the support of the positive forcing. Positive and negative additions for the same label have disjoint strict anchor regions, so repetitions introduce no conflict. Indeed any opposite pole introduced later lies in the opposite strict anchor region and therefore cannot meet this support. Original poles and previously introduced opposite cores consequently remain protected throughout. Replacing the profile by its negative proves the other assertion. ◻

</details>

We now prove a Hardy estimate with a positive, dimension-dependent coefficient. Its coefficient must be chosen before the more accurate flatness used later.

<a id="stmt-local-rough"></a>
<div id="local:rough" class="lemma">

<a id="local:rough-estimate"></a>
**Lemma 51** (Uniform rough Hardy coefficient). *For each $m\ge8$ there are numbers $\eta_0>0$ and $c_0>0$ with the following property. Suppose $F$ satisfies <a href="#local:flatness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:flatness">[local:flatness]</a> with $\eta\le\eta_0$ in a sufficiently accurate buffered chart. There are $d_F>0$ and $C_F<\infty$ such that, on the wall and on either joined side, $$\label{local:rough-estimate}
 c_0\int_{\delta<d_F}\delta^{-2}v^2
             \le\mathcal N(v)+C_F\|v\|_2^2.$$ The constants $\eta_0,c_0$ depend only on $m$ and a fixed permitted chart distortion. The constants $d_F,C_F$ may depend on the compact piece, its scale threshold, and the local smooth metric, weight, and pressure. No principal-curvature bound for the wall is assumed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-local-rough"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-rough</summary>

*Proof.* Put $s=m-8$. At scale $r$, the one-sided plane condition covers $F\cap B_r(x)$ by at most $C_m\lambda^{-s}$ balls of radius $\lambda r$, with centers in $F$, where $\lambda=8\eta_0$ can be made as small as desired. To see the bound, take a maximal $\lambda r$-separated family in the set. Projection to the approximating $s$-plane changes pairwise distances by at most $2\eta_0r$; the projections are separated by a fixed multiple of $\lambda r$ in an $s$-ball of radius $2r$. Its elementary Euclidean packing bound gives the claim, with harmless changes in $C_m$ and the covering radius. Absorb these fixed changes into the initial choice of $\lambda$. Choose $\eta_0$ so small that the resulting covering number is at most $\lambda^{-(s+1/2)}$.

<a id="local:density-integral"></a>
Iterating the same covering at centers in $F$ gives, for $0<t\le d$ below a fixed fraction of $r_F$, a covering by at most $C(d/t)^{m-7.5}$ balls of radius $t$ of the part of $F$ controlling a ball of radius $d$. Here one first centers a ball of radius a fixed multiple of $d$ at a nearby point of $F$ and then iterates; the constants use only the fixed rough choice. Enlarging the final balls by a fixed factor proves the tubular-volume estimate <a id="local:tube-volume"></a>$$\label{local:tube-volume}
 |B_d(x)\cap\{\delta<t\}|
       \le C d^{m-7.5}t^{7.5},\qquad 0<t\le d,$$ whenever $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,F)\le d$ and the slightly enlarged ball is in the chart at allowed scales. Metric and Euclidean distances are uniformly comparable here. Dyadically splitting the regions $2^{-k-1}d<\delta\le2^{-k}d$, and treating $\delta>d$ separately, gives $$\label{local:density-integral}
 \int_{B_d(x)}\delta^{-5.5}\,dx\le C d^{m-5.5}.$$ Indeed the sum of the bounds is a geometric series with ratio $2^{-2}$. In particular this density is locally integrable.

<a id="local:rough-U-bounds"></a>
Choose a nonnegative smooth chart cutoff $\chi$, equal to one on a neighborhood of $F$, and, using Euclidean coordinates, define <a id="local:rough-potential"></a>$$\label{local:rough-potential}
 U(x)=1+\int\chi(y)\delta(y)^{-5.5}|x-y|^{-(m-4)}\,dy.$$ For small $\delta(x)>0$, the part $|x-y|<c\delta(x)$ has $\delta(y)$ comparable to $\delta(x)$; it supplies the lower bound for $U$. The same region supplies the upper bounds for $U$ and its first derivatives. On the successive larger annuli use <a href="#local:density-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:density-integral">[local:density-integral]</a>; their contributions have orders $(2^k\delta)^{-1.5}$ and $(2^k\delta)^{-2.5}$ respectively. The parts above the allowed local scale are bounded constants depending on $F$. Shrinking the final distance threshold absorbs them, and gives dimension-dependent constants $c,C>0$ such that $$\label{local:rough-U-bounds}
 c\delta^{-1.5}\le U\le C\delta^{-1.5},
 \qquad |\nabla U|\le C\delta^{-2.5}.$$

<a id="local:U-superharmonic"></a>
Set $a=m-4$ temporarily. For any Euclidean $d'$-plane $T$, direct differentiation of $K(z)=|z|^{-a}$ gives $$-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_T D^2K(z)
 =a|z|^{-a-2}\left(d'-(a+2)|\operatorname{proj}_T(z/|z|)|^2\right)
 \ge a(d'-a-2)|z|^{-a-2}.$$ For $d'=m$ or $m-1$ the last coefficient is positive. A sufficiently small, dimension-dependent metric distortion preserves a fixed positive fraction of it for traces in metric orthonormal frames. The connection and weight terms in the weighted Laplacian involve only first derivatives of $U$. On $S_{\rm reg}$ the restriction formula adds its mean-curvature vector paired with $\nabla U$. This vector is locally bounded, since $H_{\rm base}=p-\partial_\nu\log\omega$ by <a href="#cut:gmt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:gmt">42</a>. There is no dependence here on individual principal curvatures. Integrating the positive second-derivative kernel over $|x-y|<c\delta(x)$ gives $c\delta^{-3.5}$. Equations <a href="#local:rough-U-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:rough-U-bounds">[local:rough-U-bounds]</a>, followed by another reduction of the distance threshold, therefore imply, in the ambient space and on the regular wall, $$\label{local:U-superharmonic}
 -\frac{\Delta_\omega U}{U}\ge c\delta^{-2},
 \qquad |\nabla\log U|\le C\delta^{-1}.$$ The leading constants again depend only on the fixed rough data. For example the first-order errors before division have size at most $C_F\delta^{-2.5}$, one power lower than the positive term. The differentiated kernels through order two are locally integrable off $F$: their strongest singularity is $|x-y|^{-(m-2)}$. Since the density is bounded near any such $x$ and integrable elsewhere on its compact support, ordinary differentiation under the integral, with a vanishing small diagonal ball, proves that $U$ is $C^2$ off $F$. No diagonal distributional term is present.

<a id="local:vector-hardy"></a>
For a weighted domain $Q$, completing a square and integrating by parts gives the vector-field identity in inequality form $$\label{local:vector-hardy}
 \int_Q|\nabla v|^2\ge
 \int_Q(\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_\omega A-|A|^2)v^2
       -\int_{\partial Q}(A\cdot\nu_Q)v^2.$$ Choose a smooth cutoff $\rho$, equal to one near $F$ and supported in the chart, and set $A_b=-b\rho\nabla\log U$ in the bulk. On the wall use $A_h=-b\rho\nabla_S\log U$. For $0<b\le1$, where $\rho=1$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_\omega(-b\nabla\log U)-b^2|\nabla\log U|^2
 =-b\Delta_\omega U/U+b(1-b)|\nabla\log U|^2
 \ge bc\delta^{-2}.$$ Outside a smaller neighborhood of $F$ all errors from these fields are bounded by a piece-dependent constant.

On the cylinder take the horizontal field $A_h$ and add the vertical component $a(x)\partial_t$, where $$a(x)=\frac{.8}{1.7}\,b\rho(x)
               \bigl(1+|\nabla\log U|_g^2\bigr)^{1/2}
                 \big|_{S_{\rm reg}}.$$ It has zero divergence in the vertical direction and satisfies $1.7a\ge .8|A_b|$ at the seam. The outward cylinder normal there is $-\partial_t$. Applying <a href="#local:vector-hardy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:vector-hardy">[local:vector-hardy]</a> with coefficients $.8$ and $1.7$ on the two pieces, their boundary terms sum to $$\int_{S_{\rm reg}}
       \bigl[-.8 A_b\cdot\nu_\Omega+1.7a\bigr]v(0)^2\ge0.$$ This explicitly permits arbitrary matching seam values. The vertical square costs at most $C b^2\delta^{-2}+C_F$ in the cylinder interior. Choose $b>0$ sufficiently small, using the universal constants in <a href="#local:U-superharmonic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:U-superharmonic">[local:U-superharmonic]</a>, so that this is absorbed by half the positive term of order $b\delta^{-2}$. All remaining localization terms cost $C_F\|v\|_2^2$. This proves <a href="#local:rough-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:rough-estimate">[local:rough-estimate]</a> on a joined side. The wall proof is the same calculation without the seam or the vertical field. Taking the smaller leading coefficient makes one $c_0$ valid for the wall and both sides. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-local-sharp"></a>
<div id="local:sharp" class="proposition">

<a id="local:sharp-estimate"></a>
**Proposition 52** (The local shielding estimate). *There is a sufficiently small flatness number $\eta>0$, depending only on $m$ and the fixed numerical coefficients, with the following property. Let $F$ be any piece from <a href="#local:flat-pieces" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:flat-pieces">49</a> for this flatness number, in a sufficiently accurate chart. For the potential of <a href="#local:shell" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:shell">50</a>, there are $\varepsilon_F>0$ and $C_F<\infty$ such that, for every $0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_F$, $$\label{local:sharp-estimate}
 1.16\int D_\varepsilon v^2
           \le .78\mathcal N(v)+C_F\|v\|_2^2.$$ This holds on the true wall and either joined side, and hence on translated compact tests in a pure wall cylinder. The constants $\varepsilon_F,C_F$ may depend on the local smooth data and $F$, but are independent of $\varepsilon$. No vanishing trace condition is imposed at a joined seam.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-local-sharp"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-sharp</summary>

*Proof.* We give the parameter order first. Fix the rough number $\eta_0$ and coefficient $c_0$ from <a href="#local:rough" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:rough">51</a>. Below, constants in partition gradient bounds depend only on dimension and the fixed chart distortion. Choose $\xi>0$ so small that the rough estimate pays their total squared-gradient error with at most $.04$ of $\mathcal N$. Next choose a large fixed $K$, then a small fixed $\alpha>0$, and finally a strong flatness $\eta\le\eta_0$, as specified below. Only after the piece $F$ has been selected for this $\eta$ do we choose its small distance threshold and then $\varepsilon_F$. In particular $C_F$ is never used to choose $\xi$, $K$, or $\eta$.

Put $a_-=1/2$, $a_+=2e^{5\pi}$ and $R_1=K\varepsilon$. The shell supporting $D_\varepsilon$ lies in $a_-\varepsilon<\delta<a_+\varepsilon$. Choose a smooth angle $\vartheta(\delta)$ equal to zero for $\delta\le a_+\varepsilon$ and equal to $\pi/2$ for $\delta\ge\xi R_1$, with $$|\nabla\vartheta|\le\xi/\delta.$$ Such a function is obtained by a smooth transition in $\log(\delta/(a_+\varepsilon))$ of length at least a fixed multiple of $1/\xi$. Thus it suffices to choose $K\ge (a_+/\xi)\exp(C/\xi)$, independently of the piece. Set $q=\cos\vartheta$ and $q_\infty=\sin\vartheta$. They extend as constants across $F$, and $|\nabla q|^2+|\nabla q_\infty|^2\le\xi^2\delta^{-2}$.

Take a smooth normalized squared partition $\sum_i\phi_i^2=1$ on chart boxes of scale $R_1$, with uniformly bounded overlap and $\sum_i|\nabla\phi_i|^2\le C_mR_1^{-2}$. One construction translates a fixed compactly supported bump on a Euclidean lattice and divides each bump by the square root of the sum of their squares. Restrict the same ambient functions to the wall and freeze them on the cylinder. Only finitely many boxes meet $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}q$, which is compact in the chart at the scales in use. The family $q\phi_i,q_\infty$ is a squared partition, and its total gradient square is at most $C_m\xi^2\delta^{-2}$ on $\{\delta<\xi R_1\}$ and zero elsewhere. The estimate follows from $\delta<\xi R_1$ wherever $q\ne0$.

<a id="local:localization"></a>
For a squared partition $\sum_i\chi_i^2=1$, direct expansion gives <a id="local:IMS"></a>$$\label{local:IMS}
 \sum_i\mathcal N(\chi_iv)
 =\mathcal N(v)+
   \int a(x)\sum_i|\nabla\chi_i|^2v^2,$$ where $a(x)$ is $.8$ on bulk and $1.7$ on wall or cylinder. The mixed terms vanish because $\sum_i\chi_i\nabla\chi_i=0$. Choose $\varepsilon_F$ small enough that $\xi R_1<d_F$. Equation <a href="#local:rough-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:rough-estimate">[local:rough-estimate]</a> and the choice of $\xi$ now give $$\label{local:localization}
 \sum_i\mathcal N(q\phi_iv)
 \le1.04\mathcal N(v)+C_F\|v\|_2^2.$$ For example it is enough to impose $1.7C_m\xi^2/c_0\le.04$; the other partition term is nonnegative and has been discarded. Lipschitz distance cutoffs are allowed by approximation on the regular test supports.

Consider one box meeting the support of $q$. Choose a nearby point of $F$ and apply <a href="#local:flatness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:flatness">[local:flatness]</a> at a radius $L_mR_1$, where a fixed $L_m$ includes the box and every nearest point of $F$ needed in it. Taking $\varepsilon_F$ smaller ensures this is below $r_F$. Let $L$ be the resulting $(m-8)$-plane and define $$\zeta^2=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_{\rm Eucl}(x,L)^2+\alpha^2\varepsilon^2,
 \qquad P=\tfrac12\zeta^2.$$ The Euclidean Hessian of $P$ is an orthogonal projection of rank eight. Its trace on every $(m-1)$-dimensional subspace is at least seven. Its gradient has size at most $\zeta$, and throughout the box $\zeta\le C_mR_1$ (after fixing $\alpha$ and $K$).

<a id="local:plane-potential"></a>
For either the ambient or the tangential weighted derivatives, the vector field $A=d_1\nabla\log\zeta$ has potential $$\label{local:plane-potential}
 (\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_\omega A-|A|^2)\zeta^2
 =d_1\Delta_\omega P
       -(2d_1+d_1^2)|\nabla P|^2/\zeta^2.$$ Let $\tau$ bound the chart’s metric distortion. The rank traces have error at most $C_m\tau$, and $|\nabla P|^2/\zeta^2\le1+C_m\tau$. The connection, weight, and wall mean-curvature contributions to $\Delta_\omega P$ have size at most $C_FR_1$. Thus one may arrange $C_m\tau\le .005$ and $C_FR_1\le .025$, uniformly in the box and its plane. Equation <a href="#local:plane-potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:plane-potential">[local:plane-potential]</a> then gives $$\begin{array}{ll}
 d_1=1\text{ in bulk}:&
       \mathop{\mathrm{div}}_\omega A-|A|^2\ge4.9\zeta^{-2},\\
 d_1=2\text{ on wall}:&
       \mathop{\mathrm{div}}_\omega A-|A|^2\ge5.9\zeta^{-2}.
 \end{array}$$ Indeed the respective Euclidean lower bounds are $8-3=5$ and $2\cdot7-8=6$. Only the mean curvature of the wall enters this calculation, so these bounds remain valid arbitrarily near its singular set on the regular test supports. The positive term $\alpha^2\varepsilon^2$ also makes the fields nonsingular along $L$; the estimates hold inside the inner shell as well.

<a id="local:box-bound"></a>
In the bulk use $A_b=\nabla\log\zeta$. On a joined cylinder use $A_c=2\nabla_S\log\zeta+\beta\zeta^{-1}\partial_t$, where $\beta=(.8/1.7)\sqrt{1+C_m\tau}$ is chosen so that $\beta/\zeta\ge(.8/1.7)|A_b|$ at the seam. The metric distortion determines this fixed upper factor. The vertical component has zero divergence and is orthogonal to the horizontal component. Its only interior cost is $\beta^2\zeta^{-2}$. The boundary terms in <a href="#local:vector-hardy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:vector-hardy">[local:vector-hardy]</a> are nonnegative after summing, by exactly the cancellation used in <a href="#local:rough" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:rough">51</a>. After multiplication by the coefficients in <a href="#local:norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:norm">[local:norm]</a>, the limiting interior coefficients are $$.8\cdot5=4\quad\text{in bulk},\qquad
 1.7\cdot6-.8^2/1.7>9.8\quad\text{in the cylinder},$$ and $1.7\cdot6=10.2$ on the wall alone. With the explicit error bounds just imposed, the coefficients are at least $.8\cdot4.9=3.92$ in bulk, $1.7\cdot5.9-(.8^2/1.7)1.005>9.6$ in the cylinder, and $1.7\cdot5.9>10$ on the wall. In particular all exceed $3.8$. Applying the vector inequality to $q\phi_iv$ gives $$\label{local:box-bound}
 \mathcal N(q\phi_iv)\ge
       3.8\int\zeta^{-2}(q\phi_iv)^2.$$ This is a bound throughout the localized support; its nonnegative right side may therefore be restricted to the shell.

We finally compare $\zeta$ with $\delta$ on that shell. A nearest point $z\in F$ lies in the ball used for the plane condition, so $$\zeta(x)\le 1.005\delta(x)+L_m\eta K\varepsilon
                                  +\alpha\varepsilon.$$ The first factor is ensured by the fixed chart accuracy and its buffer, after shrinking the scale so that a minimizing short path to $F$ stays in the chart. Since $\delta\ge a_-\varepsilon$ there, choose $\alpha/a_-\le .005$ and then $L_m\eta K/a_-\le .015$. These choices give $\zeta\le1.03\delta$. Notice that $K$ was already fixed using the rough coefficient, and that the strong flatness was chosen before selecting $F$. Since $3.8/(1.03)^2>3.5$, <a href="#local:box-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:box-bound">[local:box-bound]</a> controls $3.5\delta^{-2}$ on the shell. Summing over boxes uses $q=1$ there and $\sum_i\phi_i^2=1$, and then <a href="#local:localization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:localization">[local:localization]</a> gives $$3.5\int_{\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}D_\varepsilon}\delta^{-2}v^2
                  \le1.04\mathcal N(v)+C_F\|v\|_2^2.$$ Together with <a href="#local:shell-potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:shell-potential">[local:shell-potential]</a>, this proves the stronger coefficient bound $$1.16\int D_\varepsilon v^2
 \le\frac{1.16\cdot2.12\cdot1.04}{3.5}\mathcal N(v)
                   +C_F\|v\|_2^2,
 \qquad
 \frac{1.16\cdot2.12\cdot1.04}{3.5}<.731<.78.$$ This yields <a href="#local:sharp-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:sharp-estimate">[local:sharp-estimate]</a>. A compact test on a pure cylinder can be translated to positive height in a joined cylinder and extended by zero onto the bulk, so it obeys the same estimate. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-local-choice-order"></a>
<div id="local:choice-order" class="remark">

*Remark 53*. The choices can be summarized without a hidden dependence on the compact piece: dimension and chart distortion; rough flatness $\eta_0$ and coefficient $c_0$; localization parameter $\xi$; logarithmic taper and $K$; $\alpha$ and the stronger flatness $\eta$; the compact pieces supplied for that $\eta$; finally their individual distance thresholds and shell scales. Shrinking a threshold absorbs large-scale kernel tails and smooth geometric errors. It never changes the leading coefficient used to select the stronger flatness. All estimates are weighted base estimates, so the number of previously appended circle factors does not replace $m$ in any dimension or rank count.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
### Global shielding and extension to cofaces

The local annular cost does not add once for every singular piece: a concentration argument bounds a new shrinking cost by the maximum of the old and local form coefficients. We prove that assertion first, then transport each chosen addition to cofaces, and finally show that only finitely many additions are needed on a whole packet. The finite-label hypotheses in this appendix are extended in [countable:finite-data,countable:step] [countable:finite-data](#countable:finite-data), [countable:step](#countable:step). Each label may already have its own positive potential floor $p_j^*$; none of the estimates uses a positive lower bound common to all countably many labels.

We keep the true cuts fixed throughout this section. Only profiles of remaining labels, their cores, and their potentials are changed. In particular, none of the convergence assertions below concerns minimizers for a changing pressure.

<a id="stmt-shield-domain"></a>
<div id="shield:domain" class="definition">

**Definition 54** (The matching form domain). Let $E$ be a parent packet of dimension $m$, with true wall $S$ and singular set $\Sigma$. A raw piece $Z$ means either $S_{\rm reg}$ or one of the two joined spaces $$Z=\Omega\mathbin{\cup}_{S_{\rm reg}}
                 (S_{\rm reg}\times[0,\infty)).$$ The tests on a joined space are compactly supported, piecewise smooth functions whose two traces agree at the regular seam and whose support avoids $\Sigma$ and the cylinders over $\Sigma$. The wall has the corresponding compact tests away from $\Sigma$. All measures include the old warp weight. Put <a id="shield:hilbert"></a>$$\label{shield:hilbert}
 \mathfrak h_Z(v)=\mathcal N_Z(v)+e_0\norm{v}_{L^2(Z)}^2,
 \qquad e_0=\frac{\lambda_r-\lambda_{r+1}}{100}>0,$$ where $\mathcal N$ is the reserved gradient form in <a href="#cut:reserve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:reserve">47</a>: its gradient coefficients are $.8$ on the bulk and $1.7$ on the wall or cylinder. Write $\mathcal H_Z$ for the completion of these tests in $\mathfrak h_Z^{1/2}$. Pure cylinders $S_{\rm reg}\times\mathbb R$ use the coefficient $1.7$ throughout. The definitions also apply after adjoining finitely many Euclidean product coordinates, with the same coefficients and $e_0$. Such products are used only to identify limits on translated windows; they do not append further circle warps or change the weight. A compact window means a fixed compact ambient base window together with a bounded cylinder interval, when a cylinder is present.

</div>

The trace matching in this definition is essential. There is no Dirichlet condition at the seam. A pure-cylinder test can be translated into the cylindrical part of a joined space, with zero bulk part. Consequently every form inequality on that joined space for potentials pulled back from its base also holds on the pure cylinder.

<a id="stmt-shield-compactness"></a>
<div id="shield:compactness" class="lemma">

**Lemma 55** (Compactness on joined windows). *Assume $m\ge8$. On any fixed compact window, a bounded sequence in $\mathcal H_Z$ has a strongly convergent $L^2$ subsequence. The following moving version also holds. Work in fixed smooth ambient geometry on each expanding window, as is the case after translation in an exact product end. Suppose positive weights and pressures converge smoothly there, and the true phases and walls converge as in [cut:gmt,cut:end-convergence] [cut:gmt](#cut:gmt), [cut:end-convergence](#cut:end-convergence). Consider normalized tests on the corresponding raw pieces $Z_i$, all supported in a fixed compact window strictly inside a larger window of convergence. After passage to a subsequence there is a function $v$ on the limiting raw piece $Z_\infty$ such that:*

1.  *On every regular compact subwindow, the tests converge weakly in $H^1$ and strongly in $L^2$ in the smooth graphical identifications. At a limiting regular seam the two traces agree.*

2.  *No $L^2$ mass is lost in neighborhoods of the limiting singular set. In particular, integrals of $v_i^2$ converge to those of $v^2$, including integrals against any fixed bounded continuous ambient potential and its pullback to the wall and cylinder.*

<a id="shield:liminf"></a>
3.  *The limit belongs to $\mathcal H_{Z_\infty}$ and $$\label{shield:liminf}
     \mathfrak h_{Z_\infty}(v)
           \le\liminf_i\mathfrak h_{Z_i}(v_i).$$*

*These assertions include pure cylinders, translated cylinder intervals, and Euclidean product limits. Constants are local to the enlarged window; no uniform bound among all packets is asserted. If the limiting wall is smooth on the enlarged window, the same conclusions follow from ordinary Rellich compactness and the bounded trace maps in the smooth graphical charts, in every base dimension.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shield-compactness"><summary>Proof of stmt-shield-compactness</summary>

*Proof.* We give the estimates for tests first; they extend to the completions by density. On the enlarged window the weights are uniformly comparable to a positive constant, the ambient geometry is uniformly smooth, and the unweighted base mean curvature of the regular walls is bounded. The latter bound follows from the prescribed weighted mean curvature and the bounded first derivative of the logarithm of the weight. The perimeter varifolds have density at least one and no additional boundary term on this interior window, by <a href="#cut:gmt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:gmt">42</a>. They therefore meet the hypotheses of the Sobolev inequality used below. No bound on the individual principal curvatures is needed.

<a id="shield:submanifold-sobolev"></a>
We use the submanifold Sobolev inequality in the following precise form. For a $d$-dimensional Euclidean rectifiable varifold of density at least one with generalized mean curvature $H$, and a nonnegative compactly supported test $f$, $$\label{shield:submanifold-sobolev}
 \left(\int f^{d/(d-1)}\right)^{(d-1)/d}
 \le C_d\int\bigl(\abs{\nabla f}+\abs H f\bigr).$$ This holds in arbitrary codimension; see Simon (2014, chap. 4, Section 5, Theorem 5.7) <a id="pm-cite-55"></a>[[simonGMT](#ref-simonGMT)]. This is the submanifold Sobolev inequality of Michael and Simon (Michael and Simon 1973) <a id="pm-cite-56"></a>[[michaelSimon1973](#ref-michaelSimon1973)]. A smooth extension of the fixed ambient chart metric to a compact manifold and a fixed smooth isometric embedding (Nash 1956) <a id="pm-cite-57"></a>[[nash1956](#ref-nash1956)] give the corresponding local inequality in our charts. The extra mean curvature of the embedding is bounded on this fixed compact geometry. For the moving-domain assertion the embedding is chosen once in the fixed ambient geometry; its constants are therefore independent of the wall in the sequence. Applying <a href="#shield:submanifold-sobolev" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:submanifold-sobolev">[shield:submanifold-sobolev]</a> to a power of the test gives the usual $H^1$ Sobolev exponent on the wall. Applied to $S_{\rm reg}\times\mathbb R$, it gives, with $$q=\frac{2m}{m-2},\qquad b=\frac{2(m-1)}{m-2},
 \qquad 2(b-1)=q,$$ a local $L^q$ bound on the cylinder. A test on the half-cylinder is reflected evenly at its base before this application. The wall alone has the stronger exponent $2(m-1)/(m-3)>2$.

Here and below cutoffs are taken on the enlarged window and are pulled back to both seam sides. The cutoff errors are bounded by the local $L^2$ norm. Normalize $\mathfrak h_Z(v)\le1$. Choose a cylinder cutoff which is one at $s=0$ and vanishes at the outer end of a fixed bounded interval. The fundamental theorem of calculus and Hölder give <a id="shield:trace"></a>$$\label{shield:trace}
 \int_{S_{\rm reg}}\abs{v(0)}^b
 \le C\int_{S\times[0,L]}
       \bigl(\abs v^b+\abs v^{b-1}\abs{\partial_s v}\bigr)
 \le C.$$ The last step uses the cylinder $L^q$ bound and $2(b-1)=q$.

<a id="shield:bulk-bv"></a>
The bulk need not have a uniform smooth boundary, so we use a BV calculation there. Extend $\abs{v_{\rm bulk}}^b$ by zero in the ambient chart, including its compact cutoff. For an initial test its support misses the singular set, and the BV jump formula gives precisely the boundary contribution $\int_{S_{\rm reg}}\abs{v(0)}^b$ in addition to the interior variation. This is also obtained directly by integration by parts against unit vector fields on the regular side. The ambient $L^1$ Sobolev inequality and Cauchy–Schwarz therefore yield, for $J=\int_\Omega\abs v^q$, $$\label{shield:bulk-bv}
 J^{(m-1)/m}
 \le C\left(
       \int_\Omega\abs v^b+
       \int_\Omega\abs v^{b-1}\abs{\nabla v}
       +\int_{S_{\rm reg}}\abs{v(0)}^b\right)
 \le C(1+J^{1/2}).$$ Here $bm/(m-1)=q$; the first integral on the right is at most $J^{1/2}\norm v_2$, because $b=(q+2)/2$. Since $(m-1)/m>1/2$, <a href="#shield:bulk-bv" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:bulk-bv">[shield:bulk-bv]</a> bounds $J$. Rescaling removes the normalization. Thus all pieces have a local $L^p$ bound for some $p>2$ controlled by their matching form norm. This argument extends a power as a BV function; it constructs no $H^1$ extension operator for the bulk.

We next exclude concentration in the moving version. On a slightly larger compact window let $F$ be the compact portion of the limiting singular set. Its ambient volume and its limiting perimeter measure are zero. For a closed $\delta$-neighborhood of $F$, perimeter measure convergence and the closed-set upper bound for weakly convergent measures imply $$\limsup_i\mu_{S_i}(\overline{U_\delta(F)})
       \le \mu_{S_\infty}(\overline{U_\delta(F)}),$$ with a fixed outer compact cutoff if necessary. The right side tends to zero as $\delta\downarrow0$. The same conclusion for bulk measure follows from ambient volume bounds, and for a cylinder follows by multiplying the wall measure by the bounded interval length. Hölder and the local $L^p$ estimates give $$\int_{A_i}\abs{v_i}^2
 \le C\mu_i(A_i)^{1-2/p}.$$ Thus the order is first $i\to\infty$ at a fixed singular neighborhood, then $\delta\downarrow0$. Neither disappearing components nor a thin boundary layer can retain $L^2$ mass.

Off $F$, graphical convergence and the absence of stray wall support supply smooth charts for both bulk phases and their seams. Rellich compactness in those charts gives the asserted weak $H^1$ and strong $L^2$ limits. The trace map on each fixed regular chart is bounded and linear; weak convergence of both traces preserves their equality. Combining these regular limits with the no-concentration conclusion proves all the stated $L^2$ and bounded-potential convergence. Exhausting the regular pieces gives lower semicontinuity of their gradient integrals.

It remains to justify membership in the limit completion, rather than assume it. On this one limiting geometry, monotonicity and the local area bound give $$\mu_S(B_{2a}(x))\le C a^{m-1}$$ for sufficiently small balls in the fixed window. Since $\dim_{\rm H}\Sigma\le m-8<m-3$, a compact singular portion has ball covers, inside arbitrarily small neighborhoods, with $\sum_i a_i^{m-3}$ arbitrarily small. For cutoffs equal to one on these balls, supported on their doubles, and with gradients at most $C/a_i$, the maximum of the cutoffs has squared-gradient integral at most $$C\sum_i a_i^{m-3}$$ on the wall and on bounded cylinder intervals. The bulk cost is at most $C\sum_i a_i^{m-2}$ and is smaller. A finite subcover suffices. Use the same base cutoff on the bulk and cylinder, preserving seam matching. First truncate the limit function to a bounded function; then multiply it by one minus these cutoffs. The derivative of the cutoff has vanishing energy after truncation, and the other error tends to zero by absolute continuity of the already finite gradient integrals. Its $L^2$ error tends to zero as well. The resulting functions miss the singular set. Matching traces glue their two $H^1$ pieces across every regular seam chart; ordinary mollification in such charts, and a partition of unity, approximate them by the defining tests. This proves membership and <a href="#shield:liminf" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:liminf">[shield:liminf]</a>. Capacity is used only on the limit; no cheap cutoff on every approximating singular set, and no convergence theorem for the entire moving Sobolev spaces, is needed. The same proof applies to pure cylinders and product limits. For a product with $a$ extra coordinates, use ambient dimension $m+a$ and singular-dimension bound $m+a-8$ in the preceding exponents; the strict codimension needed for the capacity estimate is unchanged. Thus this argument supplies exactly regular graphical identifications, strong $L^2$ convergence without singular concentration, matching seam traces, and the lower semicontinuity used in the later form transfers. It does not assert smooth convergence at singular points. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-shield-maximum"></a>
<div id="shield:maximum" class="lemma">

<a id="shield:local-input"></a>
<a id="shield:maximum-bound"></a>
**Lemma 56** (Maximum of successive form costs). *Fix a packet and its true pieces. Let $V\ge0$ be a fixed bounded potential, pulled back to cylinders, satisfying <a id="shield:old-bound"></a>$$\label{shield:old-bound}
 \int_Z Vv^2\le\theta\mathfrak h_Z(v)$$ on its wall and both joined sides. Let $F\subset\Sigma$ be a compact piece and let $A_\varepsilon=1.16D_\varepsilon$ be one of the nonnegative shell potentials of [local:shell,local:sharp] [local:shell](#local:shell), [local:sharp](#local:sharp). In particular its base support lies in a fixed compact set and shrinks to $F$, and $$\label{shield:local-input}
 \int_Z A_\varepsilon v^2\le .78\mathcal N_Z(v)+C_F\norm v_2^2$$ for all sufficiently small $\varepsilon$, on all three pieces. For every $\eta>0$, a sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$ gives simultaneously $$\label{shield:maximum-bound}
 \int_Z(V+A_\varepsilon)v^2
 \le\bigl(\max\{\theta,.78\}+\eta\bigr)\mathfrak h_Z(v).$$ It suffices that $V$ be bounded on the compact base windows used below. In particular the assertion applies to a finite sum of compact additions on the initial radial parent.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shield-maximum"><summary>Proof of stmt-shield-maximum</summary>

*Proof.* First $\int A_\varepsilon v^2\to0$ for every fixed $v\in\mathcal H_Z$. Indeed <a href="#shield:local-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:local-input">[shield:local-input]</a> bounds these forms uniformly by a constant times $\mathfrak h_Z$, whereas for a defining test the integral is zero for small $\varepsilon$, since its support misses $F$. Density and Cauchy–Schwarz for the nonnegative form give the assertion.

<a id="shield:ims"></a>
We detail the localization needed on noncompact pieces. If $\sum_\alpha\chi_\alpha^2=1$ is a locally finite Lipschitz partition which has the same seam traces, then $$\label{shield:ims}
 \sum_\alpha\mathfrak h_Z(\chi_\alpha v)
 =\mathfrak h_Z(v)+
   \int_Z c_Z\sum_\alpha\abs{\nabla\chi_\alpha}^2v^2,
 \qquad
 c_Z=\begin{cases}.8&\text{on bulk},\\1.7&\text{on wall or cylinder}.
 \end{cases}$$ The mass term $e_0\norm v_2^2$ allows the last integral to be at most $\tau\mathfrak h_Z(v)$ for any prescribed $\tau>0$. For example use a sine/cosine pair of a Lipschitz cutoff angle, equal to $\pi/2$ on a fixed compact base set containing $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}A_\varepsilon$ for all small $\varepsilon$, and zero outside a sufficiently large compact neighborhood. Its transition width makes its derivative arbitrarily small. The cosine part sees only $V$. Its bound is the assumed global form inequality for $V$; no compact support assumption on $V$ is needed. The sine part has compact base support, where the old potential is bounded. On a joined side also use a squared partition into cylinder intervals of a sufficiently large fixed length. The first member is identically one near $s=0$ and extends as one on the bulk; all the others vanish near the seam and extend as zero there. Their squared gradients have an arbitrarily small sum. All cutoffs in the base are pulled back to the cylinder.

Choose the partition error so small that a violation of <a href="#shield:maximum-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:maximum-bound">[shield:maximum-bound]</a> forces one localized test to have quotient larger than $\max\{\theta,.78\}+\eta/2$. This follows directly by summing the potential integrals and <a href="#shield:ims" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:ims">[shield:ims]</a>; for instance it suffices to have $(\max\{\theta,.78\}+\eta/2)(1+\tau)
 <\max\{\theta,.78\}+\eta$. The selected test has compact base support and a bounded cylinder interval. Passing to a subsequence, its interval either stays in a fixed bounded range, or escapes to infinity. In the latter case it is translated to a fixed interval on the pure cylinder. Both the old bound and <a href="#shield:local-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:local-input">[shield:local-input]</a> hold there by translating its tests into the original joined side. Thus in all cases the selected sequence lies in one fixed Hilbert space, in a fixed compact window.

Suppose now that $\varepsilon_i\downarrow0$ gives such violating tests $v_i$, normalized by $\mathfrak h(v_i)=1$. By <a href="#shield:compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:compactness">55</a> and weak Hilbert compactness, $v_i\rightharpoonup v$ in this fixed space and $v_i\to v$ strongly in $L^2$. Write $w_i=v_i-v$. Then $$\mathfrak h(w_i)=1-\mathfrak h(v)+o(1),\qquad \norm{w_i}_2\to0.$$ The bounded old potential satisfies $\int Vv_i^2\to\int Vv^2\le\theta\mathfrak h(v)$. The new form vanishes on $v$, and its mixed term vanishes by Cauchy–Schwarz and the uniform bound on $w_i$. Consequently <a href="#shield:local-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:local-input">[shield:local-input]</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \limsup_i\int(V+A_{\varepsilon_i})v_i^2
 &\le\theta\mathfrak h(v)+.78\limsup_i\mathcal N(w_i)\\
 &\le\theta\mathfrak h(v)+.78(1-\mathfrak h(v))
 \le\max\{\theta,.78\}.
\end{aligned}$$ This contradicts the selected quotient. There are only three raw pieces, so the same sufficiently small scale works for all of them. ◻

</details>

We next extend a single chosen addition. The scale in <a href="#shield:maximum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:maximum">56</a> is fixed before this next limit is taken.

<a id="stmt-shield-extension"></a>
<div id="shield:extension" class="lemma">

<a id="shield:extension-support"></a>
<a id="shield:extended-bound"></a>
**Lemma 57** (Extension with late fronts). *Let $B$ be a packet, let $D\ge0$ be a fixed smooth compactly supported potential addition on $B$, and let $V$ denote the old cumulative potential on every packet. Suppose these old potentials are exactly compatible and obey the common form bound $\theta\mathfrak h$. Suppose also that $$\int(V_B+1.16D)v^2\le\beta\mathfrak h_B(v),\qquad \beta\ge\theta,$$ on the wall and joined sides over $B$. In each of the finitely many coface occurrences $B\times(T,\infty)^d\subset E$, introduce a smooth potential $D_E$ with $$\label{shield:extension-support}
 0\le D_E\le D\circ\pi_B,\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}D_E\subset K\times\prod_{i=1}^d[R_i,\infty),
 \qquad K\Subset B.$$ Use consistent functions on the normal coordinates identified by commuting face chains, so that these extensions are compatible. For every $\eta>0$ all starts can be chosen sufficiently large that $$\label{shield:extended-bound}
 \int(V_E+1.16D_E)v^2\le(\beta+\eta)\mathfrak h_E(v)$$ on every affected raw piece. The conclusion concerns the same fixed true cuts; it uses their product convergence from <a href="#cut:end-convergence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:end-convergence">43</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shield-extension"><summary>Proof of stmt-shield-extension</summary>

*Proof.* Only finitely many occurrences are involved by <a href="#packet:category" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="packet:category">16</a>. Here and below a source is extended along the directed face-to-coface relations, through their finite depth. Closing under arbitrary alternating face and coface walks is neither required nor performed. For a common normal coordinate take the largest of the finitely many requested starts, so commuting chains give identical occurrences. It is enough to prove the assertion for any sequence of their fronts with $\min_iR_i\to\infty$, passing to a fixed occurrence and piece if needed. Choose every localization width below using $\eta$ and $e_0$ first, independently of the front positions.

Separate the new support from its complement with a sine/cosine pair as in <a href="#shield:ims" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:ims">[shield:ims]</a>. More explicitly, an angle can be $\pi/2$ times a product of a compact base cutoff, equal to one on $K$, and normal cutoffs which equal one on $[R_i,\infty)$ and vanish before $R_i-L$. Enlarge the compact base window and $L$ until the sum of squared derivatives is sufficiently small. Extend the angle by zero outside the product sector. Taking the fronts beyond all collar thresholds makes this extension well defined. The cosine test misses the addition and has the old bound $\theta$. Partition the sine part further into fixed large boxes in every normal coordinate, and into fixed large intervals in cylinder height when applicable. The total localization error is as small a fraction of $\mathfrak h$ as required.

If <a href="#shield:extended-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:extended-bound">[shield:extended-bound]</a> failed along fronts tending out, the same quotient selection as in <a href="#shield:maximum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:maximum">56</a> would give normalized tests on selected boxes with quotient at least $\beta+\eta/2$. Such a box must meet the new potential support, since otherwise the old bound $\theta\le\beta$ applies. Its center therefore has $$t_i\ge R_i-O(L)\longrightarrow\infty
 \quad\text{for every }i=1,\ldots,d.$$ Its base window is fixed and compact. Recenter every normal coordinate. A bounded cylinder interval stays in a bounded-height joined window, or, after another subsequence, is translated to a pure cylinder. The ambient geometry on the recentered windows is exactly the product geometry, and the true cuts converge to those over $B$ times $\mathbb R^d$. The old potential is exactly $V_B\circ\pi_B$ there, because the fronts also exceed its fixed product thresholds. The new potential is bounded above by the fixed bounded function $D\circ\pi_B$.

<a id="shield:extension-limit"></a>
Apply <a href="#shield:compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:compactness">55</a>. For its limit $v$, $$\label{shield:extension-limit}
 \limsup\int(V_E+1.16D_E)v_E^2
 \le\int(V_B+1.16D)\circ\pi_B\,v^2,
 \qquad \mathfrak h_{\rm lim}(v)\le1.$$ The face form bound applies to the right side by slicing the $\mathbb R^d$ coordinates. For defining tests this is Fubini’s theorem: the gradient terms in the extra directions are nonnegative, and all coefficients $.8,1.7,e_0$ are the same in every base dimension. Approximation in the limit completion, proved in <a href="#shield:compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:compactness">55</a>, gives the result for $v$. In a pure-cylinder limit use the face joined bound after translation in cylinder height. Thus the right side of <a href="#shield:extension-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:extension-limit">[shield:extension-limit]</a> is at most $\beta$, a contradiction. Finite incidence permits simultaneous choices for all occurrences. This limit compares bounded potential integrals on moving domains; it uses no subtraction of vectors belonging to different form spaces. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-shield-caps"></a>
<div id="shield:caps" class="lemma">

**Lemma 58** (Compatible caps and cores). *A compact forcing update from <a href="#local:shell" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:shell">50</a> extends to all its cofaces so as to satisfy <a href="#shield:extension-support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:extension-support">[shield:extension-support]</a>, preserve the profile gradient inequality with coefficient $a_r$, and enlarge only the designated cores. The extension is an exact product sufficiently far in every old face collar and is made once per occurrence, independent of the order of commuting face relations.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shield-caps"><summary>Proof of stmt-shield-caps</summary>

<a id="shield:cap-formula"></a>
*Proof.* Consider a plus addition on $B$, with forcing profile $u$ and shell potential $D$. Choose a compact base window $K\Subset\{h_j>t_j\}$ containing both $\overline{\{u> -\infty\}}$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}D$ in its interior. The first set includes the inner pole region; keeping only $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}D$, which is annular, would not suffice for this extension. In a coface product over $K$ put $$\label{shield:cap-formula}
 u_E(x,t)=\min\{u(x),w_1(t_1),\ldots,w_d(t_d)\},\qquad
 D_E(x,t)=D(x)\prod_{i=1}^d\chi_i(t_i).$$ Each cap $w_i$ is $-\infty$ up to a late front, then crosses to $+\infty$, and remains there, with $$2\abs{w_i'}\le .58w_i^2+p_j^*$$ on its finite band. For example integrate $2w_i'=.58w_i^2+p_j^*$ on the finite band and extend by its poles. The crossing length is $2\pi/\sqrt{.58p_j^*}<\infty$; finite truncations remain locally Lipschitz at both ends. Only this label’s positive floor is used. Each smooth cutoff $\chi_i$ takes values in $[0,1]$ and *finishes* increasing to one before $w_i$ begins its crossing. Choose these cutoff fronts to satisfy <a href="#shield:extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shield:extension">57</a> for the already fixed $D$ and shell scale. Outside this product occurrence use the inactive value $-\infty$. The old profile is replaced by its maximum with $u_E$.

For locally Lipschitz finite truncations, a finite minimum or maximum has the derivative of one active branch almost everywhere (on a tie, the branch derivatives agree almost everywhere on the tie set). If the active finite branch is $u$, every cap has already left its $-\infty$ plateau; hence every $\chi_i=1$ and the entire shell potential $D$ is available. If it is a cap, its derivative cost is paid by the preexisting baseline: $$.58w_i^2+p_j^*\le a_rw_i^2+\Pi_j+D_E.$$ The old branch retains its old inequality.
